Modules, quadratic recognition, and
the exact Morita domain

6.1     A module resolution needs no scalar augmentation
Let A be a unital algebra over a field and let M be a left module. Define
                               Pn (A, M ) = A ⊗ A⊗n ⊗ M,        n ≥ 0,
in degree −n. The ith face for 0 ≤ i < n multiplies the adjacent algebra factors ai , ai+1 ; the last
face applies an to M . Let ∂ = ni=0 (−1)i di and augment P0 → M by the action.
                              P

Theorem 6.1 (Resolution of an arbitrary module). P∗ (A, M ) → M is a functorial free left A-
module resolution. For a right module N , N ⊗A P∗ (A, M ) computes N ⊗LA M . Applying HomA (−, L)
computes RHomA (M, L).

Proof. Associativity and the module axiom give di dj = dj−1 di for i < j. The corresponding terms
of ∂ 2 occur with opposite signs, so ∂ 2 = 0. On the augmented underlying vector-space complex set
                h(a0 ⊗ · · · ⊗ an ⊗ m) = 1 ⊗ a0 ⊗ · · · ⊗ an ⊗ m,    h(m) = 1 ⊗ m.
The identities d0 h = 1 and di h = hdi−1 for i > 0 give ∂h + h∂ = 1. Hence the augmentation is
a quasi-isomorphism. Every term is free on its middle vector space, and the complex is bounded
above in cohomological degree, so it is a projective resolution. The two derived calculations follow
by using that resolution.

   For dg algebras and modules over a field, give internal degree q and simplicial degree n total
degree q − n, and use dint + (−1)q ∂. The faces commute with internal differential, so the two terms
anticommute. The signed extra degeneracy is (−1)q h. The simplicial filtration has free graded
quotients on complexes of vector spaces and gives a homotopically projective resolution. For a direct
verification of derived Hom, an acyclic target has acyclic Hom from each such free quotient. The
finite-stage Hom tower is degreewise surjective, and its kernel quotients are acyclic; the resulting
inverse-limit complex is acyclic. Thus the resolution is valid without treating an arbitrary unbounded
complex as projective.
   The tensor bar of Chapters 1–2 used an augmentation to select the module ℸ. The resolution
here selects M directly. In particular it remains available for a Weyl algebra, where a unital scalar
augmentation does not exist.

                                                 21
Chapter 6. Modules, quadratic recognition, and the exact Morita domain


6.2     The diagonal and the two-sided tensor
For an ordinary algebra A, the case of the bimodule A is the two-sided bar

                                        A ⊗ A⊗n ⊗ A −→ A.

Its same unit-insertion contraction proves exactness. The Alexander–Whitney map cuts the internal
word into two pieces and distributes the endpoint coefficients. It is a chain map because every face
either remains on one side of the cut or occurs at the cut in two cancelling terms. Two cuts give
the same three subwords in either order, so it is coassociative. Applying bimodule Hom gives the
cup product of Chapter 14. The distinction between this diagonal resolution and an augmentation
resolution is present in the source and target of every term.
   For a right A-module N and a left A-module M , the complex with terms N ⊗ A⊗n ⊗ M and
those same faces is their derived tensor product. Iterating over two algebras gives a bisimplicial
complex. Realizing first in either simplicial direction, or taking the diagonal realization, gives the
same homotopy colimit. In a strict module presentation this is the ordinary associativity comparison

                                 (L ⊗LB N ) ⊗LA M ≃ L ⊗LB (N ⊗LA M ).

Here a genuine bisimplicial object exists because the two module actions commute. Merely displaying
two independent differentials would not supply that hypothesis.


6.3     The diagonal part of a quadratic bar
Let V be finite-dimensional, let R2 ⊂ V ⊗ V , and let A = T (V )/(R2 ) with generators of weight one.
Put
                                            n−2
                                                  V ⊗i ⊗ R2 ⊗ V ⊗(n−i−2) ⊂ V ⊗n
                                            \
              C0 = ℸ,     C1 = V,    Cn =                                         (n ≥ 2).
                                            i=0

Place Cn in cohomological degree −n and weight n. Deconcatenation restricts to these subspaces:
every relation condition contained within either side of a cut is one of the displayed conditions, and
                                                                    L
tensoring vector spaces preserves their intersections. Thus C = n Cn is a conilpotent coalgebra,
with zero differential.
   The weight-n, length-n part of the reduced bar is V ⊗n . The differential lands in a direct sum
indexed by the position of the sole weight-two entry. Its kernel is exactly the displayed intersection
Cn . There is no incoming differential in that weight and length, since a word of length n + 1 has
weight at least n + 1. Therefore
                                         Cn = TorAn,n (ℸ, ℸ)                                     (6.1)
as the top-length cycles inside the bar, and these cycles give a natural coalgebra map C → BA.

Theorem 6.2 (Quadratic recognition with its actual complex). The following conditions are
equivalent:

 (i) TorA
        i,j (ℸ, ℸ) = 0 for i ̸= j;

 (ii) C → BA is a weightwise quasi-isomorphism;

(iii) the canonical map ΩC → A is a weightwise quasi-isomorphism;

                                                     22
Chapter 6. Modules, quadratic recognition, and the exact Morita domain


(iv) the augmented left Koszul complex A ⊗ C → ℸ, with

                                     d(a ⊗ v1 · · · vn ) = av1 ⊗ v2 · · · vn ,

     extended linearly on Cn , is exact off its augmentation.

When they hold, the Yoneda algebra is the quadratic dual

                                      Ext∗A (ℸ, ℸ) ∼
                                                   = T (V ∗ )/(R2⊥ ),
with V ∗ in cohomological degree one and the chosen graded-dual tensor signs.

Proof. The bar has no components with length larger than weight. Equation (6.1) identifies all its
diagonal homology with C. Thus (i) and (ii) are equivalent. Positive-weight bar–cobar reconstruction
preserves the weightwise equivalences and its unit and counit are equivalences. Applying it to
C → BA proves the equivalence of (ii) and (iii), including the converse by applying bar.
  The displayed Koszul differential squares to zero because the first two slots of Cn lie in R2 and
hence their product is zero in A. The next slots still lie in Cn−1 by its intersection definition, so the
map has the stated target. If the complex is a resolution, tensoring it with ℸ kills its differential
and leaves Cn of weight n. It is a free resolution, so (iv) implies (i).
    For the converse, construct a minimal graded free resolution over the connected algebra A. At
each step choose a weight-homogeneous basis of the syzygy modulo A+ times that syzygy and
lift it. The resulting free module surjects: a nonzero cokernel would have a least weight, and its
least-weight class could not equal an element of A+ times a lower-weight class. Every differential
has entries in A+ , so tensoring the minimal resolution with ℸ kills all its differentials and identifies
its generators with Tor. Under (i), the generators in degree n all have weight n.
   We show inductively that this minimal resolution is the displayed one. Degree one has generators
V , and degree two has generators R2 . In degree n, the weight-n part of the preceding free module
is V ⊗ Cn−1 . Its kernel under the preceding differential is

                                 (V ⊗ Cn−1 ) ∩ (R2 ⊗ V ⊗(n−2) ) = Cn .

The first factor imposes all relation conditions except the first adjacent pair; the second imposes
that last condition. All minimal syzygy generators have this weight, so they are generated by this
kernel. Their inclusion and differential are exactly first-letter multiplication. This proves exactness
of the Koszul complex, hence (iv).
   Finally in the Koszul case the bar coalgebra is quasi-isomorphic to C. The Yoneda product is the
dual of its coproduct. The annihilator description of Cn says that the dual tensor algebra is divided
by the ideal generated by R2⊥ . All weight pieces are finite-dimensional, so no full-dual completion is
being substituted for this graded Yoneda algebra. This proves the last assertion.

   The differential in the usual left module bar has the internal-face sign opposite to the reduced-bar
convention used at the start of the book. Multiplying length-n reduced words by (−1)n identifies
those reduced complexes. This harmless global change must be made explicitly when comparing
signed chain maps; it has no effect on the intersection spaces, exactness, or the diagonal Tor criterion
above.
   This theorem is a quadratic recognition theorem. The unconditional counit ΩBA → A is not: it
holds for algebras which fail every diagonal purity test. The difference is the small subcoalgebra C
and its comparison to the full bar.

                                                    23
Chapter 6. Modules, quadratic recognition, and the exact Morita domain


6.4     A weightwise duality without an undeclared Hom topology
Suppose a positive-weight dg algebra has perfect complexes in every weight. In a fixed total
weight, its bar uses finitely many word lengths and finitely many weight decompositions. Define the
weightwise dual Dw by dualizing each perfect weight piece and retaining the weight label; products
after duality are completed in that same weight. Then

                                           K(A) = Dw BA

is a completed augmented associative algebra. The dual of deconcatenation is concatenation, and
the transpose bar differential is a derivation.
   The finite-weight signed calculation gives Dw BA ∼
                                                    = Ω(Dw A) when the right side is applied to
the dual coalgebra, with the corresponding continuous completion. Applying the identity twice and
using biduality gives
                                       K2 (A) ≃ ΩBA ≃ A
in the weight-complete category. This is a contravariant equivalence on the finite-perfect-weight
class. Its proof is weightwise finite duality and the cut-complex reconstruction; no infinite sum is
interchanged with an algebraic dual.
   This construction can be compared with RHomA (ℸ, ℸ) only after choosing the latter’s grading
and continuity conventions. For A = ℸ[x] the ordinary double augmentation dual is ℸ[[x]], not ℸ[x].
The weight-complete statement agrees with that example. It does not turn an uncompleted module
category into a complete one by notation.


6.5     What a compact probe reconstructs
Let A be a dg algebra over a field and let P be a perfect left A-module. Set

                          E = RHomA (P, P )op ,         P ∨ = RHomA (P, A).

The opposite is intentional: P is an (A, E)-bimodule by evaluation, and P ∨ is an (E, A)-bimodule.
There are adjoint functors

                             P ⊗LE − : ModE ⇄ ModA : RHomA (P, −).

Perfectness gives the finite-dual identification

                                     RHomA (P, M ) ≃ P ∨ ⊗LA M.

It holds for P = A, is preserved by shifts and finite sums, by taking a cone, and by taking a retract.
These operations generate the perfect modules, so this is a proof rather than a claim that every
module has a dual.

Theorem 6.3 (The precise Morita theorem). The adjunction restricts to an equivalence between
the thick subcategory generated by P and Perf(E). It restricts further to an equivalence between the
localizing subcategory generated by P and all of ModE . It is an equivalence with all of ModA exactly
when P generates that category.



                                                   24
Chapter 6. Modules, quadratic recognition, and the exact Morita domain


Proof. The adjunction counit is an equivalence on P and the unit is an equivalence on the free
E-module, by the definition of E and the dual evaluation. The loci where the comparisons are
equivalences are closed under shifts, cones, finite sums and retracts. This proves the statement on
perfect and thick subcategories.
   Since P is perfect, the right adjoint RHomA (P, −) preserves coproducts; the finite-dual formula
proves this directly. Both adjoints are exact, so their equivalence loci are localizing. The same
generator argument proves the localizing statement. Finally every object of ModA lies in that image
exactly when the localizing subcategory generated by P is all of ModA . This is the stated generation
condition, not a consequence of faithfulness of the ordinary action.

   The Weyl counterexample remains in force. Its polynomial vacuum is perfect and faithful but has
a nonzero derived-orthogonal module, so it generates a proper localizing subcategory. The theorem
reconstructs that subcategory and no more.


6.6     The two-sided square when the generator hypothesis holds
Assume now that P is a perfect generator. The evaluation maps give equivalences of bimodules

                                 P ⊗LE P ∨ ≃ A,        P ∨ ⊗LA P ≃ E.

The first is the Morita counit at A; the second is the finite-dual calculation of the endomorphisms
of P . For an A-bimodule M set
                                      Φ(M ) = P ∨ ⊗LA M ⊗LA P.
Its inverse is N 7→ P ⊗LE N ⊗LE P ∨ . Associativity of the actual two-sided bar tensors and the two
evaluation equivalences cancel the adjacent kernels in both composites. They also give

                          Φ(A) ≃ E,       Φ(M ⊗LA N ) ≃ Φ(M ) ⊗LE Φ(N ).

All coherence diagrams use the same reassociation and evaluation maps, so this is a monoidal
equivalence of bimodule categories.

Corollary 6.4 (The Morita centre square). Under this hypothesis, Z(A) ≃ Z(E) as E2 algebras.
The diagonal bimodule and every actual bimodule action are transported by Φ.

Proof. The centre is the endomorphism object of the tensor unit in the bimodule category. A
monoidal equivalence preserving that unit preserves its two coherent compositions and hence its E2
structure. The displayed formula sends the diagonal to the diagonal, proving the claim. The internal
endomorphism-of-unit theorem is the same ordinary Deligne input used elsewhere; the nontrivial
assertion here is the constructed monoidal equivalence with its actual generator domain.

   Koszul–Morita statements require identifying which of these hypotheses a proposed augmentation
or boundary probe satisfies, or replacing the domain by an explicitly completed or coderived category.
The word bar construction alone does not establish the generator hypothesis. A two-sided square
whose corner categories have not been supplied is not the theorem just proved.




                                                  25
Chapter 7

The positive-weight Koszul–Morita
square

The scalar bar–cobar theorem has a module version, but its category must be specified. This
chapter proves the comparison for nonnegative auxiliary weights over a field. It supplies the module–
comodule equivalence, the two-sided comparison, and the tensor–cotensor law in that category.
It does not replace the corresponding coderived or unbounded-weight statements by an unstated
hypothesis.
   Let A = ℸ ⊕ w>0 Aw be an augmented dg algebra with positive auxiliary weight, and put
                 L
                                                     L
C = BA. Differentials preserve weight. A module M = w≥0 Mw has nonnegative weights and an
action adding weights. A left C-comodule N has the same kind of grading, with coaction
                                                                               M
                     ∆N (n) = 1 ⊗ n + ∆N (n),               ∆N (Nw ) ⊂               Ci ⊗ Nw−i .
                                                                               i>0

Iterating its reduced coaction at most w times gives zero on weight w. Thus conilpotence is a
consequence of the stated weight condition. There is no cohomological boundedness hypothesis.
Weak equivalences mean quasi-isomorphisms in each auxiliary weight.


7.1    The two twisting functors and their signs
Let τ : C → A be the canonical degree-one twisting cochain, sending [a] to a and all other reduced
lengths to zero. It obeys
                                 dA τ + τ dC + µ(τ ⊗ τ )∆C = 0
with the graded tensor signs fixed at the start of the book.
  Define K(M ) = C ⊗ M , with left coaction ∆C ⊗ 1, and differential

                                     dK = dC ⊗ 1 + 1 ⊗ dM + βM ,

where on a nonempty word
                                                   P
                                                              (|aj |−1)
                   βM ([a1 | · · · |an ] ⊗ m) = (−1)    j<n               [a1 | · · · |an−1 ] ⊗ an m.

It is zero on the empty word. In particular βM ([a] ⊗ m) = am: there is no extra (−1)|a| in this
last-slot action. The internal differential and this action anticommute by the module Leibniz rule.

                                                       26
Chapter 7. The positive-weight Koszul–Morita square


The two arity-two terms are (−1)|a| abm from bar multiplication and (−1)|a|−1 abm from two last-slot
actions, and cancel. Disjoint operations cancel by tensor signs. This proves d2K = 0 in every word
length, and deconcatenation commutes with the differential by separating cuts before and after the
affected slot.
   Define L(N ) = A ⊗ N with its free left action and differential
   dL (a ⊗ n) = dA a ⊗ n + (−1)|a| a ⊗ dN n − (−1)|a|          aτ (c) ⊗ n′ ,                              c ⊗ n′ .
                                                        X                                          X
                                                                                      ∆N (n) =                       (7.1)
The minus sign is essential. On a generator 1 ⊗ n, the internal/twisting anticommutator is minus
dA τ + τ dC , while two twistings give minus µ(τ ⊗ τ )∆C . Their sum vanishes by the twisting equation.
The graded Leibniz rule extends this calculation to a ⊗ n. Coassociativity accounts for every
twice-coacted term, so d2L = 0.
Proposition 7.1 (The twisting adjunction). There are natural bijections between dg A-module
maps L(N ) → M and dg C-comodule maps N → K(M ). The counit and unit are
                                                           c ⊗ 1 ⊗ n′                                c ⊗ n′ .
                                                       X                                         X
         εM (a ⊗ c ⊗ m) = aεC (c)m,        ηN (n) =                            for ∆N (n) =

Proof. A graded free-module map out of A ⊗ N is determined by a degree-zero map f : N → M .
Equation (7.1) says that it is a chain map exactly when
                                  dM f − f dN + µM (τ ⊗ f )∆N = 0.
A graded map from N to the cofree comodule C ⊗ M is determined by its counit component f ,
and is (1 ⊗ f )∆N . Projecting its chain-map defect to M gives precisely the same equation. A map
or a map defect into a cofree comodule is determined by that projection, so the condition is also
sufficient. This proves the adjunction and the displayed unit and counit. The two triangle identities
follow by applying the coalgebra counit to the coaction, or the algebra unit to the action. These are
actual maps, not an existence statement about a duality.


7.2     The counit is the ordinary module bar
The complex LK(M ) = A ⊗ BA ⊗ M has its internal differential, bar multiplication, a negative
first action and a positive last action in the one-letter case. It is the normalized two-sided module
resolution, in the chosen shifted signs. Here is the full comparison with the totalization convention
of Theorem 6.1.
   For homogeneous unsuspended inputs of simplicial degree n and internal total degree q, the
differential of that totalization is dint + (−1)q ∂. The signed identification with A ⊗ (sA)⊗n ⊗ M is
                                                    Pn
                                                              i|ai |+n|m|
               a0 ⊗ a1 ⊗ · · · ⊗ an ⊗ m 7−→ (−1)n+      i=1                 a0 ⊗ [a1 | · · · |an ] ⊗ m.              (7.2)
Increasing |ai | by one changes the exponent by i; increasing |m| changes it by n. These are exactly
the internal suspension signs. For the first face the change of the exponent is −1 − i≥1 |ai | − |m|,
                                                                                      P

converting (−1)q into −(−1)|a0 | . For an interior face i, the resulting exponent is ij=0 |aj | + i − 1,
                                                                                     P

which is the prefix sign times the binary suspended bar sign. For the last face it is n−1
                                                                                      j=0 |aj | + n − 1,
                                                                                     P

equal in parity to the last-slot action sign. Thus every face and every internal term is checked.
    The unnormalized module bar is contracted by unit insertion. Its degeneracy subcomplex is
contractible, and the normalized quotient is LK(M ) under (7.2). Hence the counit LK(M ) → M
is a weightwise quasi-isomorphism. Its proof uses only the actual module axiom and the normalized
simplicial identities, not Koszul purity of A.

                                                  27
Chapter 7. The positive-weight Koszul–Morita square


7.3     Why the unit is also an equivalence in this category
Both K and L preserve weightwise quasi-isomorphisms. For K in total weight w, filter increasingly by
the weight of its C factor. Twisting lowers that weight; the finite associated-graded pieces are tensor
products Ci ⊗ Mw−i with their internal differential. Over a field these preserve quasi-isomorphisms.
For L, filter increasingly by the weight of N . Its twisting lowers that weight, and the same finite
tensor argument applies. Only finitely many integers between zero and w occur, although their
weight spaces may be infinite-dimensional complexes.
   The functor L is conservative on this weight category. If L(N ) is acyclic, its weight-zero part is
N0 , so N0 is acyclic. Inductively suppose all Nj with j < w are acyclic. In (A ⊗ N )w , the terms
with positive A weight form a subcomplex, since twisting raises that weight. Its finite filtration
has acyclic tensor quotients by the induction hypothesis. The quotient is Nw . Thus Nw is acyclic
as well. This proves conservativity by induction, and the same argument applied to cones detects
quasi-isomorphisms.

Theorem 7.2 (Positive-weight module–comodule reconstruction). After inverting weightwise quasi-
isomorphisms, L and K are inverse equivalences between the nonnegative-weight module and comodule
categories specified above. The statement holds for the corresponding weight-complete categories with
completed weight tensor products.

Proof. The counit was proved to be a quasi-isomorphism. The triangle identity gives

                                      εL(N ) ◦ L(ηN ) = idL(N ) .

Hence L(ηN ) is a quasi-isomorphism. Conservativity of L makes ηN a quasi-isomorphism. Since
the functors preserve the chosen equivalences, the adjunction descends and its unit and counit are
equivalences. This proves the claimed equivalence, including its natural mapping comparisons. For
weight completion, apply each map and each finite filtration in weight w, then take the product over
w. Products of complexes of vector spaces preserve these weightwise equivalences. The coproducts
and coactions land in the tensor completed in that same weight; ordinary elementwise conilpotence
of the product is not asserted.

   The lower weight bound cannot simply be omitted. For A = ℸ[x] with x of positive weight,
the module M = ℸ[x, x−1 ] has K(M ) = ℸ ⊗LA M ≃ 0 because multiplication by x is invertible
on M . Yet M ̸= 0. It has weights unbounded below and is not in the theorem’s category. The
counterexample shows precisely where the induction and conservativity would fail.


7.4     Bimodules and the diagonal bicomodule
Applying the same construction on both sides sends an A–B bimodule M to

                                    KA,B (M ) = BA ⊗ M ⊗ BB,

with the left and right coactions, the bar differentials, and the two module twisting terms. The
right-sided signs are obtained by applying the graded opposite operation to the left-sided formulas;
the tensor interchange then fixes their total signs. Because the original left and right actions
commute, the two twisting terms anticommute in the total differential. Applying the one-sided
equivalences successively proves the bimodule–bicomodule equivalence in the same weight class.

                                                  28
Chapter 7. The positive-weight Koszul–Morita square


   For the regular diagonal M = A, the map
                                                                 X
                         BA −→ BA ⊗ A ⊗ BA,              c 7−→       c(1) ⊗ 1 ⊗ c(2)

is a chain map of bicomodules. The two twisting contributions cancel by coassociativity, exactly
as in the unit of the one-sided adjunction. It is that unit applied to the regular comodule, hence
a quasi-isomorphism. Thus the regular diagonal bimodule corresponds to the regular diagonal
bicomodule by a specified comparison, not by an identification of their underlying graded spaces.


7.5     Derived tensor becomes derived cotensor
For a right C-comodule P and a left C-comodule Q, the unnormalized two-sided cosimplicial cobar
has terms P ⊗ C ⊗r ⊗ Q for r ≥ 0. Its first coface is the right coaction on P , its internal cofaces
are coproducts on the inserted C factors, and its last coface is the left coaction on Q. The coface
differential is r+1         i
               P
                   i=0 (−1) di . For internal degree q, total degree is q + r and the total differential is
dint + (−1) q P        i
                i (−1) di . Normalize by the counit codegeneracies. The degree-r normalized term then
inserts r copies of the reduced coaugmentation coideal, with their desuspensions and the induced
Koszul signs. This is the derived cotensor used here. All coaction and coproduct maps are the
actual coalgebra maps. In a fixed total positive weight there are only finitely many normalized
lengths, so the totalization has no unbounded length ambiguity.
Theorem 7.3 (The tensor–cotensor comparison). For composable positive-weight bimodules M, N
there is a natural equivalence

                             KA,D (M ⊗LB N ) ≃ KA,B (M ) □R
                                                          BB KB,D (N ).

It is compatible with triple composition and the diagonal comparisons.

Proof. The right side has a concrete two-sided cobar resolution. Its two relevant endpoints are
cofree as graded BB-comodules, so the ordinary cotensor identifies with

                                      BA ⊗ M ⊗ BB ⊗ N ⊗ BD,

embedded in the degree-zero cobar term by the coproduct of its middle BB. The induced differential
is exactly the middle bar differential plus the right B-action on M and left B-action on N , together
with the outer differentials. Coassociativity makes the cobar differential of this inclusion zero.
Compatibility with the vertical differential follows by separating a coproduct cut from the slot on
which a bar or module face acts. Thus the inclusion is a chain map.
   It is a quasi-isomorphism. Filter by the total number of original B letters across all middle BB
factors. Bar multiplication and the B-module twisting terms lower this number; the cobar coproduct
preserves it. At fixed total weight this is a finite filtration. On associated graded the two endpoints
are genuinely cofree dg comodules without the lowering twists. The augmented cosimplicial complex
then has the elementary counit contraction: apply the coalgebra counit to its first cofree BB factor.
The term splitting that factor gives the identity, while all other splits cancel with the opposite order
of contraction. At augmented length the counit composed with the coproduct is the identity on the
single retained BB. This proves the associated-graded quasi-isomorphism and hence the original
one by the finite filtration.
   The surviving middle complex M ⊗ BB ⊗ N is exactly the normalized two-sided bar for M ⊗LB N .
Including the two outer coactions identifies it with the left side. Its signs are the same module-bar

                                                    29
Chapter 7. The positive-weight Koszul–Morita square


signs as (7.2) and their right-opposite form. For three bimodules, both comparisons use the same
two retained middle bars. Coassociativity makes the two endpoint inclusions equal, and Fubini for
their finite weightwise totalizations identifies the two orders of collapse. The diagonal case is the
preceding bicomodule unit. This proves associativity and unit compatibility of the comparison.

   Consequently the equivalence of endomorphisms of the regular diagonal is compatible with the
monoidal composition of bimodules and bicomodules. Since morphisms in the stated categories
preserve auxiliary weight, its first conclusion is the weight-preserving centre comparison

   Z wt=0 (A) := RHomweight-graded A-bimodules (A, A) ≃ RHomweight-graded BA-bicomodules (BA, BA),

with the ordinary internal E2 structure on endomorphisms of the monoidal unit. This is the
Hochschild–coHochschild corner of the Koszul–Morita square in its proved weight-preserving domain.
   There is a precise extension to other homogeneous operation weights. The same proofs work
when each module or comodule has any fixed lower bound b on auxiliary weight: start the induction
at b and bound normalized word lengths in total weight w by w − b. The functors commute with
auxiliary weight shifts. Applying the equivalence to maps from the diagonal into each fixed weight
shift of the diagonal gives the comparison for every homogeneous component of the graded internal
centre, compatibly with their compositions. An unrestricted product over operation weights, an
ungraded Hom containing arbitrary weight-mixing maps, or a continuous Hom for another filtration
is not silently identified with that graded centre. Its topology and continuity require their own
comparison. The name “coHochschild” here refers to the displayed actual derived bicomodule
endomorphism object, not to a substituted native chiral deformation complex.
   The general theory has classical antecedents in bar–cobar and Koszul duality [7, 25]. The proofs
here locate the finite filtrations, the unit map, and the tensor–cotensor comparison on which this
particular complete-weight statement depends. Extension to another weight range, a nonlinear
factorization subcategory, or another notion of coalgebra weak equivalence requires a new comparison,
rather than dropping those hypotheses from the theorem just proved.




                                                 30
Chapter 8

Locality, the two products, and their
units

8.1    The species calculation
For a species F , define the Hadamard and Cauchy products by
                                                                  M
                (F ⊙ G)(I) = F (I) ⊗ G(I),      (F ∗ G)(I) =             F (S) ⊗ G(T ).
                                                                 I=S⊔T

The decomposition is ordered and may have empty blocks. The units differ: the Hadamard unit is
ℸ on every finite set; the Cauchy unit is ℸ only at the empty set.
   Expanding (A∗B)⊙(C∗D) gives two independent decompositions of I. Expanding (A⊙C)∗(B⊙D)
gives one. Including the summands where the decompositions coincide gives a natural map ι from
the latter to the former, after the middle tensor swap. Projection to those summands gives π in the
reverse direction. Thus
                                    πι = 1,     e = ιπ,    e2 = e.
The image is the aligned sector. On three iterated decompositions the two possible successive
projectors both retain exactly the triple-equal sector. This proves the elementary coherence of the
projector. It does not make e the identity.
   If F is nonzero on an r-element set with r > 0, choose disjoint such sets S, T . The summand
indexed by (S, T ) in one factor and (T, S) in the other is nonzero and is killed by e. No full
subcategory containing the constant Hadamard unit can turn every such comparison into an
isomorphism. Restriction of one comparison to the image of its projector is a different statement.
  For the singleton-supported species F ({∗}) = ℸ, F (I) = 0 otherwise,

                                     (F ∗ F )({1, 2}) = ℸ ⊕ ℸ.

The two summands are the nonempty decompositions ({1}, {2}) and its reverse. They are not
empty-block terms. No quotient or no-go theorem may be deduced from the opposite count.




                                                31
Chapter 8. Locality, the two products, and their units


8.2     The direction of interchange
A useful double-monoid compatibility uses the inclusion direction:
                                  m⊥ (mX ⊙ mX )ι = mX (m⊥ ∗ m⊥ ).
Its unit diagrams must also be imposed. The projection-direction analogue has a severe degeneracy
with the two units. In the double sum use decompositions (I, ∅) and (∅, I). Cauchy multiplication
by its unit produces arbitrary elements x, y ∈ A(I), while projection kills their mismatched pair.
The law would force m⊥ (x, y) = 0. The Hadamard unit then forces A(I) = 0 in positive arity.
   This is a refutation of that particular direction with those unit axioms. It is not a refutation
of every homotopy-coherent mixed structure. For a nondegenerate combinatorial example use
A(I) = End(V ⊗I ), composition as the Hadamard product, and tensor product on aligned disjoint
decompositions. The middle-factor interchange is ordinary functoriality of tensor products of
operators. This example verifies the combinatorics and does not manufacture a chiral collision map.


8.3     Factorization is not a stable subcategory
A factorization condition identifies the value at a disjoint union with a tensor product of the values.
For two such objects F, G, the pointwise direct sum at two points has only the two summands
F12 ⊕ G12 . Factorization of the one-point sum would require
                                        (F1 ⊕ G1 ) ⊗ (F2 ⊕ G2 ),
which has two additional cross terms. Thus pointwise direct sums do not in general remain factorizing.
A category defined by these equivalences cannot simply be called a stable linear ambient category.
    This does not prohibit every geometric realization in a factorization category. A preservation
theorem can hold for specified sifted colimits when tensor commutes with those colimits. The point
is that tensor coalgebras, kernels, cones and normalization must first be formed in an actual stable
category, or be justified by the appropriate nonlinear universal property. The failure of direct sums
is not a proof that all realizations fail.
   Compatibility must include every operation. A binary equivariant product need not make an
A∞ structure equivariant. For example let a reduced graded space have a in degree zero and b in
degree −1, with m2 = 0, m3 (a, a, a) = b, and every operation involving b zero. All composites of
two nonzero higher products vanish, so this is a minimal reduced A∞ algebra. The involution fixing
a and negating b preserves m2 but not m3 . The same logical issue applies to a proposed locality
test checking only binary multiplication squares.


8.4     The actual chiral tensor
Here is the geometric input used for the native associative construction. Work in the enhanced
stable category of right D-modules on the nonempty Ran space of a smooth characteristic-zero curve.
For r objects its chiral tensor on a finite-set chart is described by the finite sum over surjections
π : I ↠ {1, . . . , r}, taking external products of the block components, restricting to the locus
where different blocks are disjoint, and extending by the chiral direct-image convention. This is
the standard Ran chiral tensor of [1, 2]. Its composition and transition coherences are part of that
construction, not supplied merely by the species count.

                                                   32
Chapter 8. Locality, the two products, and their units


   No surjection exists when r > |I|. Consequently a chiral word of length r vanishes on charts of
cardinality less than r. At a finite support-cardinality stage, sufficiently long tensor products vanish.
This is the pro-nilpotent mechanism behind the operadic theorem, and it differs from an assumed
positive conformal weight.
   The nonempty Ran tensor is nonunital. A unit for an augmented associative problem is adjoined
externally when required. The pointwise tensor and the chiral tensor are different monoidal
structures, with different support behavior. In particular the finite-cardinality word bound for the
latter is not a word bound for the former.


8.5     Three bars, with their domains
The following notation fixes the distinction.
   For an augmented associative algebra for a specified transverse or pointwise tensor, B ⊥ is the
ordinary associative word bar for that tensor. For an associative algebra for the actual chiral
           ch is the associative operadic bar for that tensor. Its finite-cardinality stages admit
tensor, BAss
the cut-complex reconstruction of Chapter 2, using the pro-nilpotent framework. For a chiral Lie
            ch is the chiral Chevalley construction, with symmetric suspended chains and the Jacobi
algebra, BLie
differential.
    There is a commutator morphism from associative to Lie operations in any suitable symmetric
monoidal category. There is also a bar–Chevalley comparison for an actual enveloping algebra with
its PBW and convergence hypotheses. These facts do not identify the three displayed bars on an
arbitrary object. Even over a point the comparison from the Chevalley chains of the augmentation-
ideal commutator algebra to the associative bar need not be an equivalence. For A = ℸ[x], the
commutator Lie algebra xℸ[x] is abelian. Its nonzero degree-one class x2 maps to [x2 ] = b[x|x], a
boundary in BA.
   A formal-disc realization of a chiral Lie operation uses the full singular distribution and yields
a vertex-type bracket. An associative chiral algebra and a nonlocal vertex algebra have different
a priori operation domains. An equivalence requires vacuum, truncation, expansion and descent
comparisons; it does not follow from writing both products in a spectral parameter. This distinction
remains in force in the explicit field examples later.

Chapter 9

A collision is a derived algebra

9.1    Two charts and one overlap
Let R = ℸ[z], or ℸ[[z]] when a formal base is explicitly intended, and put K = R[z −1 ]. Glue two
copies of Spec R along Spec K. The finite Cech complex for derived functions is

                            C 0 = R ⊕ R,       C 1 = K,     d(a, b) = b − a.

Its Alexander–Whitney multiplication is

               (a, b)(c, d) = (ac, bd),    (a, b)v = av,     v(c, d) = vd,     vw = 0.

These formulas are associative. For example ((a, b)v)(c, d) = avd = (a, b)(v(c, d)). The Leibniz
identity on two degree-zero inputs is

                                     bd − ac = (b − a)d + a(d − c),

and the remaining cases either reduce to this scalar action or have degree-two target zero. Thus
this is a dg associative algebra, with unit (1, 1).
  Its cohomology is H 0 = R and H 1 = J = K/R. The induced algebra is the square-zero extension
H = R ⊕ J[−1]. This does not make C quasi-isomorphic to H as an R-algebra.
Theorem 9.1 (Relative nonformality detected by idempotents). There is no zigzag of R-linear dg
algebra quasi-isomorphisms between C and its cohomology algebra H. Their derived special fibres
have respectively the degree-zero algebras

                            C ⊗LR ℸ ≃ ℸ × ℸ,       H 0 (H ⊗LR ℸ) ∼
                                                                 = ℸ[θ]/θ2 .

Proof. Both terms of C are flat and the complex is bounded. Hence ordinary tensor computes
its derived fibre. Localization K ⊗R ℸ is zero, and the remaining degree-zero multiplication is
componentwise on ℸ2 .
   For the cohomology algebra resolve J by [R ,→ K] in degrees −1, 0. Form the square-zero
dg algebra R ⊕ [R ,→ K][−1]. It is a bounded flat R-complex and maps multiplicatively by a
quasi-isomorphism to H. Its fibre is ℸ ⊕ ℸθ in degree zero, with θ2 = 0. Derived extension of scalars
preserves algebra equivalences, so a zigzag would identify these two fibre algebras. The first has
two nontrivial orthogonal branch idempotents and no nonzero nilpotent; the second has a nonzero
nilpotent and no such idempotent decomposition. They are not isomorphic.

                                                   34
Chapter 9. A collision is a derived algebra


    The underlying cohomological dimensions of these fibres are equal. The test detects multiplication,
not a missing vector-space dimension. It also explains why replacing a derived coefficient algebra by
its cohomology before specialization can corrupt an otherwise exact calculation.


9.2     A commutative coefficient model
In characteristic zero the same derived functions admit a commutative model

                T 0 = {f (s) ∈ K[s] : f (0), f (1) ∈ R},        T 1 = K[s]ds,   df = f ′ (s)ds.

Products are polynomial products with ds2 = 0. Define
                                                                                     Z 1
                   I(f ) = (f (0), f (1)),                                I(gds) =         g(s)ds,
                                                                                      0
                W (a, b) = (1 − s)a + sb,                                 W (v) = vds,
                            Z s                Z 1
                 h(gds) =         g(t)dt − s         g(t)dt,               h(f ) = 0.
                             0                  0

Then IW = 1 and dh + hd = 1 − W I. The endpoint conditions for h hold because its values at
both endpoints are zero.
                      R1
                           On a polynomial f , the last identity is f − f (0) − s(f (1) − f (0)); on a
one-form it is gds − ( 0 g)ds.
   Integration is not multiplicative for the ordinary Cech cup product. In fact

                       W (a, b)W (c, d) − W (ac, bd) = −s(1 − s)(b − a)(d − c).

The comparison preserves the commutative structure homotopy coherently, or one retains the
commutative model itself. A linear contraction is not a strict algebra map. None of this changes
Theorem 9.1, which concerns the further replacement by the cohomology algebra.


9.3     Principal parts and supported derivatives
The quotient J = K/R has basis z −n−1 for n ≥ 0 in the polynomial case. Multiplication by z is
surjective on J and kills only the line ℸz −1 . Consequently

                                                    J ⊗LR ℸ ≃ ℸ[1].

The nonzero class is a Tor class, not the ordinary tensor J/zJ, which is zero.
   Let D = ℸ[z]⟨∂⟩/([∂, z] − 1). The left module D/Dz identifies with J by

                                             ∂ n δ 7−→ (−1)n n!z −n−1 .

The relation zδ = 0 is respected, and the commutator gives z∂ n δ = −n∂ n−1 δ, exactly the same
action on principal parts. Both modules have the displayed bases, so the map is an isomorphism.
This is the derivative-supported target that a scalar residue discards.
   Indeed Res(f (z)dz) is ℸ-linear on J, but is not R-linear to ℸ = R/(z). The residue of z · z −2 is
one, whereas z acts by zero on the target. Replacing the supported D-module by that scalar target
therefore breaks the module type of the operation.

                                                           35
Chapter 9. A collision is a derived algebra


9.4     A boundary transgression, not a projection
Restriction to the first chart is the dg algebra map ρ : C → R, given by (a, b) 7→ a and v 7→ 0. Its
kernel is [R ,→ K] in degrees 0, 1. Thus

                                              ker(ρ)[1] ≃ K/R

with the latter in degree zero. The shift records that a bulk principal-part class was in degree one.
Projection alone kills that class. A boundary operation using it must include a relative transgression,
residue or duality of the indicated degree, rather than treat projection as if it preserved a degree-zero
singular field.
   This construction supplies a coefficient-level map. To apply it to a bulk–boundary theory, a
state-space action compatible with the coefficient multiplication is still needed. The distinction is
especially important in arity three, where two routes to the boundary can differ by a nontrivial
homotopy.


9.5     The whole pole filtration
For a smooth divisor D ⊂ X 2 , the pole filtration has

                grm OX 2 (∗D) = OX 2 (mD)/OX 2 ((m − 1)D) ∼
                                                          = OD (mD),             m ≥ 1.

The last line is the mth tensor power of the normal bundle. It transforms nontrivially under a
change of transverse coordinate. For a meromorphic function am (z − w)−m , a logarithmic residue
                                                             P

of a one-form detects one normal symbol, not all of the higher symbols. Recovering higher modes
requires jets or the full localized operation. A coordinate-free inverse from a single logarithmic
generator to every higher principal part has not been defined by the residue identity.
   The same distinction appears under derived restriction. The localization K has zero derived fibre
at z = 0. The quotient K/R has a nonzero shifted fibre. A construction localizing first and then
taking a closed fibre cannot be identified with one retaining principal parts and then specializing.
The two operations have different exact triangles.


9.6     Duality changes the coefficient problem
On finite holonomic charts, Verdier duality changes direct-image extension across an open set
into proper-support extension and changes extraordinary inverse-image compatibility into ordinary
inverse-image compatibility. Transporting a chiral tensor through this duality therefore gives an
opposite-polarity tensor. It does not give an endofunctor of an unchanged coefficient category by
notation alone.
    For a finite dualizable word window the elementary identity is that the dual of the bar coalgebra
is the cobar of the dual coalgebra. The dual deconcatenation is multiplication. In the suspension
convention used here, the shift map is (tf )(sa) = (−1)|f | f (a). Transposing the two bar cogenerator
maps then gives exactly the two cobar generator maps. Finite charts allow this identity to assemble
when their duality transformations and transitions agree. The dual of a bar is already an algebra;
applying a coalgebra cobar functor to that algebra would be a type error.


                                                    36
Chapter 9. A collision is a derived algebra


  Neither this identity nor Verdier biduality identifies a reflected-level vertex algebra with an
augmentation dual. That requires a separate representing pairing and a comparison with the relative
endomorphism algebra. The polarized construction and the diagonal centre of the later chapters
remain distinct.

10.1     The order-chart scheme
For a finite label set I put RI = ℸ[zi | i ∈ I] and SI = Spec RI . For each total order σ take an
affine chart Uσ = SI . If σ, τ disagree on some pair orders, put
                                                  Y
                                  fστ =                           (zi − zj ),
                                          {i,j} reversed by σ,τ

with one arbitrary orientation for each factor. Glue the charts by the identity on D(fστ ).
   For three orders, the union of inversion sets from any anchor is the set of pairs on which the three
orders do not all agree. Thus the two triple-overlap opens agree. The gluing maps are identities, so
satisfy the cocycle condition. The result is a scheme XI , with every finite chart intersection affine:
                                                                  −1 
                                                           r
                                                           Y
                            Uσ0 ∩ · · · ∩ Uσr = Spec RI    fσ0 σj   .
                                                                       
                                                                  j=1


The projection pI : XI → SI is etale, since its restriction to every chart is the identity. It is
quasi-compact and quasi-separated. It is not generally separated. This geometry is the order-chart
construction used in the cochain raviolo formalism [3].

Proposition 10.1 (Every residue fibre). Let p ⊂ RI be a prime. Partition I into blocks B by
equality of the coordinate images in κ(p). Then the fibre is the disjoint union of B |B|! copies of
                                                                                  Q

Spec κ(p).

Proof. A chart overlap survives precisely when all its inverted differences are nonzero in the residue
field. Thus two total orders give the same point precisely when their restrictions to each equality
block agree. Every tuple of block orders extends to a total order. Distinct tuples are not identified.
Chart fibres are reduced affine points and every overlap is either the whole point or empty, so this
proves the fibre as a scheme, not only its set of points.




                                                    38
Chapter 10. All orders, cyclic branches, and excision


10.2      Binary choices are too many
For each pair {i, j} let Dij be the two-chart double of SI along D(zi − zj ), and form

                                           PI =                   Dij .
                                                           Y

                                                     {i,j}⊂I,SI

Its charts are indexed by tournaments: one orientation of each pair. The total-order charts are those
indexed by transitive tournaments. At a total three-point collision the product has eight branches,
including the two directed three-cycles, whereas the order scheme has six.
   For a chosen directed triangle, take the closed locus where its three coordinates agree and its
three orientations have that cyclic value. It is closed: in the product of the three corresponding
doubles over the triple-equality locus it is a closed-and-open component, and the equality locus is
closed in the base. Pull back to PI . Let ZI be the finite union of all these directed-triangle loci.
Theorem 10.2 (Acyclic binary reconstruction). The inclusion of the total-order charts induces an
isomorphism
                                      XI ∼= PI \ ZI .
It respects relabelling and the given affine chart coordinates.

Proof. The intersections of tournament charts invert exactly the pair differences on which their
orientations disagree. Hence the intersections of total-order charts are precisely those defining XI .
Every such chart avoids the cyclic equality loci.
   Conversely take a point of the complement and form its equality blocks in the residue field.
Within a block, the retained orientations give a tournament with no directed triangle. Such a
tournament is transitive: if a ≺ b and b ≺ c but a ̸≺ c, there is a directed triangle. Thus it is a total
order on each block. Extend those block orders to a total order on I. Every orientation changed
during this extension joins different blocks, so the corresponding difference is invertible near the
point. The point therefore belongs to that total-order chart. These charts cover the complement
with exactly the already identified gluing, proving the isomorphism. Relabelling preserves all the
constructions.

   The fibre counts are now forced:
                                                  (|B|
                                          P
                                                    2 ),
                                                                               Y
                            #(PI,p ) = 2      B                 #(XI,p ) =         |B|!.
                                                                               B

A spanning tree of binary comparisons is also insufficient. With three coincident labels it gives four
binary branches, rather than six total orders. The correct geometry is neither all independent pair
choices nor a choice of tree edges alone.


10.3      The first derived excision term
For three labels remove the common translation coordinate and put R = ℸ[x, y], so the pair
differences are x, y, x + y. Let P = P3 , U = X3 , and
                                                                                 R[x−1 , y −1 ]
                 B = RΓ(P, OP ),        C = RΓ(U, OU ),                   M=                      .
                                                                               R[x−1 ] + R[y −1 ]
The quotient has basis x−a y −b , a, b ≥ 1.

                                                           39
Chapter 10. All orders, cyclic branches, and excision


Theorem 10.3 (Cyclic branches are removed by local cohomology). There is a natural distinguished
triangle of R-module complexes
                            (M ⊕ M )[−2] −→ B −→ C −→ (M ⊕ M )[−1].
At the total collision its derived specialization is the triangle
                                             ℸ2 −→ ℸ8 −→ ℸ6 ,
where the last map forgets the two cyclic branch coordinates.

Proof. The complement consists of two disjoint cyclic branch points. Excision identifies the support
complex at either point with the affine local-cohomology complex
                               [R −→ R[x−1 ] ⊕ R[y −1 ] −→ R[x−1 , y −1 ]],
in degrees 0, 1, 2. The first map is restriction and the second is the signed difference. Its only
cohomology is M in degree two. The localization triangle for the union of the two disjoint supports
gives the first assertion.
   The finite chart covers of P and U have respectively eight and six charts, and flat affine
intersections. Derived tensor with ℸ = R/(x, y) can be applied to their bounded Cech complexes.
Every intersection of distinct charts inverts at least one difference that vanishes in this fibre. Those
terms become zero. Hence B ⊗LR ℸ = ℸ8 and C ⊗LR ℸ = ℸ6 , with the stated restriction map.
   For completeness compute the support term independently. Tensoring the Koszul resolution of ℸ
with M gives
                                     w7→(−yw,xw)          (u,v)7→xu+yv
                                 M −−−−−−−−→ M 2 −−−−−−−−→ M.
Multiplication by x is surjective, and the common kernel of x, y is ℸx−1 y −1 . The middle term is
exact: for xu + yv = 0, choose w with xw = v. Then u + yw ∈ ker x. Multiplication by y is surjective
on ker x, so choose a ∈ ker x with ya = u + yw. Replacing w by w − a gives (u, v) = (−yw, xw).
Therefore M ⊗LR ℸ = ℸ[2], and each shifted support term contributes one degree-zero line. This
proves the specialized triangle including its degrees.

   The map B → C is a map of derived coefficient algebras. The distinguished triangle is asserted
in modules, not as a direct-sum splitting of algebras. The removed terms become genuine branch
idempotents after derived specialization. Before specialization they are local-cohomology classes
two degrees higher. A dimension count alone sees neither the support map nor this degree change.


10.4      An all-arity correction complex
The same method gives a finite constructive correction in every arity. Index the directed-triangle
closed loci by a finite set TI . For a nonempty subset J ⊂ TI , write ZJ = α∈J Zα . The standard
                                                                          T

support Mayer–Vietoris complex has terms
                                  M
                                         RΓZJ (PI , O)[r − 1],      r ≥ 1,
                                 |J|=r

with alternating maps deleting a support condition. Its totalization is RΓZI (PI , O). This follows
inductively from the two-support triangle
                              RΓZ1 ∩Z2 −→ RΓZ1 ⊕ RΓZ2 −→ RΓZ1 ∪Z2 .

                                                     40
Chapter 10. All orders, cyclic branches, and excision


The latter is obtained by the Cech complex of the two complementary opens. Finite induction
proves the formula, with no infinite convergence issue.
   This is an explicit algorithm rather than a new unnamed support object. On each tournament
chart a directed-triangle condition is either empty or the equality ideal for that triple. Intersections
of nonempty conditions identify all coordinates in the connected components of the resulting equality
graph. Choose one representative in each component. The differences to that representative form
an independent regular sequence in RI . The support complex is the finite tensor of the two-term
localization complexes for those differences. Use the finite affine chart Cech complex to glue them
and the preceding alternating support complex to form their union. Finally the cone of support
sections mapping to ordinary derived sections gives RΓ(XI , O).
   Thus every term, differential and restriction map of the correction can be written in localizations
of polynomial rings. At four or more labels, simply summing one term per triangle is wrong because
cyclic support loci can intersect. The intersection terms are exactly what the Mayer–Vietoris
complex retains.

Chapter 11

The mixed configuration space behind
the order charts

11.1      Recovering the real direction rather than naming it
Let I have n elements. Write a point of C × R as (z, t) and put

                            FI = {(zi , ti )i∈I : (zi , ti ) ̸= (zj , tj ) for i ̸= j}.

This is an open smooth manifold, not a formal configuration symbol. Its complex coordinates are
the zi and its real coordinates are the ti . The order-chart scheme XI = XI of Chapter 10 has an
analytic realization XIan obtained by exactly the same finite gluing of copies of CI . Nonseparatedness
is retained: at z1 = z2 the two choices of order remain two different points.
   There is a canonical map
                                              pI : FI −→ XIan .                                  (11.1)
Given a configuration, partition I by equality of its complex coordinates. Within each block the
real coordinates are distinct and therefore give a total order. Extend these block orders to any total
order σ on I and use the point z in the chart Uσ . Two extensions differ only on pairs whose complex
coordinates differ; precisely those differences are inverted in the overlap. The point of the glued
space is consequently independent of the extension.
   This construction does not choose a total order on the complex curve. It records the real order
only when equality of complex coordinates makes that order necessary to avoid a collision. That is
why the generic fibre has one branch and a total collision has n! branches.

Lemma 11.1 (The preimage of an order chart). For a total order σ,

                                p−1
                                                \                               
                                 I (Uσ ) =             {zi ̸= zj } ∪ {ti < tj } .
                                               i≺σ j
                                                                                          T
This is open in FI . For a nonempty set Σ of orders, the preimage of UΣ = σ∈Σ Uσ consists of
those configurations for which zi ̸= zj whenever two orders of Σ disagree on i, j, and for which every
remaining coincident pair has its common prescribed real order. In particular pI is continuous.

Proof. A point belongs to Uσ exactly when the orders on all equal-coordinate blocks are the
restrictions of σ. This is the displayed condition. The union inside each factor is open, and the

                                                         42
Chapter 11. The mixed configuration space behind the order charts


intersection is finite. Intersecting the conditions for all σ ∈ Σ forces noncoincidence on a pair
oriented both ways. On a pair oriented only one way it leaves the one indicated inequality when
the coordinates coincide. These are precisely the stated conditions on the intersection. Continuity
follows because the Uσ form an open cover and the coordinate expression of the map there is
projection to z.


11.2     A contraction on every multiple overlap
Choose an order σ0 ∈ Σ and distinct real constants ai increasing in that order. For z ∈ UΣ the
point (z, a) belongs to p−1
                         I (UΣ ). Set

                   sa (z) = (z, a),    Hu (z, t) = (z, (1 − u)t + ua),     0 ≤ u ≤ 1.

Every inequality which must hold is a strict linear inequality between two real coordinates, and
both its endpoints satisfy it. Their convex interpolation therefore satisfies it as well. A pair on
which the orders disagree has unequal complex coordinates throughout, since Hu does not move z.
Thus Hu remains in the same preimage for all u.

Proposition 11.2 (Overlapwise deformation retraction). Projection p−1  I (UΣ ) → UΣ is a homotopy
equivalence, with section sa and the displayed deformation retraction. The fibre over each point is a
nonempty convex open subset of RI . The set of choices of a respecting σ0 is convex as well.

Proof. The preceding calculation proves that H0 = id, H1 = sa pI and that Hu fixes the image of
sa . With z fixed, the only restrictions on t are strict inequalities within equality blocks. Their
intersection is convex and nonempty. The same observation proves the last assertion. No statement
about the contractibility of a groupoid of unmarked objects is involved.

   The contractions need not agree for different choices of an anchor chart. The comparison maps
used below do agree: they are the canonical pullbacks by pI . A local contraction proves that a
particular canonical map is a quasi-isomorphism; it is not used as an undeclared globally strict
choice of inverse.


11.3     The partial de Rham–Dolbeault complex
On an open subset V ⊂ FI , define

                               Aq,r        ∞        q           r
                                HT (V ) = C (V ) ⊗ Λ ⟨dz̄i ⟩ ⊗ Λ ⟨dti ⟩.

Smooth coefficients and the exterior generators are interpreted locally, so this notation describes a
sheaf. The differential is the restriction of the ordinary de Rham differential to these directions:

                                           DHT = ∂¯z + dt .
                                                2 = 0, its degree is one, and it is a derivation of the
The total exterior sign is used. In particular DHT
wedge product. A holomorphic derivative ∂zi is not included in DHT . This omission is mathematical
data: restoring it will change the cohomology in the next section.



                                                  43
Chapter 11. The mixed configuration space behind the order charts


   On the preimage of an overlap the homotopy H fixes the complex coordinates and is affine in the
real coordinates, independently of z. Its pullback therefore preserves partial differential forms. The
usual integration operator                     Z      1
                                         Kα =             ι∂u H ∗ α du
                                                  0
has degree −1. Differentiating H ∗ α and integrating in u gives

                                     DHT K + KDHT = p∗I s∗a − id                                   (11.2)

with the convention that H0 = id and H1 = sa pI ; replacing K by −K gives the opposite convention.
Equation (11.2) is the ordinary homotopy formula and involves no factorial division in the coefficient
ring of functions; the integrations are real analytic operations over C.

Theorem 11.3 (All-arity analytic coefficient comparison). There is a canonical equivalence of
derived sheaves of commutative differential graded algebras
                                                 ∼
                                          OXIan −−→ RpI∗ AHT

and hence an equivalence
                                      RΓ(XIan , O) ≃ Γ(FI , AHT ).
The first map is pullback of holomorphic functions. The comparison is defined for every finite I,
commutes with relabelling, and commutes with forgetting a subset of labels.

Proof. On p−1 I (UΣ ), the retraction and the homotopy formula identify the complex of mixed forms
with the Dolbeault complex of UΣ . Pullback of a holomorphic function is exactly the composite of
its inclusion in that Dolbeault complex and projection pullback. Each UΣ is the analytic complement
of a finite set of hyperplanes in CI , the analytification of a principal affine open. It is Stein. The
Dolbeault resolution has cohomology O(UΣ ) in degree zero and none in positive degrees.
   The assertion can also be checked on sufficiently small coordinate opens and their inverse images,
so it is a quasi-isomorphism of derived sheaves, not just a equality of dimensions of global cohomology.
The sheaf of mixed forms is fine on the open manifold FI , and its finitely many form degrees give an
acyclic sheaf resolution for global sections. Apply the finite-cover Cech comparison to the Uσ and
their preimages. Every multiple-overlap map is the canonical pullback just proved to be a quasi-
isomorphism; finite totalization preserves these quasi-isomorphisms. Multiplication is preserved by
pullback before taking cohomology. Compatible commutative derived-section models, for example
Thom–Whitney models of the finite diagrams, retain this multiplication and all coherences.
   A permutation just relabels (zi , ti ) and the orders. Forgetting labels restricts the equal-coordinate
orders to those labels. Both maps commute exactly with pI and preserve DHT . Naturality follows
from these equalities of maps, not from a simultaneous choice of the local contractions.

Remark 11.4 (Algebraic and analytic coefficients are different). The theorem concerns analytic
holomorphic functions. It does not identify RI [f −1 ] with all holomorphic functions on its ana-
lytification. Already for one label ez belongs to the analytic ring and not to C[z]. The algebraic
coefficient CI maps to the analytic coefficient, and supplies the algebraic, finite-pole sector used
earlier. Equivalence of those two coefficients is not asserted. Formal completion is yet another
specified coefficient operation. The analytic theorem realizes the actual mixed geometry without
using an invalid algebraization claim.



                                                      44
Chapter 11. The mixed configuration space behind the order charts


11.4      Topologization and the configuration ring
Add the holomorphic de Rham directions to AHT . The resulting differential is the full de Rham
differential of FI . On the order-chart side this gives the analytic de Rham complex. On every
affine overlap the algebraic de Rham comparison theorem identifies algebraic and analytic de Rham
cohomology; finite Cech totalization extends it to the glued space. Consequently there is a ring
comparison
                                       ∗
                                     HdR (XI /C) ∼= H ∗ (FI ; C).                            (11.3)
The external inputs in this step are precisely the smooth affine algebraic de Rham comparison [24]
and the ordinary de Rham theorem for smooth manifolds. The overlap contraction has already
supplied the part special to this nonseparated geometry.
   Identify C × R with oriented R3 . For i ̸= j, the direction map
                                                                          rj − ri
                                  dij : FI → S 2 ,           dij (r) =

gives a degree-two class aij = d∗ij [S 2 ], with the sphere volume normalized to integral one. The
antipodal map of S 2 has degree −1, so aji = −aij . Its square vanishes since H 4 (S 2 ) = 0.

Theorem 11.5 (The entire topologized cohomology ring). The ring in (11.3) is generated by the
aij of degree two, with relations

             aji = −aij ,      a2ij = 0,        aij ajk + ajk aki + aki aij = 0        (i, j, k distinct).

After ordering I = {1, . . . , n}, a vector-space basis consists of the products which, for every j ≥ 2,
choose either no factor or exactly one factor aij with i < j. In particular
                                       n−1
                                                                 dim H ∗ (FI ; C) = n!.
                                       Y
                            PI (q) =         (1 + jq 2 ),
                                       j=1


Proof. Forget the last point. A local trivialization is obtained by choosing disjoint small balls
around the retained points and moving those balls by compactly supported isotopies. This exhibits
the forgetting map as a locally trivial bundle, with fibre R3 minus n − 1 points. Removing small
disjoint balls and joining their boundaries to a common base point shows that this fibre has the
homotopy type of a wedge of n − 1 two-spheres. The restrictions of a1n , . . . , an−1,n integrate to
the Kronecker delta on those small sphere generators. They therefore give global classes lifting
a basis of the fibre cohomology. The Leray–Hirsch argument, proved by local trivializations and
Mayer–Vietoris, makes cohomology a free module over the preceding configuration ring with basis
1, a1n , . . . , an−1,n . Induction proves the asserted vector-space basis and Poincare polynomial.
   It remains to verify and exhaust the relations. The two-point relations were just proved. For
three labels, translation and positive dilation do not affect direction classes. Consider the two
four-cycles parametrized by u, v ∈ S 2 and a fixed 0 < ϵ < 1/2:

                        (r1 , r2 , r3 ) = (0, u, ϵv),        (r1 , r2 , r3 ) = (0, u, u + ϵv).

On the first, the pullbacks of (a12 , a13 , a23 ) are (α, β, −α); on the second they are (α, α, β). This
follows by contracting the small perturbation in every nonvanishing direction map. The classes



                                                            45
Chapter 11. The mixed configuration space behind the order charts


a12 a13 and a12 a23 pair with the two cycles as the two coordinate functionals. They form the basis
of H 4 (F3 ) given by Leray–Hirsch. Evaluation on both cycles now gives
                                       a13 a23 = a12 a23 − a12 a13 ,
which is the displayed cyclic relation. Pullback by forgetting the other labels proves it in every arity.
   Finally any product containing two distinct factors ain , ajn can be reduced by this relation to
terms with only one factor involving n; a repeated factor is zero. Repetition reduces all products to
the stated normal forms. Induction reduces the remaining factors. Their previously proved linear
independence shows that the relations generate the entire relation ideal. Multiplying the dimensions
of the successive free modules gives n−1
                                     Q
                                      j=1 (1 + j) = n!.

   The appearance of n! is now the conclusion of an actual cohomology calculation. It agrees with
the number of branches at the total algebraic collision, but those branches and these cohomology
classes have different degrees and different functors behind them. They must not be identified as
complexes. For n = 3 the ring has dimensions 1, 3, 2 in degrees 0, 2, 4, whereas the collision fibre is
six-dimensional in degree zero.


11.5      The two-point residue becomes a sphere class
Remove the centre-of-mass coordinates. The order-chart coefficient is the doubled line, with algebraic
overlap C[z, z −1 ]. Its Cech–de Rham double complex has a class in total degree two represented on
the overlap by
                                               1 dz
                                                    .
                                              2πi z
Polynomial one-forms on either chart are exact, and every Laurent one-form other than the z −1 dz
term has a Laurent antiderivative. Therefore this class generates the one-dimensional HdR   2 .

   Here is its topological normalization. On the unit sphere use polar coordinates (ϑ, φ), oriented
area
                                             sin ϑ
                                         ω=        dϑ ∧ dφ.
                                              4π
The regular potentials away from the south and north poles are
                                   1 − cos ϑ                      1 + cos ϑ
                            AN =             dφ,         AS = −             dφ.
                                      4π                             4π
Their derivatives are ω and their difference is (2π)−1 dφ. On an equatorial annulus this has the
same cohomology class as (2πi)−1 dz/z, since their difference is an exact multiple of d log |z|. Stokes’
theorem therefore identifies the Cech residue class with the unit-integral sphere class. This calculation
fixes the normalization of a12 .
   The degree-one principal part in RΓ(O) is not already the degree-two sphere class. The extra
holomorphic differential supplies the second degree. This is the concrete two-point reason why
topologization and a coefficient-level bar construction cannot be silently interchanged.


11.6      What is natural before a state space is assigned
All maps above are defined on coefficient objects independent of a chiral algebra. They commute
with label permutations, forgetting labels, translations (z, t) 7→ (z + c, t + b), and positive real

                                                    46
Chapter 11. The mixed configuration space behind the order charts


rescaling of the real coordinate. A holomorphic affine rescaling of z likewise commutes with the
quotient map, with its actual complex multiplier retained. On a separated block locus, restricting
equal-coordinate orders to each block gives the same factorization isomorphism as in Chapter 10.
These identities are equalities of maps before any cohomology is taken.
   For scale-marked nested holomorphic collisions the coordinate maps of the earlier coefficient
construction must still be evaluated in their common completed ring. The present result neither
sets a formal scale equal to one nor declares an arbitrary meromorphic state-field family to act. It
supplies a missing geometric comparison: the order charts are an explicit model for the coefficient
resolution of the actual mixed configuration space. The construction of operations on Zch (A), and
their compatibility with a boundary colour, remains a different morphism problem.




                                                 47
Chapter 12

Nested collisions with a common
coefficient domain

Two expansions of a rational function need not belong to the same iterated Laurent-series ring.
An assertion that their coefficients agree therefore has to name the ring first. The constructions
below retain both the chamber faces and the independent scales of a cluster tree. They develop the
scale-marked coefficient maps from the arithmetic volume [16] on the same order-chart coefficients
used in this book.


12.1      The faces of the coefficient simplex
For a finite set I, write RI = ℸ[zi : i ∈ I] and let Ord(I) be the finite set of total orders. For
a nonempty subset Σ ⊂ Ord(I), the overlap ring RΣ inverts exactly those differences zi − zj for
which two orders in Σ disagree. Let Rall invert every pair difference. The Thom–Whitney coefficient
algebra CI consists of polynomial forms on the simplex with vertices Ord(I), with coefficients in
Rall , whose restriction to each face Σ has its coefficients in RΣ . Equivalently it is the compatible
family over all faces constructed in Chapter 10. The differential acts on simplex variables and not
on the zi .
    For an ordered pair i, j, let Σij be all total orders putting i before j. The full regularity condition
is equivalent to the following pair-face condition: restriction to Σij has no pole at zi = zj .
   Here is the proof over a field. The full condition implies the pair condition. Conversely, if every
order in a face Σ puts i before j, then Σ ⊂ Σij ; restricting twice proves absence of that pole on Σ.
Distinct unordered coordinate differences are nonassociated prime elements in the UFD RI . Inside
the full localization, the intersection of the subrings omitting each of a list of prime denominators is
the subring omitting all of them: write a fraction in reduced form and inspect its prime factors.
Apply this coefficientwise to polynomial forms on the face. This proves the full condition. The
argument uses inclusion of faces. It does not claim that an arbitrary set of orders contains every
linear extension of its common partial order; the face {123, 321} already disproves that claim.




                                                    48
Chapter 12. Nested collisions with a common coefficient domain


12.2      The substitution on every affine overlap
Partition I = I1 ⊔ · · · ⊔ Im into nonempty labelled blocks. Give the blocks outer coordinates Za , the
leaves inner coordinates ua,i , and independent scales ϵa . The substitution is

                                               za,i 7−→ Za + ϵa ua,i .                                               (12.1)

An outer total order and one inner total order in each block give a lexicographic total order on the
leaves. This map on chart labels is injective. On a nonempty set of such source labels, take the
tensor product of the corresponding outer and inner overlap rings, complete in the scales, and then
invert their product. Denote it by

                                        BJ [[ϵ1 , . . . , ϵm ]][(ϵ1 · · · ϵm )−1 ].

An element has a lower bound for its exponent in each scale. This simultaneous bound is part of
the definition.
Lemma 12.1 (All denominators have legal inverses). The coordinate substitution extends to a ring
map from the leaf overlap for the lexicographic image of J to the displayed complete localization.
These maps commute with enlarging the overlap.

Proof. An inverted leaf difference is one on which two image orders disagree. Within a block, the
inner orders must disagree, and its image is ϵa (ua,i − ua,j ). Both factors are invertible in the stated
ring. Across different blocks, the outer orders disagree. Thus Za − Zb is a unit before completing.
The image difference is
                                     (Za − Zb ) + ϵa ua,i − ϵb ub,j ,
with inverse                                                                    r
                                         1          ϵa ua,i − ϵb ub,j
                                              X         
                                                  −                                   .
                                      Za − Zb r≥0       Za − Zb
The powered term belongs to the scale ideal. Multiplying a partial sum by the denominator leaves
a remainder of arbitrarily high scale order, so the series is its inverse in the complete ring. Only the
finitely many within-block denominators of a fixed rational input produce negative scale powers,
giving a finite lower bound in each variable. Localization therefore supplies the ring map. On a
larger overlap the same inverses remain inverses and are unique, so restriction commutes with the
maps.

   The proof distinguishes a unit leading term from a nonunit leading term. Expanding 1/(ϵ + η)
without specifying a region is not licensed by this lemma.

Let tρ be the barycentric coordinates of the leaf simplex, vσ those of the outer-order simplex, and
wa,τ those of its inner simplexes. Define
                                                  Y
                     tlex(σ,τ1 ,...,τm ) 7−→ vσ       wa,τa ,      tρ 7−→ 0 for other orders.                        (12.2)
