Send dtρ to the differential of its image. The images sum to (                            vσ )   a(    wa,τa ) = 1, so both
barycentric relations are respected.

Chapter 12. Nested collisions with a common coefficient domain


Theorem 12.2 (Completed cluster coefficient map). Over a characteristic-zero field, equations
(12.1) and (12.2) define a dg commutative algebra map
                                                           !
                                                       CIa [[ϵ1 , . . . , ϵm ]][(ϵ1 · · · ϵm )−1 ].
                                                  O
                      CI −→     C{1,...,m} ⊗ℸ
                                                   a

It is equivariant under relabelling blocks and leaves. The differential of every scale is zero.

Proof. The rational coefficients have the map of the lemma, and the barycentric formula is a
polynomial differential-form map. We must show that it lands in the face-regular subalgebra, not
just the ambient full localization. Choose an outer face and inner faces. Their product maps into
the face of their lexicographic leaf orders. If the outer face always puts a before b, every image order
has that same cross-block orientation. The leaf form restricted there has no pole in any cross-block
difference between those blocks. Its substituted coefficients consequently do not require Za − Zb
to be inverted. If an inner face fixes the order of two leaves, the identical argument excludes the
corresponding inner pole. Every denominator which remains is therefore allowed on the chosen
product of faces. This applies also to coefficients of exterior simplex forms, since the barycentric
pullback preserves faces and commutes with differential.
   The target written as a tensor product requires one more check. Each coefficient algebra is the
intersection of finitely many kernels of linear face-obstruction maps in its ambient polynomial-form
space. Over a field, tensoring preserves kernels and finite intersections. Impose the outer face
conditions and then each inner set of conditions. Their common kernel inside the tensor product
of the ambient spaces is exactly the displayed tensor product of the separate coefficient algebras.
Extract each scale coefficient in this argument. The preceding denominator proof gives the common
finite lower bounds; hence the complete series has the stated target. Relabelling permutes the same
variables and vertex labels, proving equivariance.

   Over a general ring the overlap substitution still exists, but the last tensor-kernel argument
cannot be imported unchanged. A derived tensor construction or an independently flat model is
required. This is one reason to retain both the integral Cech model and the characteristic-zero
polynomial-form model rather than identify their formulas without hypotheses.


12.4      Every finite tree and every parenthesization of its substitution
Let T be a finite rooted tree with labelled leaves. Each internal vertex has its child-coordinate
variables and a total order on its children. Attach a scale ϵe to every edge leading to a nonleaf child.
A leaf coordinate is the sum of its successive child coordinates, each multiplied by the product of
edge scales already traversed. For example a three-level branch gives

                                       za,b,c = Za + ϵa Ua,b + ϵa ηa,b Va,b,c .

All substitutions will be evaluated in the common ring
                                                   !
                                                                                       ϵe )−1 ].

                                       Cchildren(v) [[ϵe : e ∈ E(T )]][(                              (12.3)

Theorem 12.3 (Scale-marked all-tree coherence). The substitutions from the original coefficient
algebra CI associated to T are defined in (12.3). Any two evaluations with the same tree, edge scales

                                                          50
Chapter 12. Nested collisions with a common coefficient domain


and leaf-coordinate substitution agree. They are compatible with grafting and with the barycentric
differential-form maps.

Proof. For two leaves take their lowest common ancestor v, and let a, b be its children on their two
branches. Their coordinate difference factors as
                                                 !
                                   Y                                                    
                    zi − zj =               ϵe       uv,a − uv,b + terms in lower scales .
                                e above v

Whenever the target overlap inverts that difference, the corresponding source orders disagree at this
lowest common ancestor. Thus uv,a − uv,b is inverted on that source overlap. The remaining factor
has a unit constant term and therefore its unique inverse is the geometric expansion in lower scales.
A fixed rational source expression contains finitely many denominator factors. They give finite
negative exponent bounds along ancestor paths, with nonnegative power series in the remaining
scales. This proves that the substitution belongs to the simultaneous complete localization, not just
to an unspecified iterated Laurent field.
   Polynomial coordinate substitution is associative; on the displayed branch its identity is (Z +
ϵU ) + ϵηV = Z + ϵ(U + ηV ). The same identity on every path proves equality of all coordinate
substitutions on the tree. Uniqueness of the inverses in the common ring extends equality to every
localized source coefficient.
   For barycentric coordinates, the iterated image of a leaf-order vertex is the product of all vertex-
order barycentric coordinates which produce it, or zero if the leaf order is not compatible with that
ordered tree. The compatible choices, when they exist, are unique: every child subtree forms a
consecutive block, and its internal order is recovered by restriction. Products of their barycentric
coordinates are associative and commute, so every evaluation gives the same image. Differentiating
proves the same result for forms. The face-regularity proof of Theorem 12.2 applies at each vertex.
Grafting lengthens paths and multiplies their scales; the same arguments prove its compatibility.

   This theorem compares maps on the original coefficient algebra followed by expansion in a
common tree ring. It does not extend an arbitrary coefficientwise map to all elements of every
intermediate completion. For example
                                           ϵr η −r−1
                                        X

                                                     r≥0

has no lower bound in the η exponent and is not in ℸ[[ϵ, η]][(ϵη)−1 ]. Ignoring that distinction would
invalidate the assertion of a common target.


12.5     A three-leaf calculation and the missing units
Take blocks {1, 2} and {3}, with

                             z1 = Z1 + ϵu1 ,          z2 = Z1 + ϵu2 ,   z3 = Z2 .

The inner pole becomes ϵ−1 (u1 − u2 )−1 . On an outer overlap that inverts Z1 − Z2 , the cross poles
become
                             (zi − z3 )−1 =   (−ϵui )r (Z1 − Z2 )−r−1 .
                                            X

                                                     r≥0




                                                           51
Chapter 12. Nested collisions with a common coefficient domain


On a face fixing the outer order, a coefficient form has no arbitrary cross-block pole in the first
place; its substituted face therefore needs no such inversion. Pole regularity and the expansion rule
are part of one construction.
  There are four lexicographic order vertices, 123, 213, 312, 321. The two interleaved vertices 132, 231
map to zero. If v, w are the two chosen outer and inner barycentric coordinates, their images are

              t123 = vw,   t213 = v(1 − w),    t312 = (1 − v)w,   t321 = (1 − v)(1 − w).

For example dt123 = w dv + v dw. The degree-one mixed coefficient is explicit, and the sum of the
four degree-zero images is one.
   Unary identity substitutions may be retained as actual identity maps, without a new scale.
But setting a formal small scale equal to one in an arbitrary infinite series is not an allowed ring
map. A full unital bulk–boundary operad must state its units, colours, completion domains, and
comparison between these scale charts. The constructed tree system supplies coherent interior
coefficient substitutions with their actual domains. It does not by itself define the missing state
operations of the native mixed theorem.

Chapter 13

The diagonal of an order chart

13.1      Residue fields detect the whole complex
We first justify the unbounded categorical argument. Let R be a polynomial ring over a field. If
M ∈ D(R) satisfies M ⊗LR κ(p) = 0 for every prime, then M = 0.
   Here is a direct proof. The residue fields generate D(R) as a localizing subcategory. To see
this, prove R/p belongs to their localizing subcategory by induction on its Krull dimension. In
dimension zero it is its residue field. In positive dimension its fraction field is κ(p). The quotient of
that field by R/p is the filtered union of finitely generated torsion submodules. Each has a finite
prime filtration with prime quotients R/q for strictly larger primes. They belong by induction.
Closure under extensions and filtered colimits puts the quotient, and then R/p, in the subcategory.
In particular it contains R.
   For fixed M , the objects N such that N ⊗LR M = 0 form a localizing subcategory, because derived
tensor is exact and preserves colimits. The hypothesis puts every residue field in it, hence also R.
Therefore M = 0. This proof uses no boundedness of M .


13.2      A compact generator that is not an ordinary affine coordinate
          ring
Write X = XI , R = RI , and C = RΓ(X, OX ), retaining its full E∞ structure. All categories below
are enhanced derived categories. Derived quasi-coherent descent, tensor products and internal Homs
are the standard foundational framework; the specific generation and diagonal arguments are proved
here.
Theorem 13.1 (Monoidal reconstruction of order-chart coefficients). The structure sheaf is a
compact generator of Dqc (X), and derived global sections give a symmetric monoidal equivalence
                                                        ∼
                                       RΓ : Dqc (X) −
                                                    → ModC .

The tensors are ⊗LOX and ⊗LC , respectively.

Proof. The finite affine cover has affine intersections. Derived global sections are computed by its
bounded Cech totalization of affine sections. Each term commutes with direct sums, and a finite
totalization does too. Thus OX is compact.

                                                   53
Chapter 13. The diagonal of an order chart


   For generation suppose RΓ(F ) = 0. Derived base change to any residue field of R can be
performed on this finite Cech complex. On an affine intersection it is the ordinary associativity of
derived tensor, and the base scheme and its intersections are flat over R. Hence

                                   RΓ(F ) ⊗LR κ(p) ≃ RΓ(Xp , F |LXp ).

By Proposition 10.1 the fibre is a finite disjoint union of affine points, so the restriction to each point
is zero. On every original affine chart, the complex representing F consequently has zero derived
tensor with every residue field of R. The detection argument above makes that chart restriction
zero. Descent gives F = 0.
   For clarity, the compact-generator Morita proof is explicit. Put C = R End(OX ). The left adjoint
to R Hom(OX , −) sends C ⊗ V to OX ⊗ V and extends via free resolutions to N ⊗LC OX . The
adjunction unit is an equivalence on C and its equivalence locus is localizing, hence is all C-modules.
The counit is an equivalence on OX and its equivalence locus is localizing, hence all of Dqc (X) by
generation.
   Finally global sections have their natural lax symmetric monoidal map

                                RΓ(F ) ⊗LC RΓ(G) −→ RΓ(F ⊗LOX G).

For fixed G, both sides preserve colimits in F . The map is an equivalence at F = OX , where it
is the unit map. Generation makes it an equivalence for all F . Its associativity and symmetry
coherences are those of the lax structure. Thus the equivalence is strong symmetric monoidal.

   This does not assert that X = Spec H 0 (C) as an ordinary scheme. For the doubled line
H 1 (C) = K/R is indispensable. The nonconnective derived coefficient algebra is the input to the
equivalence. Replacing it by H(C) changes the monoidal category, as the idempotent test shows.
   The same proof applies to a finite product X ×S · · · ×S X. Its charts and intersections are
flat affine opens over S and its residue fibres are finite disjoint unions of points. A double Cech
computation gives
                                     RΓ(X ×S X, O) ≃ C ⊗LR C
with the coherent commutative structure. The two orders of finite totalization agree, and their
affine terms are precisely the tensor products of the two intersection localizations. This proves the
required Kunneth comparison here, rather than assuming it for an arbitrary nonproper scheme.


13.3      The relative diagonal is an affine open immersion
Let Y = X ×S X. On the chart Uσ ×S Uτ = S, the inverse image of the diagonal j : X → Y is
D(fστ ). Hence j is an affine open immersion. It need not be closed; the two origins are the basic
example.
  Put D = C ⊗LR C. Under the monoidal equivalence for Y , the algebra j∗ OX corresponds to the
D-algebra C with structure map multiplication D → C. Indeed its global sections are RΓ(X, OX ),
and pulling the two factors of a global function to the diagonal gives their product.
   On each chart of Y , j∗ OX is a flat localization algebra. Thus

                                       j∗ OX ⊗LOY j∗ OX ≃ j∗ OX .


                                                    54
Chapter 13. The diagonal of an order chart


Transporting through the monoidal equivalence gives the crucial identity

                                             C ⊗LD C ≃ C.                                         (13.1)

It is the diagonal multiplication D → C that is a homological epimorphism. The map R → C is not
being declared a homological epimorphism; in general C ⊗LR C ̸= C.


13.4      No additional relative Hochschild directions
Theorem 13.2 (Relative centre of order-chart coefficients). The canonical scalar action induces an
equivalence
                                                ∼
                                             C−
                                              → ZR (C).
It is an equivalence for the internal associative E2 structures, with the left side restricted from
its coherent commutative structure. In particular the statement retains the full derived coefficient
algebra, not only its cohomology.

Proof. The diagonal module C over D corresponds to j∗ OX . The derived direct image for an open
immersion is fully faithful, with j ∗ Rj∗ = 1. Therefore

                              RHomD (C, C) ≃ RHomY (j∗ OX , j∗ OX )
                                              ≃ RHomX (OX , OX ) = C.

Under this equivalence the scalar-action map is the identity. This proves the underlying equivalence
and preserves composition.
   For the higher assertion one can use the canonical central action of the commutative algebra C
on itself. It defines the standard E2 map to the associative centre. In a commutative dg model its
map is inclusion of nullary cochains; the multiplication, zero self-brackets and higher commutative
coherences give that same action. Alternatively use the monoidal fully faithful restriction argument
in the next theorem. The ordinary Deligne/centralizer framework supplies the E2 identification,
and its forgetful functor is conservative. Since the displayed underlying map is an equivalence, it is
an E2 equivalence.

   This is a relative statement over the separation-coordinate ring R. It does not erase absolute
tangent directions over ℸ. For example Zℸ (ℸ[z]) has the derivation class ∂z , while Zℸ[z] (ℸ[z]) = ℸ[z].
Nor does Theorem 13.2 prove that C is formal: Theorem 9.1 proves the contrary for the two-chart
coefficient.


13.5      Relative centralization for every coefficient algebra
Let A be an associative algebra in ModC . The coherent commutative action of C is central. Write

                                ER = A ⊗LR Aop ,         EC = A ⊗LC Aop .

There is a central scalar description EC ≃ ER ⊗LD C. The diagonal identity implies

                                          EC ⊗LER EC ≃ EC .

                                                    55
Chapter 13. The diagonal of an order chart


One may verify the latter by a two-sided bar resolution: move the central D-actions together,
identify the middle scalar bar with C ⊗LD C, and use (13.1). Thus restriction from EC -modules to
ER -modules is fully faithful. Indeed its extension–restriction counit is tensor with the displayed
multiplication equivalence.

Theorem 13.3 (Coefficient-relative centre comparison). For every associative C-algebra A, restric-
tion identifies
                                        ZR (A) ≃ ZC (A)
as internal associative E2 algebras. The right side is the centre relative to the full coefficient algebra
C.

Proof. The diagonal bimodule A belongs to the fully faithful image just described. Thus its derived
endomorphisms in the two bimodule categories agree. The image is closed under relative tensor over
A: tensors of C-linear bimodules are again C-linear, with the same tensor construction in the larger
category. Restriction is therefore a fully faithful monoidal functor preserving the unit bimodule
A. The E2 structure on endomorphisms of that unit is functorial for such monoidal functors. This
proves the higher comparison, not only its cup product.

   Transport through Theorem 13.1 gives the same result for algebra objects in Dqc (X). The word
“internal” is essential. Without a localization theorem, the internal quasi-coherent Hom is not
automatically the unrestricted sheaf of all local cochains. Infinite products of quasi-coherent sheaves
can differ from their naive sheafwise products. The next chapter supplies precise sufficient conditions
and a counterexample, so the centre comparison does not silently assert an invalid sheafification
theorem.


13.6      Absolute coordinate directions and finite resolutions
The preceding relative calculation has a useful absolute control. On S ×ℸ S the base diagonal is
cut out by the regular sequence ziL − ziR . Pull this Koszul resolution to X ×ℸ X. Inside its inverse
image, the order-chart diagonal is the affine open immersion already studied. Restricting to that
open and applying its fully faithful direct image retains the finite Koszul endomorphism calculation.
Thus the absolute diagonal calculation has the expected exterior coordinate directions in addition
to the derived functions.
   This paragraph describes the finite diagonal method, not an assertion of a chosen global E2
formality map. Identifying its underlying complex with polyvector fields uses the regular-sequence
Koszul calculation; identifying every higher operation with a strict Schouten model would require
a formality theorem. The distinction will become decisive for ramification, where even a relative
commutative refinement is obstructed by a special-fibre bracket.




                                                   56
Chapter 14

Centres, complete braces, and change
of coefficients

14.1      The diagonal cochain complex
For an ordinary algebra A projective over R, the unnormalized diagonal bar resolution has Pn =
A ⊗R A⊗n ⊗R A. Its augmented underlying complex contracts by inserting the unit. The terms are
projective as Ae -modules when the middle tensors are projective over R. Applying bimodule Hom
gives
                              n
                             CR (A, A) = HomR (A⊗n , A),      n ≥ 0.
The usual differential is

                     (bf )(a1 , . . . , an+1 ) = a1 f (a2 , . . . , an+1 )
                                                      n

For dg inputs one uses the internal differential and the suspension convention, retaining the product
totalization in Hom. Derived Hom need not be a direct sum over arities.
   For ordinary cochains the insertion product is
                            p−1
                                   (−1)i(q−1) f (1⊗i ⊗ g ⊗ 1⊗(p−i−1) ),
                            X
                  f ◦g =                                                             f ∈ C p, g ∈ C q .

It includes q = 0: a value is inserted into a slot. A nullary outer operation has no slots. An-
tisymmetrization in shifted degrees p − 1, q − 1 gives the Gerstenhaber bracket. Expanding the
associator of ◦, nested insertions cancel and disjoint insertions occur in opposite orders with the
Koszul interchange sign. This proves the graded pre-Lie identity and therefore the graded Lie
identity for the bracket. With m the multiplication, [m, m] = 0 is associativity, and a degreewise
sign change identifies [m, −] with b.
   Higher braces sum all disjoint ordered insertions. The same nested/disjoint partition proves their
identities in every arity. The cup product is induced by inserting two cochains into m. The ordinary
Deligne theorem identifies this full brace structure with a chain model of an E2 action [4, 5, 6]. The

                                                             57
Chapter 14. Centres, complete braces, and change of coefficients


insertion formulas and their algebraic identities are explicit here; the topological equivalence with
little disks is a foundational input. It is not the native holomorphic–topological mixed theorem.


14.2      Three different endomorphism problems
The augmentation dual RHomA (R, R) requires an actual augmentation. A probe algebra
RHomA (M, M ) requires a specified module and remembers only the subcategory detected by that
module. The diagonal centre ZR (A) resolves A as a bimodule. Its degree-zero part is the ordinary
centre, not an arbitrary endomorphism ring.
  For A = ℸ[x], the diagonal resolution is the two-term Koszul complex on xL − xR . Applying
Hom gives
                           HH• (A, A) = ℸ[x] ⊗ Λ(η),     |η| = 1,
where η is the derivation ∂x . Insertion gives [η, x] = 1. Consequently this E2 centre has no unital
E2 augmentation to ℸ: a Gerstenhaber algebra morphism on cohomology would send the displayed
identity to 0 = 1. The boundary augmentation x 7→ 0 does not propagate to an augmentation of
the full centre. Forgetting the bracket can permit an augmentation of the cup algebra, which is a
different statement.


14.3      Base change with the correct finite object
Theorem 14.1 (Perfect-diagonal base change). Let A be a dg R-algebra whose diagonal is perfect
over AeR . For a derived scalar extension R → S, the canonical comparison is an equivalence
                                                   ∼
                                     S ⊗LR ZR (A) −
                                                  → ZS (A ⊗LR S).

It preserves the internal associative E2 structure.

Proof. For a diagonal module P , consider the comparison from scalar extension of RHomAeR (P, A)
to derived Hom from PS into AS . It is an equivalence at P = AeR , where both sides are AS . The
equivalence locus is closed under finite sums, shifts, cones and retracts, since all the functors involved
are exact. A perfect diagonal is a retract of a finite cell object built from this generator. Hence the
comparison is an equivalence at P = A. Scalar extension preserves the bimodule tensor and its unit,
so the induced centre comparison respects the E2 operations. No exchange of an infinite product
with tensor is used.

   The finite object is the diagonal bimodule. It need not be finite over R, as the enveloping
examples show. Conversely an algebra can be a bounded finite free R-complex and have an infinite
diagonal resolution whose cochains accumulate in a single degree.
Proposition 14.2 (A bounded-coefficient alternative). Let A be an ordinary algebra finite projective
over R. Let D be a commutative dg R-algebra whose underlying complex is bounded and termwise
flat. Then the complete Hochschild cochain comparison
                                    •            •
                              D ⊗R CR (A, A) −→ CD (D ⊗R A, D ⊗R A)

is an isomorphism of the derived cochain models, with their cup and brace operations. In particular
it computes the relative centre after this derived coefficient extension.

                                                   58
Chapter 14. Centres, complete braces, and change of coefficients


Proof. In arity n, finite projectivity identifies the target Hom with D ⊗R HomR (A⊗n , A). Suppose
D is supported in degrees [a, b]. A total cochain of degree q receives contributions only when
q − n ∈ [a, b]. Thus only finitely many arities occur in that degree. The product totalization can
therefore be interchanged with the displayed bounded tensor, degree by degree. Bar resolutions
remain projective/cofibrant relative resolutions after base change; their scalar factors are finite
projective and the bounded flat coefficient tensor is derived. The differential, cups and every fixed
brace are the same finite insertions on either side, with the coefficient Koszul signs. This proves the
assertion including the operations.

   This proposition does not apply to an internally graded finite algebra merely because its underlying
complex is bounded. Input degrees can cancel arity degrees; the next calculation exhibits exactly
that cancellation. Nor does it allow an arbitrary unbounded coefficient complex in place of D.


14.4      An entirely explicit complete brace algebra
Let A = R ⊕ Rϵ, |ϵ| = 1, ϵ2 = 0, d = 0. In the suspended normalized model put e = sϵ, of degree
zero. Define cochains
                           Fn (e⊗n ) = s1,    En (e⊗n ) = e,  n ≥ 0.
Their shifted degrees are −1 and 0; their actual Hochschild degrees are 0 and 1. Nullary terms are
included. The internal reduced multiplications are zero. The exterior terms on Fn cancel because
b2 (s1, e) = e and b2 (e, s1) = −e, and those on En vanish. Thus the entire cochain differential is
zero.
Theorem 14.3 (Every brace in the odd square-zero model). For p, q1 , . . . , qr ≥ 0,
                                                               !

The result is zero for r > p. Any brace with an inner Fq is zero.

Proof. Every inner Eq outputs e. Its shifted degree and all input degrees are zero, so each allowed
choice of r ordered distinct outer slots has the same sign and value. There are pr choices. The

14.5      Locality of infinite operations
Let L<N denote cochains in arities below N . A brace with outer arity p, inner arities q1 , . . . , qr ,
and output arity n obeys                 X
                                 n=p+        qi − r,  p ≥ r.
                                                i

Thus p ≤ n + r and every qi ≤ n. The correct window map is

                                     L<N +r ⊗ (L<N )⊗r −→ L<N .

It is not in general an operation on L<N alone. Every output coefficient uses finitely many input
components. Each finite brace identity can be checked in one sufficiently large common input window,
so these maps define the complete action. The bound is sharp: Fn+r {E0 , . . . , E0 } = n+r
                                                                                         r Fn .
   In characteristic zero the ideal (uN ) is not a bracket ideal. Therefore the cup quotient by that
ideal is not a quotient of the complete centre structure. The operations are continuous with an
arity-dependent shift, not levelwise operations on every fixed quotient.


14.6      Localization which passes every finite test and still fails
Take R = ℸ[t]. The comparison on degree-zero cohomology in this model is

                                      R[[u]][t−1 ] −→ R[t−1 ][[u]].

It misses n≥0 t−n un . Any element on the left has a uniform lower bound on all powers of t
          P

appearing in its coefficients; this series does not. Every finite truncation is nevertheless in the image,
since
                                     N                    N
                                           t−n un = t−N         tN −n un .
                                     X                    X

                                     n=0                  n=0

The failure is not nonflatness of localization. It is failure to commute localization with the infinite
product which the centre actually uses.
   A local complete cochain sheaf can be constructed as the product of its operation sheaves, with
the arity-budget maps above. It need not be quasi-coherent. An internal quasi-coherent centre
uses the product in the quasi-coherent category instead. They agree under appropriate finiteness
conditions, not by definition. This qualification is part of Theorem 13.3, and prevents the order-chart
argument from creating a new localization error.


14.7      Morita transport preserves the centre only on the category
          transported
Let A, B be dg algebras over a field. Suppose that bimodules A PB and B QA are supplied with
compatible equivalences
                                P ⊗LB Q ≃ A,     Q ⊗LA P ≃ B.
These are hypotheses about actual bimodules and maps, not conclusions drawn from equality of
Hilbert series.


                                                    60
Chapter 14. Centres, complete braces, and change of coefficients


Theorem 14.4. The equivalence of bimodule categories

                                        F (X) = Q ⊗LA X ⊗LA P

is monoidal for relative tensor products and carries A to B. Consequently it induces an equivalence
of ordinary derived centres, including their E2 structures.

Proof. The inverse sends Y to P ⊗LB Y ⊗LB Q. In either composite the two adjacent inverse bimodules
cancel by the displayed equivalences. The monoidal comparison is the composite

                        F (X) ⊗LB F (Y ) ≃ Q ⊗LA X ⊗LA (P ⊗LB Q) ⊗LA Y ⊗LA P
                                         ≃ Q ⊗LA (X ⊗LA Y ) ⊗LA P.

The unit comparison is F (A) ≃ B. Associativity and unit coherence are the associativity of derived
balanced tensor and the compatible inverse-bimodule data. Endomorphisms of the monoidal unit are
therefore identified. The two coherent ways of composing these endomorphisms, whose interchange
gives the E2 structure, are carried to the corresponding compositions by the monoidal equivalence.
This identifies that structure, not only its cohomology vector space.

   For A = ℸ × ℸ with augmentation given by the first projection, the augmentation dual is
RHomA (ℸ, ℸ) = ℸ. But the full centre of A is ℸ × ℸ, whereas the centre of the augmentation-
generated category is ℸ. Thus an augmentation dual cannot be substituted into the theorem without
supplying the required category and inverse bimodules. The second direct factor is a nonzero sector
invisible to the selected augmentation.


14.8      Double centralization calculated with its topology
There is a one-variable calculation in which completion is forced rather than postulated. Let
A = ℸ[x], with |x| = 0 and augmentation x 7→ 0. Its two-term augmentation resolution gives a dg
algebra model
                               A! ≃ Λ(η),    |η| = 1,     dη = 0.
                                                                         x
Here is a direct multiplicative verification. On the resolution K = [A −
                                                                       → A] in degrees −1, 0, let
the degree-one endomorphism η be the identity coefficient map from K −1 to K 0 . It is closed and
squares to zero. The unit and this endomorphism represent the two Ext classes, so Λ(η) → EndA (K)
is a quasi-isomorphism of dg algebras.
   The reduced bar of Λ(η) has basis (sη)⊗n , n ≥ 0, all in degree zero, and zero differential. The
bar resolution computes the second augmentation dual using Hom from the direct sum of these free
generators. Hence                                 Y
                               RHomΛ(η) (ℸ, ℸ) ≃      ℸxn = ℸ[[x]]
                                                        n≥0

as an algebra concentrated in degree zero. Deconcatenation gives the power-series multiplication.
The double-centralizer map is the natural inclusion ℸ[x] → ℸ[[x]] under these choices of generator.
   No degreewise finiteness argument turns this product into a direct sum: every bar word has the
same total degree. Restricting to the polynomial dual would change the Hom object. This example
supports a derived augmentation-completion theorem under suitable general hypotheses, but is not
by itself such a theorem for every algebra or for an undeclared geometric completion.

15.1     Two diagonals, two different questions
Let R = ℸ[z1 , . . . , zn ], let S = Spec R, and let X be an order-chart scheme of Chapter 10. More
generally the proof below applies to a finite gluing of copies of S by identity coordinate maps
on principal affine opens, provided its residue fibres are finite disjoint unions of points. Put
C = RΓ(X, OX ) with the actual commutative derived-section structure.
   The relative theorem used the diagonal into X ×S X. The absolute diagonal has a larger target:

                            ∆R : X → X ×S X,            ∆ℸ : X → X ×ℸ X.

Its extra normal coordinates are the differences of the two copies of zi . They cannot be removed by
calling both targets a product of configuration spaces.
   The calculation of this chapter extends the two-coordinate normal-diagonal argument in the
cross-volume manuscript [30]. The extension is to arbitrary order-chart arity and includes a separate
calculation of the Gerstenhaber bracket. The multiplicative argument alone does not calculate that
bracket.


15.2     The matched-chart neighbourhood
Write Uσ for the displayed affine charts and set
                                                        [
                            W = X ×ℸ X,         W0 =        (Uσ ×ℸ Uσ ) ⊂ W.
                                                        σ

Every point of the diagonal lies in W0 . On it define global functions

                                   ui = zi,L − zi,R ,       1 ≤ i ≤ n.

Lemma 15.1 (A regular diagonal inside the correct open set). The absolute diagonal factors as
    i     ℓ
X− → W0 −→ W , with ℓ open and i the closed immersion cut out by the regular sequence u1 , . . . , un .
Its conormal bundle is free on these classes.

                                                   62
Chapter 15. Absolute centres and the return of the coordinate directions


Proof. On Uσ × Uσ , the change of variables from (zL , zR ) to (u, zR ) is an invertible polynomial
change. The ui are therefore a regular sequence and their quotient is the diagonal copy of R. On an
intersection of matched charts, the ring is a localization of R ⊗ℸ R in the appropriate left and right
overlap polynomials. Localizing preserves exactness of a regular-sequence Koszul complex. Setting
u = 0 gives exactly the original chart overlap. Thus the zero schemes on the matched charts glue to
X, not to the additional off-diagonal branch pairs in W . Regularity and the conormal basis are
local assertions verified by these chart computations.

   Let
                      P = OW0 ⊗ℸ Λ(e1 , . . . , en ),          |ei | = −1,        dP ei = ui .
The augmentation P → i∗ OX is a finite locally free resolution. Exactness follows either by
successively tensoring the one-variable injective multiplication complexes, or by induction on
the regular sequence just established. Denote exterior contraction with ei by ιi , a degree-one
endomorphism. Then
                              ι2i = 0, ιi ιj = −ιj ιi , [dP , ιi ] = 0.
                                                                              P
The square brackets here are graded commutators. Since dP = i ui ιi , these identities follow by
expanding the exterior contractions; no appeal to an eventual cohomology dimension is needed.


15.3      An explicit multiplicative quasi-isomorphism
Use compatible commutative models for derived global sections and put

                                           D = RΓ(W0 , OW0 ).

The first projection p1 : W0 → X gives an actual cdga map C → D. For example, on an intersection
it sends f (z) to f (zL ), and finite Thom–Whitney realization makes these maps compatible with
every overlap and with multiplication.
   The derived global endomorphism algebra of P is represented by
                                                                                  X
                    E = D ⊗ℸ Endℸ (Λ(e1 , . . . , en )),           dE = dD + [          ui ιi , −].
                                                                                    i

The matrix factor is finite. Consequently its tensor product commutes with the derived section
construction without any product-versus-limit issue. Define

                Φ : C ⊗ℸ Λ(η1 , . . . , ηn ) −→ E,           f 7→ p∗1 f,   ηi 7→ ιi ,       |ηi | = 1.   (15.1)

The differential on the source is just that of C.

Theorem 15.2 (Absolute diagonal reconstruction in every arity). There is an equivalence of unital
associative derived ℸ-algebras
                               Zℸ (C) ≃ C ⊗ℸ Λ(η1 , . . . , ηn ).
It is represented geometrically by (15.1), the open-immersion comparison, and the product compact-
generator equivalence. At the associative level it respects the displayed coordinate map R → C. The
theorem by itself is not an equivalence to an E∞ algebra equipped with its restricted E2 structure.




                                                        63
Chapter 15. Absolute centres and the return of the coordinate directions


Proof. The contractions and scalar coefficients satisfy the exterior and graded centrality relations,
and commute with the differential in precisely their graded degrees. Thus Φ is a dg algebra map.
  Postcompose an endomorphism of P with P → i∗ OX . This gives a quasi-isomorphism of sheaf
complexes
                            Hom(P, P ) −→ Hom(P, i∗ OX ),
because P is finite locally free and the augmentation cone is acyclic. In the target the ui vanish, so
its Koszul differential is zero. Its derived global sections are

                                           C ⊗ℸ Λ(e∨            ∨
                                                   1 , . . . , en ).

The composite with Φ is the identity on coefficients and takes a product of contractions to the
corresponding exterior functional, with the sign of the ordered contractions. In each exterior degree
this is an invertible signed basis change. Hence the composite is an isomorphism of complexes and
Φ is a quasi-isomorphism. Multiplicativity of Φ was proved before using this linear criterion.
   Open-immersion direct image is fully faithful on the derived category of sheaves of modules: its
restriction counit is the identity. In this quasi-compact, quasi-separated setting the relevant objects
are quasi-coherent. It therefore identifies the derived endomorphisms on W0 with those of R∆ℸ∗ OX
on W and preserves composition.
    Finally the product W has a finite cover by the affine products of order charts. The same residue-
field argument as Theorem 13.1, now over R ⊗ℸ R, proves that OW is a compact generator. A finite
double-Cech computation gives RΓ(W, O) = C ⊗ℸ C. Under the resulting monoidal equivalence,
R∆ℸ∗ OX is the diagonal module C. Its derived endomorphism algebra is Zℸ (C) by definition. This
proves the assertion in the stated category.

   The proof retains the entire nonformal coefficient algebra C. It does not replace it by a graded
vector space with the same dimensions. In particular every finite arity has a concrete diagonal
resolution, even though the geometric coefficient itself may have infinite-dimensional cohomology.


15.4      The bracket is not the commutator of the displayed cup model
Coordinate derivatives act on every overlap localization and commute with its restriction maps.
They also act on the Thom–Whitney model, leaving the simplex variables fixed. Thus there are
commuting degree-zero derivations

                                     ∂i : C → C,              [dC , ∂i ] = 0.

Viewed as unary Hochschild cochains they give degree-one classes. Their brackets with a nullary
cochain f and with one another are given by direct insertion:

                                     [∂i , f ] = ∂i f,        [∂i , ∂j ] = 0.

    To identify their normalization in Theorem 15.2, work on a matched affine chart. Antisym-
metrization sends the Koszul generator ei to the one-letter normalized bar generator [zi ] and sends
ei1 ∧ · · · ∧ eir to the signed sum of the r! corresponding bar words. Interior bar terms cancel in pairs
because the coordinates commute; the two boundary terms are the coordinate-difference Koszul
differential. Thus this is a comparison of resolutions. Evaluating ∂j on [zi ] gives δij . The derivation
class therefore corresponds to ηj , with no unchosen scalar.

                                                         64
Chapter 15. Absolute centres and the return of the coordinate directions


   This comparison is compatible with localizations. For a denominator g, a derivation necessarily
satisfies ∂i (g −1 ) = −g −2 ∂i g, so the local operation is uniquely the restriction of the global coordinate
derivation. The comparison on the finite overlap diagram consequently gives the same global
class, rather than a collection of classes whose differences might lie in higher Cech degree. The
function part is the relative-centre scalar action; forgetting relative linearity maps it to the absolute
centre. That map comes from the monoidal restriction of bimodule categories and preserves the E2
operations.
Theorem 15.3 (The full cohomological Gerstenhaber structure). Under the cup-algebra identifica-
tion
                           H ∗ Zℸ (C) ∼
                                      = H ∗ (C) ⊗ℸ Λ(η1 , . . . , ηn ),
the Gerstenhaber bracket is uniquely determined by
                               [c, c′ ] = 0,     [ηi , c] = ∂i c,      [ηi , ηj ] = 0
for homogeneous coefficient classes c, c′ ∈ H ∗ (C), and by the degree-−1 graded skew-symmetry and
Leibniz identities. In particular
                                             [ηi , zj ] = δij .

Proof. The scalar relative-centre map has source the commutative coefficient algebra, so its cohomo-
logical bracket is zero. The two other identities were calculated by unary and nullary insertion, and
the Koszul comparison fixed the identification of the unary generators. The Gerstenhaber bideriva-
tion identity on Hochschild cohomology then determines the bracket on all products. The associative
comparison proves that those products span the entire cohomology, so no further classes or bracket
values remain unspecified. The ordinary Gerstenhaber identity used here is the all-arity Hochschild
insertion theorem of Chapter 14; it is not a new identification of native chiral operations.

   For example, for ungraded coefficients f, g, the bracket of f ηi and gηj is
                                     [f ηi , gηj ] = f ∂i (g)ηj − g∂j (f )ηi .
The same formula with the specified total degrees determines its signs for higher coefficient coho-
mology. On a principal-part class the derivatives genuinely act: for the doubled line,
                                  [η, [z −m ]] = −m[z −m−1 ],          m ≥ 1.
It is incorrect to retain the exterior generators but declare all brackets with the collision sector to
vanish.
   The map (15.1) compares associative products. Its contraction generators commute with scalar
multiplication as endomorphisms. The Gerstenhaber bracket is a different, higher operation, not
their degree-zero commutator. Equipping the right side only with its commutative dg algebra
structure and zero bracket therefore does not turn (15.1) into an E2 comparison. The displayed
nonzero bracket is an immediate obstruction to that inference.


15.5      Coordinate scalars are no longer central for the higher struc-
          ture
Corollary 15.4 (A scalar-base obstruction). If n > 0, the absolute E2 centre does not admit an
R-linear E2 structure with its displayed coordinate scalar action and the bracket of Theorem 15.3.

                                                        65
Chapter 15. Absolute centres and the return of the coordinate directions


In particular its derived associative specialization along R → R/(z1 , . . . , zn ) is not automatically
specialization of an E2 algebra over R.

Proof. An E2 algebra in complexes of R-modules has R-multilinear homology operations. Its bracket
satisfies [a, r · 1] = r[a, 1] = 0. Here [ηi , zi ] = 1. These statements are incompatible. The underlying
associative model has a central map from R, so an associative derived tensor can still be formed;
the contradiction concerns the stronger operation structure, not that tensor product.

   This explains why restoring the absolute directions is more than tensoring a relative answer
with a few formal symbols. It changes which functions may be treated as scalar coefficients for the
higher operations. The relative base-change theorems remain valid in their relative categories. They
cannot be applied to the absolute bracket after changing its scalar category without checking this
obstruction.


15.6      The relative-to-absolute cone and the doubled line
At the associative level, the natural map from the relative centre to the absolute one is the inclusion
of exterior degree zero. Its cone is
                                             n
                                                        n
                                               C[−r]⊕( r ) .
                                            M

                                             r=1

It is computed from the actual map, not by subtracting two Hilbert series.
  For the doubled line put R = ℸ[z], K = R[z −1 ], and P = K/R. Then H 0 (C) = R, H 1 (C) = P .
The absolute centre has

                              H 0 = R,      H 1 = Rη ⊕ P,        H 2 = P η,

and no other degrees. Its entire bracket is fixed by [η, z] = 1, [η, z −m ] = −mz −m−1 in principal
parts, and zero brackets between coefficient classes.
   As an associative R-algebra the absolute centre is still not relatively formal. Its actual derived
fibre is
                                        (ℸ × ℸ) ⊗ Λ(η),
whereas the derived fibre of its cohomology algebra has degree-zero algebra ℸ[θ]/θ2 . Indeed the
summand P contributes the square-zero Tor generator and the exterior generator contributes only
positive degree. The degree-zero algebra of the actual fibre is ℸ×ℸ. An R-linear associative formality
zigzag would identify these two algebras, which have different nilradicals. This specialization test
concerns associative structure, consistently with Corollary 15.4.


15.7      A ramified pullback has a simple absolute geometry
For the earlier family z = xm , the pullback of the doubled z-line is two copies of the x-line glued on
D(xm ) = D(x). Its derived functions are exactly

                                Cz ⊗Lℸ[z] ℸ[x] ≃ RΓ(doubled x-line, O),




                                                   66
Chapter 15. Absolute centres and the return of the coordinate directions


by the finite affine Cech calculation and flatness of the monic root extension. The absolute centre of
this algebra is the doubled-line answer in the coordinate x, independently of m. The relative centre
over ℸ[z], in contrast, retains the ramification and the cochain bδ = mzβ of Chapter 16.
   Thus the ramification exponent is not an invariant of the unmarked absolute glued space. It
is an invariant of that space together with its map to the z-line. This is a direct example where
remembering more coordinates in the absolute centre and remembering the structural map in a
relative theory are complementary, not interchangeable, enrichments.

cohomology bracket

16.1     A moving multiple root
Let R = ℸ[t], with char ℸ = 0, and let

                                  Am = R[x]/(xm − t),       m ≥ 2.

It is free over R with basis 1, x, . . . , xm−1 . As a ℸ-algebra it is a polynomial algebra, but as an
R-algebra it is ramified. Absolute and relative Hochschild centres consequently answer different
questions.
  Write E = Am ⊗R Am . With variables X, Y ,
                                                              m−1
             E∼
                                                              X
              = ℸ[X, Y ]/(X m − Y m ),     u = X − Y,    v=         X m−1−i Y i ,   νv = 0.
                                                              i=0

The diagonal module is Am = E/(ν).

Theorem 16.1 (The complete periodic diagonal resolution). The complex
                                     ν     v    ν
                                  ··· −
                                      →E−
                                        →E−
                                          → E −→ Am −→ 0

with d2r+1 = ν and d2r+2 = v is a free diagonal resolution. Applying bimodule Hom gives Am in
every nonnegative cochain degree, with

                                   d2r = 0,      d2r+1 = mxm−1 .

Thus
              HH0R (Am ) = Am ,    HH2r+1
                                     R    (Am ) = 0,    HH2r+2
                                                          R    (Am ) = Am /(mxm−1 ).

Proof. In the polynomial domain ℸ[X, Y ], multiplication by the nonzero polynomial ν cancels. If
νf belongs to (νv), then f ∈ (v); hence annE (ν) = (v). Similarly annE (v) = (ν). These equalities
prove exactness at every positive resolution degree, and the augmentation has kernel (ν). On the
diagonal ν becomes zero and v becomes mxm−1 . The ring Am =      ∼ ℸ[x] is a domain and this latter
element is a nonzero divisor. Its kernel and cokernel give the displayed groups.

                                                 68
Chapter 16. Ramification hidden from the cohomology bracket


   A degree-two periodicity map, the identity on each free term after shifting by two, is a chain map.
Its Yoneda powers act by periodicity. If β denotes its degree-two class, the cup algebra is therefore

                             HH•R (Am ) = Am [β]/(mxm−1 β),                  |β| = 2.
All degrees are even, so its degree-−1 Gerstenhaber bracket is zero. This vanishing is a statement
about the family cohomology, not a proof that the family cochain algebra is commutative or
E2 -formal.


16.2      A bar representative with its normalization
On the fixed free basis, multiplication has the form
                                                µt = µ0 + tβ,
where                                  (
                            i    j       xi+j−m ,      i + j ≥ m,
                         β(x , x ) =                                     0 ≤ i, j < m.
                                         0,            i + j < m,
It is normalized, since inserting 1 cannot overflow. Associativity for the polynomial family µs implies
[µ0 , β] = 0 and [β, β] = 0 by comparing coefficients of s, s2 . Hence it is a Hochschild cocycle for
every t.
   Its class is exactly the periodicity class, not an unspecified nonzero multiple. Map the degree-one
generator of the periodic resolution to [x] in the normalized diagonal bar. Map the degree-two
generator to
                                              m−1
                                              X
                                                     [xi |x]xm−1−i .
                                              i=1

The bar differential telescopes to m−1  i    m−1−i , which is v applied to [x]. The potential last
                                  P
                                   i=0 x [x]x
term [xm ] = [t] vanishes in the normalized relative bar. Evaluating the carry cochain on this sum
leaves only i = m − 1, with value one. This fixes the generator and its product normalization.
   Define the normalized unary cochain
                                        δ(xi ) = ixi ,        0 ≤ i < m.
It is not an R-derivation of Am . Direct substitution in the Hochschild differential gives
                                                    bδ = mtβ.
When i + j < m the derivation defect is zero. When i + j ≥ m, the two input degrees sum to i + j,
while the output basis exponent is i + j − m. Their difference is precisely m, multiplied by tβ(xi , xj ).
This proves the identity on every basis input.


16.3      The special fibre and its full Gerstenhaber algebra
Set Bm = Am /tAm = ℸ[x]/xm . The same periodic resolution applies, but multiplication by mxm−1
now has kernel (x). The unary cochain δ becomes the Euler derivation. Its class generates the odd
groups as a ℸ[x]/xm−1 -module. Periodicity gives
                                        ℸ[x, δ, β]
                HH• (Bm ) =                                   ,   |x| = 0,    |δ| = 1,   |β| = 2,
                                (xm , xm−1 δ, xm−1 β, δ 2 )

                                                         69
Chapter 16. Ramification hidden from the cohomology bracket


where the algebra is graded commutative. The odd square is zero in characteristic zero. The
periodicity chain map identifies multiplication by β on each positive group; the degree-zero action
and the annihilators are read from the same resolution.
   The complete Gerstenhaber bracket is determined by

                    [δ, x] = x,     [δ, β] = −mβ,       [β, x] = [β, β] = [δ, δ] = 0,

and the biderivation identities. These formulas have direct cochain checks. The first is evaluation of
the derivation on x. For the second, on an overflowing pair δβ has output exponent i + j − m while
the two inserted derivations contribute i + j, giving −mβ; both sides vanish on nonoverflowing
pairs. For [β, x], the two insertions cancel because the carry function is symmetric. For [β, β], both
double-overflow composites are nonzero exactly when i + j + k ≥ 2m, and then have the same
value xi+j+k−2m . The self-bracket of a unary derivation vanishes directly. The formulas respect the
displayed relations, so give the bracket on the whole cohomology algebra.
   In particular this bracket is not zero even for m = 2. It is not recovered from the zero Gerstenhaber
bracket of the family cohomology by underived specialization.


16.4     Why derived base change is valid in this example
The base-change assertion has to be proved before it can be used as a formality obstruction. The
ordinary normalized cochains of Am are finite free R-modules in each nonnegative cochain degree,
since the reduced basis x, . . . , xm−1 is finite free. Multiplication by t is termwise injective. The
quotient cochains are exactly the normalized cochains of Bm , with the same insertion formulas.
   Resolve ℸ = R/(t) by the commutative dg algebra K = R[η], |η| = −1, dη = t, η 2 = 0. It is
a bounded free R-complex. Proposition 14.2 identifies K ⊗R CR (Am ) with the derived coefficient
extension of the cochain centre, including the brace operations. The map killing t, η to Cℸ (Bm ) is
a quasi-isomorphism: on underlying complexes it is the map from the cone of termwise injective
multiplication by t to the quotient. It is a morphism of the cochain operation structures. Thus

                                      ℸ ⊗LR ZR (Am ) ≃ Zℸ (Bm )

as E2 algebras. No assertion that every infinite diagonal satisfies arbitrary base change has been
used.

Theorem 16.2 (No relative commutative refinement). The internal E2 algebra ZR (Am ) is not
equivalent, over R, to the restriction of an E∞ algebra. In particular it is not relatively E2 -formal
to its zero-differential cohomology algebra with its zero Gerstenhaber bracket, under a formality
convention which factors that zero-bracket model through commutative algebras.

Proof. Derived scalar extension of an E∞ algebra is still E∞ . Its restricted E2 structure has
zero Gerstenhaber bracket on cohomology: the degree-one little-disks class becomes zero in the
commutative operad. An R-linear E2 equivalence to such a refinement would therefore, after the
proved base change, force the Gerstenhaber bracket of Zℸ (Bm ) to vanish. But [δ, x] = x ̸= 0 in Bm .
This contradiction proves the stronger first assertion, and hence the stated formality obstruction.

  The proof does not rely on an unproved identification of a chiral deformation object with ordinary
Hochschild cochains. It is an obstruction in a completely specified relative associative centre. Nor

                                                  70
Chapter 16. Ramification hidden from the cohomology bracket


does it claim that every notion of a chosen higher-operation model on the cohomology module is
impossible: such a model may retain nontrivial higher operations and is not the formal zero-bracket
replacement excluded here.


16.5      The connecting map identifies the hidden class
The short exact sequence of cochains under multiplication by t gives a connecting map
                                        HH1 (Bm ) −→ HH2R (Am )[t].
By the explicit identity for bδ, it sends xa δ to mxa β for 0 ≤ a ≤ m − 2. These classes form bases
on both sides, so it is an isomorphism. This is the exact mechanism creating the odd special-fibre
classes from even torsion in the family centre.
  The cohomology algebra has the right torsion groups to produce the special-fibre dimensions
and cup groups by derived tensor. What it lacks is the higher operation whose boundary is the
parameter times the carry cocycle. The bracket test therefore distinguishes more than dimensions,
and more than the derived additive special fibre.


16.6      The entire two-root cochain algebra
For m = 2 the normalized bar has one reduced letter. Let Fn (x⊗n ) = 1 and En (x⊗n ) = x, in cochain
degree n. The complete normalized cochain differential is
                               (                                      (
                                 2En+1      n odd,                        2tFn+1      n odd,
                       bFn =                                  bEn =
                                 0          n even,                       0           n even.

All internal bar terms vanish after normalization because x2 = t is a scalar. The two exterior terms
give the displayed formulas. There is one cochain arity in each degree, so no hidden same-degree
infinite product occurs here.
   The cup products are
                       Fp Fq = Fp+q ,     Fp Eq = Ep+q = Eq Fp ,            Ep Eq = tFp+q .
Put a = F1 . As a strict dg associative algebra this is
                     R[x] ⊗ T (a)
                                  ,       |x| = 0,      |a| = 1,      dx = 0,      da = 2xa2 .
                      (x2 − t)
The odd generator is associative, not exterior: a2 ̸= 0. The differential is a derivation since
d(a2 ) = da a − a da = 0. Here δ = xa and β = a2 , so dδ = 2tβ.
  Every higher brace is also explicit. An inner Fq gives zero by normalization. For inner Eq1 , . . . , Eqr
and outer Fp or Ep , the output is the same type in arity p + qj − r, multiplied by
                                                                P

                                                   Pr                P            

This is the signed insertion formula: lj is the chosen outer slot, and the parenthesis counts the

Thus this is an all-arity cochain model, not only a periodic complex together with a guessed bracket.

                                                         71
Chapter 16. Ramification hidden from the cohomology bracket


16.7     A strict Gerstenhaber shortcut is impossible
The two-root model has the strict cup relation x2 = t and the chain bracket [δ, x] = x, but δ is not
closed. A strict R-linear Gerstenhaber biderivation with these same data would give

                                  [δ, x2 ] = 2x2 = 2t,    [δ, t] = 0,

a contradiction. The actual chain bracket is not a strict derivation of the cup product on arbitrary
nonclosed cochains. Its defect is

                                δ(ab) − δ(a)b − aδ(b) = −(bδ)(a, b).

At a = b = x this equals −2t. The carry cochain supplies precisely that homotopy. On cohomology
the defect vanishes, which is why the Gerstenhaber biderivation law holds there.
   Replacing the family by a strict dg Gerstenhaber algebra with the same naive generators would
therefore introduce an error even before a chiral realization is attempted. The correct repair retains
the full brace operations and the nonclosed unary cochain. This finite example explains why
cohomological bracket calculations are insufficient to construct a mixed chain-level theory.




                                                  72
Chapter 17

The exact commutative-refinement
locus of a finite family

17.1     From a counterexample to a criterion
The ramified root computation showed that an even cohomology algebra can conceal a noncom-
mutative higher structure. It is natural to ask whether the phenomenon is particular to xm − t.
It is not. For a finite locally free commutative family over a regular characteristic-zero base, the
existence of a commutative refinement of its Hochschild E2 structure is exactly the etale condition.
   The adjective “relative” remains essential. Throughout this chapter the centre is ZR (A), and an
E∞ refinement is over the same ring R and restricts to the given E2 structure. Merely giving the
cohomology module a commutative product is not a refinement in this sense.


17.2     The particular base changes needed in the proof
Let A be an ordinary unital algebra whose underlying R-module is finite projective. Its unnormalized
Hochschild cochains are
                                 q
                               CR  (A) = HomR (A⊗R q , A),   q ≥ 0.
We use the unnormalized model here to avoid assuming that a chosen scalar splitting of the unit
has been supplied. Every displayed module is finite projective. There is exactly one arity in each
cochain degree; the same-degree infinite product from the odd square-zero example does not occur.

Lemma 17.1 (Flat and finite-Koszul base change). Hochschild cochains of such an A commute with
flat scalar extension. They also commute, as derived E2 algebras, with quotient by a finite regular
sequence in R. The assertion remains valid after a flat localization followed by such a quotient.

Proof. For a flat extension R → S, finite projectivity identifies every cochain module after tensoring
with S with the corresponding Hom over S. Products, insertions and the Hochschild differential
use only the original multiplication and permutations of inputs, so these identifications preserve all
brace formulas. Flat tensor is the derived tensor, giving the assertion.
   For a regular sequence r1 , . . . , rd , use the bounded free commutative dg resolution

                           K = R ⊗ Λ(ϵ1 , . . . , ϵd ),   |ϵi | = −1,   dϵi = ri .

                                                     73
Chapter 17. The exact commutative-refinement locus of a finite family


The underlying derived scalar extension of the cochain complex is K ⊗R CR     ∗ (A). The operation

formulas extend by the graded tensor rule. Every total degree has only the d + 1 possible coefficient
degrees of K, so the extension commutes with all cochain totalizations involved. Finite projectivity
of A identifies the terms with the cochains over the dg base K of K ⊗R A.
   The augmentation to the ordinary quotient has the correct cochain comparison. To check it on
complexes, tensor the bounded acyclic cone of K → R/(r1 , . . . , rd ) with the cochain terms. Those
terms are flat. In each fixed cochain degree this is an acyclic bounded complex. The resulting
double complex is bounded in the coefficient direction and bounded below in the cochain direction,
so finite diagonals, or its elementary finite filtration in each total degree, prove acyclicity of the
total comparison cone. Insertion formulas are unchanged by the quotient; the comparison preserves
the complete operation structure.
   Equivalently one may perform the one-parameter cone argument successively. Every intermediate
quotient of the finite projective cochain modules is projective over its quotient base, and the next
regular element acts injectively there. This proves the required quasi-isomorphism without claiming
that every unbounded diagonal commutes with arbitrary base change. The ordinary brace-to-E2
identification is the fixed foundational comparison used throughout the volume.

   For a regular noetherian ring and a prime p, first localize at p. A regular system of parameters
in Rp is a finite regular sequence and resolves its residue field. The lemma therefore applies to every
residue-field fibre of a finite projective ordinary algebra. This is the precise use of regularity in the
theorem below.


17.3      A finite nonreduced algebra always has a detectable derivation
Lemma 17.2 (Socle derivations). Let B be a finite-dimensional commutative algebra over a
characteristic-zero field k. If B is not a product of finite separable field extensions, there are a
k-linear derivation D : B → B and b ∈ B with D(b) ̸= 0. Consequently the Gerstenhaber bracket of
HH∗ (B) is nonzero.

Proof. Decompose the Artinian algebra into its local factors. It suffices to consider a factor with
nonzero maximal ideal m. Its residue field L is finite separable over k. There is a coefficient-field
embedding L ,→ B. Here is an elementary construction. Choose a primitive element of L/k with
separable minimal polynomial g. Lift it to v ∈ B. The element g ′ (v) is a unit because its residue is
nonzero. Newton correction
                                       v 7−→ v − g(v)/g ′ (v)
squares the ideal containing its error. Since m is nilpotent, finitely many corrections produce
g(v) = 0. The induced map from the field k[T ]/g is injective and splits the residue map. This proves
the claimed coefficient-field inclusion.
  Nakayama’s argument gives m/m2 ̸= 0: if m = m2 , nilpotence would force m = 0. The socle
Soc(B) = {a : ma = 0} has a nonzero element in m, for example in the last nonzero power of m.
Choose a nonzero L-linear map
                                  ϕ : m/m2 → Soc(B) ∩ m.
Using B = L ⊕ m as an L-vector space, put D(λ + u) = ϕ(u). For u, v ∈ m the class of uv in m/m2
is zero, and multiplication of either u or v by the image of ϕ is zero. Therefore

            D((λ + u)(µ + v)) = λϕ(v) + µϕ(u) = (λ + u)D(µ + v) + (µ + v)D(λ + u).

                                                   74
Chapter 17. The exact commutative-refinement locus of a finite family


This proves the derivation identity. Extend the derivation by zero on the other local factors. It is
nonzero by construction.
   For a commutative algebra, inner derivations vanish and hence HH1 (B) = Derk (B). The insertion
bracket of a derivation and a nullary element is evaluation: [D, b] = D(b). Since HH0 (B) = B, this
nonzero value remains a nonzero Hochschild class. This proves the last assertion.

   The lemma is stronger than a dimension estimate for HH1 . It constructs an operation and an
input on which that operation is nonzero. It is exactly the data that a commutative refinement
cannot reproduce.


17.4     The equivalence with etaleness
Theorem 17.3 (Commutative refinement detects finite etaleness). Let R be a regular noetherian
commutative Q-algebra of finite Krull dimension, and let A be a finite locally free commutative
R-algebra. The following are equivalent:
 (i) A is finite etale over R;
 (ii) the canonical coefficient map A → ZR (A) is an E2 equivalence, with A carrying its commutative
      structure;
(iii) the given relative E2 structure on ZR (A) admits an E∞ refinement over R.

Proof. For (i)⇒(ii), the multiplication A ⊗R A → A is a separable quotient: the diagonal module is
a direct summand of the finite free rank-one module over A ⊗R A. Consequently it is projective.
Derived Hom of that diagonal has no positive cohomology and ordinary endomorphism algebra
A. The canonical central coefficient action is the identity on degree-zero cohomology and is an E2
map. Its underlying quasi-isomorphism is therefore an E2 equivalence. Statement (ii) implies (iii)
by transporting the commutative refinement of A.
   Suppose (iii). For each prime p, Lemma 17.1 identifies derived scalar extension of the centre
with the centre of B = A ⊗R κ(p) over that field. An E∞ algebra remains E∞ under derived
scalar extension. Its restricted E2 structure has zero Gerstenhaber bracket on cohomology: the
positive homology class of the two-input little-disks space maps to zero in the commutative operad.
Lemma 17.2 now forces every fibre B to be a product of separable fields.
   For completeness we verify the algebraic passage from this fibre statement to (i). The finite
module of relative differentials ΩA/R has zero tensor product with every residue field of R, because
differentials commute with ordinary scalar extension and the fibre algebras are separable. Nakayama’s
lemma applied locally makes ΩA/R = 0. If T = A ⊗R A and J is its multiplication kernel, then
J/J 2 ∼= ΩA/R = 0, so J = J 2 . The ring T is noetherian and J is finitely generated. A finitely
generated idempotent ideal is generated by an idempotent: choose generators vi and a matrix with
entries in J expressing vi = j aij vj . The determinant of 1 − (aij ) annihilates J and has the form
                              P

1 − e with e ∈ J. Therefore (1 − e)e = 0, e2 = e, and J = T e. Thus the quotient A = T /J is the
summand T (1 − e), a separable diagonal. Finite local freeness over R together with this separable
diagonal is the finite etale condition. This proves (i).

   The result is not the claim that all ordinary Hochschild centres are formal. It identifies one
precisely defined refinement locus for finite commutative families. The regular-base hypothesis was
used to compare with residue-field centres; it has not been silently removed.

                                                  75
Chapter 17. The exact commutative-refinement locus of a finite family


17.5     A monic polynomial and its discriminant
Let
                                                            m−1
                                                            X
               R = ℸ[a0 , . . . , am−1 ],   f (X) = X m +         aj X j ,   A = R[X]/f,
                                                            j=0

where ℸ has characteristic zero. Localizations of this base are still regular. The differentials have
presentation
                                   ΩA/R = A dX/(f ′ (X)dX).
Multiplication by f ′ (X) is an endomorphism of the rank-m free R-module A. Let ∆f be its
determinant, up to the conventional sign used for the polynomial discriminant. This sign has no
effect on its invertibility.

Corollary 17.4 (The discriminant is the exact refinement divisor). On the universal monic family,
the open locus on which the relative Hochschild E2 centre admits a commutative refinement is exactly
D(∆f ).

Proof. The element f ′ is a unit in A precisely when its multiplication endomorphism is invertible.
One direction is immediate; in the other, apply the inverse endomorphism to 1 to obtain an element
g with f ′ g = 1. A square matrix over a commutative ring is invertible precisely when its determinant
is a unit, by the adjugate formula. Thus f ′ is invertible exactly on D(∆f ). This is the finite etale
locus, and Theorem 17.3 gives the assertion.

   For f = X m − t the determinant is a nonzero scalar multiple of tm−1 . The centre is commutatively
refinable after inverting t and is not so refinable over the whole t-line. Its family cohomological
bracket nevertheless vanishes, as computed earlier. The discriminant theorem replaces a misleading
cohomology-level criterion by an exact geometric condition.


17.6     The periodic resolution for a general moving polynomial
A second useful family is A = ℸ[t, X]/(f (X) − t) for a fixed monic polynomial f ∈ ℸ[X] of degree
m ≥ 1. Its underlying absolute algebra is ℸ[X], while its structural map sends t to f (X). In ℸ[X, Y ]
put
                                                      f (X) − f (Y )
                                u = X − Y,       v=                  .
                                                          X −Y
The diagonal ring is T = ℸ[X, Y ]/uv. In the polynomial domain, if uq is divisible by uv, cancellation
of the nonzero u proves that q is divisible by v. Thus AnnT (u) = (v), and the reverse cancellation
proves AnnT (v) = (u). The alternating free resolution is exact in every degree.
   Applying diagonal Hom gives alternate zero and f ′ (X) differentials. When m ≥ 1 in characteristic
zero, f ′ is a nonzero divisor in the domain ℸ[X]. Consequently

                  HH0R (A) = A,       HH2q+1
                                        R    (A) = 0,   HH2q          ′
                                                          R (A) = A/(f )       (q ≥ 1).

The periodicity chain map is the identity after shifting by two; its Yoneda powers prove the cup
presentation A[β]/(f ′ β). This proof handles a general moving polynomial without extrapolating
the monomial Euler formula to it. The latter formula bδ = mtβ was special to f = X m .



                                                   76
Chapter 17. The exact commutative-refinement locus of a finite family


     Pulling back the doubled t-line now gives two X-lines glued along D(f (X)). Its unmarked
absolute geometry depends on the distinct zeros of f . Relative centre torsion instead lies on
f ′ (X) = 0. These are different loci. For example f = X 2 − 1 doubles the points X = 1, −1 but has
its ramification point at X = 0, over t = −1, away from the doubled fibre. This elementary example
prevents the collision divisor from being identified with the ramification divisor in a general family.


17.7     A two-root special fibre calculated in every degree
For B = ℸ[x]/x2 , the normalized cochain algebra of Chapter 16 specializes to

                                       B ⊗ T (a),          da = 2xa2 .

The even cohomology classes are 1, x in degree zero and β q = a2q modulo x in every positive even
degree. The odd classes are δβ q = xa2q+1 . The complete cup algebra is

                            ℸ[x, δ, β]/(x2 , xδ, xβ, δ 2 ),   |δ| = 1,   |β| = 2.

Its bracket is [δ, x] = x, [δ, β] = −2β, extended by the Gerstenhaber rules. This both illustrates
the general socle-derivation proof and shows why the scalar residue bracket detects more than the
parity of the family cohomology.

A centre with order and ramification
