18.1      The coefficient and the root share a parameter
Take R = ℸ[z] and let X be the line with doubled origin. Put C = RΓ(X, OX ) with its coherent
commutative structure. On X consider the algebra
                                      Am = OX [x]/(xm − z).
Its ramification parameter is the collision parameter itself. It is not a tensor product with a root
family whose parameter has been held independent of the collision. Its derived global algebra is
                            Aord    L
                             m = C ⊗R Am ,         Am = R[x]/(xm − z).
Because Am is finite free over R, this is also the derived global sections of the displayed algebra
sheaf, with its algebra structure.
Theorem 18.1 (The exact coupled centre). There is a canonical equivalence of internal associative
E2 algebras
                                    ZR (Aord      L
                                         m ) ≃ C ⊗R ZR (Am ).
The right side uses the actual root-family cochain centre, not its cohomology algebra. It retains the
coefficient algebra C, not H(C).

Proof. The coefficient-relative comparison, Theorem 13.3, gives
                                       ZR (Aord        ord
                                            m ) ≃ ZC (Am ).

For this two-chart coefficient a bounded flat commutative model is the endpoint polynomial-form
model of Chapter 9. Its degree-zero module is R2 ⊕ s(1 − s)K[s], and its degree-one module is
K[s]ds, both flat over R. The ordinary algebra Am is finite projective over R. Proposition 14.2
therefore identifies the last centre with C ⊗R ZR (Am ), preserving every cup and brace operation.
This proves the assertion and its derived interpretation.

   The proof has two genuinely different steps. The order-chart diagonal removes spurious relative
directions by a fully faithful monoidal localization. The finite-basis root calculation permits the
complete cochain base change. Neither step is a comparison of Hilbert series, and neither requires a
false perfect-diagonal hypothesis for the ramified root algebra.

                                                 78
Chapter 18. A centre with order and ramification coupled


18.2     Generic and special fibres
Away from z = 0, the two charts agree, so C[z −1 ] ≃ R[z −1 ]. The root algebra there is etale:
mxm−1 is a unit because xm = z is a unit. The periodic diagonal cochain complex has no positive
cohomology. Thus the generic localized centre is the root algebra in degree zero.
   At z = 0, the derived coefficient fibre is ℸ × ℸ. Hence

                            Aord L
                             m ⊗R ℸ ≃ Bm × Bm ,             Bm = ℸ[x]/xm .

The two branches are idempotent components, each with its own nilpotent root variable. They are
not two nilpotent directions on one local algebra.
   The centre base change in this particular family is also exact. Theorem 18.1, associativity of
derived tensor, and the root base-change proof give

                               ℸ ⊗LR ZR (Aord
                                          m ) ≃ Zℸ (Bm ) × Zℸ (Bm ).

This is the centre of the actual special-fibre algebra. One can verify the last assertion directly: the
product idempotents split the diagonal resolution, the off-diagonal bimodule components vanish, and
derived endomorphisms of the diagonal are the product of the two centres. The scalar comparison
preserves the same summands and cochain operations.
   On either branch, the special-fibre bracket has [δ, x] = x and [δ, β] = −mβ. Thus the two
kinds of information are simultaneously visible: branch idempotents from the order geometry and
ramification brackets from the root relation.


18.3     Two independent invalid simplifications
Replacing C by H(C) changes the special-fibre algebra to

                                           ℸ[θ, x]/(θ2 , xm ),

in degree zero, rather than Bm × Bm . The former is local, with nilpotent maximal ideal, while the
latter has two branch idempotents. This is an algebra-level failure before taking the centre.
   Replacing only the root centre by its zero-differential, zero-bracket cohomology algebra makes the
right side of Theorem 18.1 a restriction of a commutative derived algebra. Its derived special fibre
therefore has zero Gerstenhaber bracket. The true special-fibre centre has the displayed nonzero
bracket on both branches. This is a higher-operation failure even when the coefficient algebra is
retained.
   These two tests are independent. The first does not use the Gerstenhaber bracket. The second
does not identify branch count with a nilpotent dimension. A reconstruction which passes just one
of them has not retained the complete coupled object.


18.4     The all-arity internal action
The centre in Theorem 18.1 has the ordinary internal Deligne action on the boundary algebra Aord
                                                                                             m .
In a cochain model its mixed operations evaluate cochains on ordered boundary inputs, compose
their disjoint insertions, and multiply adjacent boundary entries. The cup and brace identities

                                                   79
Chapter 18. A centre with order and ramification coupled


establish their coherences; the usual brace-pair/Swiss-cheese comparison is the ordinary foundational
theorem from [5, 6, 11]. Coefficient extension and the fully faithful monoidal localization used above
preserve that action.
   For m = 2 there is no unspecified operation in its algebraic model. Use the basis Fn , En and the
signed slot sum in Chapter 16, tensor with the endpoint coefficient model, and use the coefficient
Koszul signs. The differential on the root factor is dF2r+1 = 2E2r+2 , dE2r+1 = 2zF2r+2 . The
coefficient differential is d(a, b) = b − a in the Cech presentation, or polynomial de Rham differential
in the commutative presentation. The total differential is their tensor sum. It squares to zero
because each factor does, and the two odd terms anticommute by the tensor convention.
  The evaluation of a fixed-arity operation uses only finitely many slots. With a bounded coefficient
model, only finitely many arities contribute in any total degree for this ordinary root algebra.
Consequently the formulas define actual operations, not formal series without a target. They
commute with chart restrictions and with the derived specialization maps already proved.
   This is a complete internal associative action parametrized by the specified collision geometry. It
is not an identification of that action with the native holomorphic–topological operation system
on Xcurve × R≥0 . The labels of an order-chart coefficient base are not, by definition, the spacetime
colours of that mixed operad. The final chapter records the additional comparison needed for that
identification.


18.5     A nonconstant Lie-bundle example
There is another useful form of coupling which does not rely on a multiple root. On the two-origin
coefficient scheme glue the Heisenberg Lie algebra with basis x, y, z0 by

                    x 7−→ x,      y 7−→ y + z −1 x,     z0 7−→ z0 ,     [x, y] = z0 .

The map preserves the bracket. Assign weights 1, 1, 2. The weight-one bundle V is the extension
of O by O with Cech class z −1 . Changing its regular splittings cannot remove that principal part.
A global section has coefficients satisfying b− = b+ = b and a+ − a− = z −1 b, so b = zc. At either
origin every global section lies in the x direction; it does not trivialize the second direction there.
  PBW and the exterior resolution are equivariant under the gluing and give a sheaf-level bar–
Chevalley comparison for the enveloping algebra. The underlying homology sheaves are

                    H0 = O,     H1 = V,    H2 = V ⊗ Oz0 ,     H3 = O(x ∧ y ∧ z0 ).

The canceled map is Λ2 V → Oz0 , an isomorphism. As in the earlier Heisenberg example, that
cancellation is not a strict coalgebra splitting. Applying the monoidal order-chart equivalence
computes the global associative bar with both the extension and bracket retained. This is a
nonconstant geometric algebra, not an unchanged ordinary algebra accompanied by a passive
character.




                                                  80
Chapter 19

Relative Weyl vacua and the algebra
of their endomorphisms

A relation with a scalar term has no augmentation obtained by killing its generators. There is
nevertheless a distinguished module, and its endomorphisms can be calculated without inventing
such an augmentation. The calculation is also a useful test of the difference between a faithful
representation and a generator of a derived category.


19.1      An operator algebra over an arbitrary ring
Let R be a commutative ring and let M = (Mij ) be an a × b matrix over R. On R[q1 , . . . , qb ] define
the commuting derivations
                                                          b
                                                          X
                                                   δi =         Mij ∂qj .
                                                          j=1

Let WM be the iterated Ore extension with generators qj , pi and relations

                                 [qj , ql ] = [pi , ph ] = 0,         [pi , qj ] = Mij .

Commutativity of the derivations makes the iterated extension independent of the order in which
the pi are adjoined, with the indicated labelled identification. Its underlying R-module has basis
q α pβ . To verify the normal form, move a pi past a coefficient by pi f = f pi + δi (f ). Two such moves
across a product agree by Leibniz, and two moves involving different pi agree because [δi , δh ] = 0.
They decrease the number of out-of-order pairs, or decrease the number of operator letters. This
proves existence and uniqueness by terminating reduction. No entry of M is divided by.
   The polynomial vacuum is

                       VM = R[q1 , . . . , qb ],          qj · f = qj f,        pi · f = δi (f ).
                            P
Equivalently VM = WM / i WM pi . The quotient is a left module; it is not an algebra quotient
when the commutators are nonzero.

Chapter 19. Relative Weyl vacua and the algebra of their endomorphisms


19.2      A finite free resolution
Let E be free with basis e1 , . . . , ea , placed in degree −1, and put

                                    K −r = WM ⊗R Λr E,

Here Rw means right multiplication by w on the coefficient of a free left module, and ιi is contraction
with ei . Thus
                                                         r

                     dK (w ⊗ ei1 ∧ · · · ∧ eir ) =            (−1)j−1 wpij ⊗ ei1 ∧ · · · ebij · · · ∧ eir .

The right multiplications commute and contractions anticommute, so d2K = 0, including in charac-
teristic two with the exterior squares imposed separately.

Theorem 19.1. The augmentation K → VM is a finite free left WM -module resolution over every
commutative ring R.

Proof. In the normal basis q α pβ , right multiplication by pi increases only βi . Consequently the
underlying complex of R[q]-modules is the tensor product of the one-variable augmented complexes
                                                     i    p
                                        0 −→ R[pi ] −→ R[pi ] −→ R −→ 0.

Each is split exact as a complex of R-modules: remove one pi from a nonconstant monomial to
define the contraction onto constants. Tensoring these explicit contractions gives exactness of the
augmented multivariable complex. The normal form identifies its quotient with VM , and each term
is a finite free left WM -module.


19.3      The strict differential graded model
Define
                                                                                                  X
      GM = R[q1 , . . . , qb ] ⊗R Λ(θ1 , . . . , θa ),     |qj | = 0,     |θi | = 1,      dqj =       Mij θi ,   dθi = 0.
                                                                                                  i

The products of the θi are exterior products, not unrestricted symmetric products in characteristic
two.

Theorem 19.2. There is a natural quasi-isomorphism of differential graded associative algebras

                                                 GM −→ EndWM (K).

In particular GM is a strict algebra model for RHomWM (VM , VM ), with its composition product.
This model commutes with arbitrary derived extension of the scalar ring.

Proof. Send qj to Rqj and θi to −ιi . The images of the qj commute, the images of the θi satisfy
the exterior relations, and the two families commute. There is a sign worth calculating rather than
suppressing:
                              [Rpi , Rqj ](w) = w(qj pi − pi qj ) = −Mij w.

                                                                82
Chapter 19. Relative Weyl vacua and the algebra of their endomorphisms


Therefore the endomorphism differential satisfies
                                                     X
                                   [dK , Rqj ] = −        Mij ιi ,       [dK , −ιi ] = 0.
                                                      i

This is exactly the differential prescribed on GM .
   Compose the map with postcomposition by the resolution augmentation:

                                       EndWM (K) −→ HomWM (K, VM ).

This second map is a quasi-isomorphism because K is bounded and termwise projective. One can
check it directly: applying HomWM (K, −) to the acyclic augmentation cone gives a finite total
complex whose columns are finite sums of acyclic complexes.
   The composite from GM is an isomorphism of underlying complexes. In exterior degree r, a
monomial q α θi1 · · · θir evaluates on the matching wedge to q α multiplied by a sign, and evaluates on
all other basis wedges of that degree to zero. With increasing index order, the sign is (−1)r(r+1)/2 .
Thus its matrix is diagonal with entries 1 or −1. Its compatibility with the differential was already
checked through the endomorphism algebra. The two-out-of-three property proves the first map is a
quasi-isomorphism of algebras.
   Finally GM has only a + 1 cohomological degrees, and each term is a free R-module. For R → S,
derived scalar extension is therefore computed by ordinary scalar extension, giving GM ⊗S . The
resolution and its endomorphism comparison are defined by the same formulas after extension. This
proves the assertion for this vacuum; it is not a general base-change theorem for arbitrary infinite
Hochschild centres.


19.4     Rank, torsion, and the failure of a field-only classification
Over a characteristic-zero field, elementary changes of the p and q bases put a matrix of rank r
into a block with r unit entries and all other entries zero. The model is then a tensor product of r
polynomial de Rham complexes and the zero-differential algebra

                                        ℸ[qr+1 , . . . , qb ] ⊗ Λ(θr+1 , . . . , θa ).

For a unit pair, the contraction is

                         q n+1
            h(q n θ) =         ,       h(q n ) = 0,          dh + hd = 1 − projection to constants.
                         n+1
Tensoring gives a quasi-isomorphism onto the displayed zero-differential algebra. This proof uses
the indicated characteristic-zero divisions.
  Over a general ring, the matrix must remain in the differential. For M = (z r ) over R = Zp [z],
auxiliary weight n ≥ 1 is the two-term complex
                                                          nz r
                                                  Rq n −−→ Rq n−1 θ.

Its cokernel is R/(nz r ). Both the arithmetic factor n and the collision factor z r are present. The
mixed-cone calculation in the integral chapter compares these actual complexes and their maps; a
rank calculation at a generic field point would discard their interaction.

19.5     Faithfulness does not imply generation
Take the first Weyl algebra over a characteristic-zero field, with [p, q] = 1. Its polynomial vacuum
V = ℸ[q] is faithful. Indeed if N              j
                                  P
                                    j=0 fj (q)∂q acts as zero, commuting it with multiplication by
q lowers its operator order and identifies N fN as the leading coefficient. Induction on N , and
characteristic zero, imply every fj vanishes.
   Nevertheless V is not a generator of all derived W -modules. Let

                     N = ℸ[p, p−1 ],     p act by multiplication,        q = −∂p .

The commutator is [p, −∂p ] = 1, so this is a nonzero W -module. Applying HomW (−, N ) to
        Rp                         p

                                  RHomW (V, N ) = 0,         N ̸= 0.

The relative endomorphism algebra controls the subcategory generated by the vacuum, not every
representation of the operator algebra. A Morita theorem must specify its generator and its generated
subcategory.


19.6     BRST differentials and what a concentration theorem supplies
Suppose A is an augmented graded algebra with a degree-one derivation Q satisfying Q2 = 0 and
preserving the augmentation. On suspended letters the internal bar differential is bQ (sa) = −sQa.
Its coderivation extension obeys b2Q = 0. The binary bar coderivation obeys b2m = 0, and

                                         bQ bm + bm bQ = 0

is precisely the graded Leibniz identity for Q. Hence the bar of the dg algebra (A, Q) is the actual
                  c
                                 
total complex T (sA), bQ + bm .
   The assertion is not applicable to a claimed vacuum augmentation which is not an algebra map.
For an unaugmented operator algebra one must instead choose an actual module and compatible
differentials on the algebra–module resolution. A trace is not that choice.
   If the cohomology of a dg algebra A is concentrated in degree zero, there is a natural zigzag of
dg algebra quasi-isomorphisms
                                            alg
                                    A ←− τ≤0    A −→ H 0 (A).
The middle algebra has Ai in negative degrees, ker(d : A0 → A1 ) in degree zero, and zero in
positive degrees. Its multiplication is inherited from A. Inclusion and projection induce the stated
cohomology isomorphisms. For compatible augmentations, bar invariance therefore gives a zigzag
between BA and B(H 0 A). This proves an equivalence without declaring that a spectral-sequence
edge homomorphism is a canonical inverse chain map. When the concentration or augmentation
hypotheses fail, that argument is unavailable; neither can be supplied by the name of a reduction
procedure.

20.1      A complete finite-mode theorem

and all other commutators zero. Consider left modules on which each yi is locally nilpotent. Since
the yi commute, this is enough for every finite product of the formulas below to be finite on a given
vector.
   In one pair define

The sum is pointwise finite, not a formal element of the ordinary Weyl algebra. Using yxn =
xn y + nxn−1 , the terms of yΠ cancel in consecutive pairs. Thus yΠ = 0, while Πv = v when yv = 0.
On a horizontal vector,

Theorem 20.1 (Locally nilpotent Weyl reconstruction). The functors
                                                                    \
                     V 7−→ R[x1 , . . . , xs ] ⊗R V,        M 7−→         ker(yi : M → M )

are inverse equivalences between R-modules and the displayed Weyl-module category. They are exact.
The first functor uses polynomial multiplication and yi = ∂xi .

Proof. The projectors Πi commute because different pairs commute. Their product projects onto the
joint vacuum. The multivariable Taylor identity, obtained by multiplying the one-variable identities,

                                                       85
Chapter 20. Vacuum reconstruction, smooth modes, and the arithmetic normal direction


shows that Φ(p ⊗ v) = pv is surjective. Applying the projectors after the appropriate monomials in

                                       X xα
                                                   ⊗ (Π1 · · · Πs )y α m.
                                       α∈Ns
                                              α!

These formulas are natural and respect every generator. The polynomial module is free over R, so
its tensor functor is exact. Its inverse is therefore exact; directly, applying the projector to a lift of
a horizontal vector proves surjectivity of the vacuum functor on epimorphisms.

   This is an equivalence in a specified category, not a statement that the vacuum generates all
Weyl modules. The module with y = ∂x + c for a nonzero constant c has no cohomology under its
two-term vacuum Hom complex: ∂x + c is invertible on polynomials, with inverse j≥0 (−1)j c−j−1 ∂xj ,
                                                                              P

pointwise finite. It is outside the locally nilpotent category.


20.2      Infinitely many pairs require one further hypothesis
Let W∞ (R) have countably many commuting Weyl pairs. Require not only local nilpotence of each
yi , but also
                     for each m ∈ M, yi m = 0 for all sufficiently large i.             (20.1)
This is smoothness for the positive modes.

Theorem 20.2 (Smooth all-mode reconstruction). Under local nilpotence and (20.1), the joint
vacuum and the polynomial Fock module in countably many variables give inverse exact equivalences
with R-modules.

Proof. For a fixed vector choose N beyond its active positive modes. The finite-mode reconstruction
gives a finite sum in creators x1 , . . . , xN . Every resulting coefficient is horizontal for those modes.
It is still killed by yi for i > N , since those operators commute with all the finite-mode projectors
and derivatives used on the original vector. Thus the coefficients belong to the full joint vacuum.
Enlarging N adds projectors which act as identity on these expressions, so the formulas agree.
Finite coefficient extraction proves injectivity exactly as before. This constructs natural inverse
maps on the entire module, with no infinite product acting on a vector. Exactness follows from the
free polynomial inverse functor.

   The extra hypothesis cannot be deleted. Over a field of characteristic zero, take basis vectors ea
indexed by sequences of nonnegative integers eventually equal to one, and let

                                   xi ea = ea+ei ,          yi ea = ai ea−ei .

The Weyl relations hold. Each yi is locally nilpotent on every finite linear combination. Nevertheless
the joint vacuum is zero. For a nonzero combination, choose i outside the finitely many exceptional
supports of its basis sequences. Every ai = 1, and yi sends the distinct basis vectors to distinct
basis vectors with coefficient one. It does not kill the combination. The module is nonzero and
violates (20.1). This explicit counterexample is retained from the cross-volume audit [31], whose
corrected finite and infinite vacuum hypotheses are used here.



                                                       86
Chapter 20. Vacuum reconstruction, smooth modes, and the arithmetic normal direction


20.3        The theorem extends to the full Heisenberg fields
Let [am , an ] = mκδm+n,0 , with κ a unit in R, and fix the central zero mode a0 to act by a scalar λ.
For n > 0 put
                                    xn = a−n ,     yn = (nκ)−1 an .
The smooth locally nilpotent modules are classified by Theorem 20.2. Their current field

                                                        an z −n−1
                                                  X
                                         J(z) =
                                                  n∈Z

is lower truncated on each vector by smoothness. A state of the vacuum Heisenberg algebra has the
field
                                                          Y ∂znj −1 J(z)
                                                           r
                              Y (a−n1 · · · a−nr 1, z) =:                :.
                                                          j=1
                                                              (nj − 1)!

Move creation modes to the left and annihilation modes to the right, keeping the zero mode as its
fixed scalar. Every commutation produces the stated central oscillator coefficient. Induction on the
number of factors gives the finite sum over cross-contractions, with each contraction a derivative of
κ(z − w)−2 . This proves all state-field identities: the vacuum is the empty product; differentiation
gives translation; and the two expansions of a product or iterate are expansions of the same finite
rational contraction sum after the prescribed denominator powers are cleared. This is the usual
Heisenberg construction [18], with its module domain now made explicit.
   A mode-preserving module map preserves every normally ordered field and its derivatives.
Conversely a field-preserving module map preserves the modes of J. Hence the reconstruction
theorem is a theorem about these full vertex modules, not merely their selected finite-mode
restrictions. Different values of the zero-mode scalar are different blocks and are not identified.

                                            ∼

Proof. The displayed Eij prove surjectivity and have the products of matrix units. PBW makes

adjugate gives an inverse. The telescoping identity for yΠ0 has only its final term left, containing
y p , so it vanishes in the quotient. Thus Π0 M = ker y for every quotient module. Matrix-unit
decomposition gives the inverse functors. Equivalently the finite Taylor identity reconstructs every
vector from its p horizontal coefficients.

retained central ring distinguishes them.

20.6       Divided operators and iterative translation

21.1      An integral complex whose fibres conceal its multiplication
Over Zp let
                          D = Zp [q] ⊗ Λ(θ),        |q| = 0,     |θ| = 1,    dq = θ.
The variable is polynomial, not implicitly completed. In auxiliary weight n ≥ 1 the complex is
multiplication by n from Zp q n to Zp q n−1 θ. Therefore
                                                             M
                            H 0 (D) = Zp ,       H 1 (D) =         Zp /(n)[q n−1 θ].
                                                             n≥1

Put α = q p−1 θ. Since d(q p ) = pα, the triple Massey product ⟨[α], [p], [α]⟩ is defined. Its representative
from the two primitives q p is
                                                 2q 2p−1 θ.
The indeterminacy is Zp [α], in weight p. The displayed class is in weight 2p, whose cohomology is
Zp /(2p). It is nonzero: for odd p, 2 is a unit modulo p, and for p = 2, 2 ̸= 0 modulo 4. Thus the
coset does not contain zero.
   Over Qp , the homotopy h(q n−1 θ) = q n /n contracts all positive weights. Over Fp , the inclusion

                                       Fp [q p ] ⊗ Λ(q p−1 θ) −→ D ⊗ Fp

is a multiplicative cohomology isomorphism, by inspection of monomial derivatives. Both fibres
are formal. The family is not: in its zero-differential cohomology algebra the corresponding two
products are zero and zero primitives give a Massey value zero, contradicting invariance of the
nonzero coset under quasi-isomorphisms. This is a multiplication-level extension obstruction over
the base.


21.2      Finite differences retain a different product
Let T (f )(q) = f (q + 1) and define

                    DT = R[q] ⊕ R[q]ϵ,       ϵf = T (f )ϵ,     ϵ2 = 0,    df = (T f − f )ϵ.

                                                     89
Chapter 21. Integral extensions and divided operators


The identity T (f g) − f g = (T f − f )T g + f (T g − g) proves the dg Leibniz rule. The falling factorials
(q)n = q(q − 1) · · · (q − n + 1) satisfy (T − 1)(q)n = n(q)n−1 . Sending q n to (q)n identifies the
underlying complexes with the preceding differential complex, but is not multiplicative: (q)2 = q 2 − q.
   Over Zp use α′ = (q)p−1 ϵ and primitive (q)p . The analogous Massey representative is
                                                                     
                                      (q)p (q)p−1 + (q)p−1 (q + 1)p ϵ.

Its leading falling-factorial coefficient is 2 in coordinate 2p − 1. Exact elements in that coordinate
have coefficient divisible by 2p, and the indeterminacy has only coordinate p − 1. Thus the class is
again nonzero. An additive identification of two complexes did not identify their multiplication or
tensor law.

21.5      Supported differential operators and Frobenius digits
On R[x] the Hasse derivative satisfies ∂ [n] xm = m   m−n . The map

   The vacuum is the supported module R[x, x−1 ]/R[x], under γn 7→ x−n−1 . Multiplication by
x lowers the index, while the negative Hasse derivatives give exactly the divided-power binomial
coefficients. This is not the ordinary polynomial vacuum with the same generator names.
   Over Fp , put σ(P ) = P p , σ(γj ) = γpj , X = γ1 , and D0 = P − γp−1 P p . The binomial identities
modulo p show that σ is an injective algebra map. On the vacuum basis γpj+i , 0 ≤ i < p, X raises
the first digit with coefficient i + 1, and D0 lowers it without borrowing from the next digit. Hence
X p = D0p = 0 and [D0 , X] = 1. They generate the full first-digit matrix algebra: XD0 has the
distinct eigenvalues 0, . . . , p − 1, so interpolation gives its diagonal projectors, and powers of X, D0
give all matrix units.
   These operators commute with σ(WΓ ), which acts on the remaining digits. They generate the
original algebra because γpj+i = γpj X i /i! and P = D0 + γp−1 P p . Faithfulness can be checked
without assumption: a finite normal sum acting on all large γn has coefficients which are finite linear

                                                       91
Chapter 21. Integral extensions and divided operators


combinations of n+d
                      
                    a . Consecutive values give a matrix of determinant one by finite differences,
forcing every coefficient to vanish only for the zero operator. Thus
                                         WΓ ∼
                                            = Matp (Fp ) ⊗ σ(WΓ ).
Keeping only the first matrix factor would discard all the remaining operator digits.


21.6     A scalar annihilator which detects an extension
Let R = Zp [z], a = b + r with b, r ≥ 1, n ≥ 1, and c = r(n − 1). Consider
                                              (z rn ,−nz a )t      (nz b ,z c )
                                  Cn :     R −−−−−−−→ R2 −−−−−→ R
in degrees −1, 0, 1. The composite is zero because a + c = b + rn. Put g = min(b, c). A direct kernel
computation gives
                          H −1 = 0,      H 0 = R/(z r+g ),          H 1 = R/(nz b , z c ).
For c ≥ b, the middle kernel is generated by (z c−b , −n), since n and z c−b are relatively prime in the
UFD R. The first image is z a times that generator. For c < b, the kernel is generated by (1, −nz b−c )
and the image is z rn times it. These are the stated exponents.
   The ideal of scalars null-homotopic on this entire complex is exactly
                                                 (z rn , nz a ).
For sufficiency use the homotopies with components H10 (u, w) = u, H11 (1) = (0, z r ), and H20 (u, w) =
−w, H21 (1) = (z r , 0). Matrix multiplication gives dH1 + H1 d = z rn id and dH2 + H2 d = nz a id.
Conversely the degree-−1 component of any scalar homotopy gives s = H 0 (z rn , −nz a ), so s lies in
this ideal.
   For p = 2, a = 3, b = 1, r = 2, n = 2, multiplication by z 3 kills both cohomology modules but is
not null-homotopic: z 3 ∈
                        / (z 4 , 2z 3 ). Thus an annihilator of cohomology is not an annihilator of the
derived object. When c > b, the exact sequence in H 1 has extension class 1 in R/(n, z min(b,c−b) ),
obtained by lifting 1 and multiplying by z b . The class is nonzero exactly when p divides n.


21.7     Tensor laws and integral intervals
The formal group laws x + y and x + y + hxy arise from additive and multiplicative composition of
operator data. Associativity of the second follows by expanding either threefold composition to
x + y + z + h(xy + xz + yz) + h2 xyz. Over Fp their p-series at h = 0, 1 are respectively zero and xp .
No invertible formal coordinate change conjugates them. A characteristic-zero logarithm is not an
integral tensor equivalence.
   Similarly ∂xp = 0 on Fp [x] does not make the truncated exponential multiplicative. It sends x to
x − t but sends xp to xp , not (x − t)p . Full translation retains all Hasse derivatives.
    An associative interval over any ring has basis e0 , e1 in degree zero and h in degree one, with
e2i = ei , e0 e1 = e1 e0 = 0, e0 h = h = he1 , e1 h = he0 = h2 = 0, and de0 = −h, de1 = h. The unit is
e0 + e1 . Direct checks give the Leibniz identity. The homotopy H(h) = e1 , H(ei ) = 0 contracts the
kernel of evaluation at endpoint zero. It is an associative, not strictly commutative, interval. The
integral polynomial de Rham interval has the torsion just calculated and cannot replace it by an
undeclared equivalence.

Chapter 22

A polarized boundary calculation

22.1      The two halves of a factorization
Let R = ℸ[x, y] and let E = R1|1 be a free supermodule. Put
                                                 !
                                              0 x
                                   D=             ,        D2 = xy id .
                                              y 0

The endomorphism algebra is Z/2-graded and has differential dF = DF − (−1)|F | F D. Since D2 is
scalar, d2 F = [D2 , F ] = 0. This is a genuine dg endomorphism algebra of a matrix factorization,
although the factorization differential itself squares to the potential rather than zero.
   Let a = E12 and b = E21 . Then

                        a2 = b2 = 0,      ab + ba = 1,        da = y,     db = x.

These equations use no division by two. The two even idempotents are ab and ba. The odd
anticommutator is the nonzero unit, not a relation that can be replaced by an exterior product.
   For an even diagonal matrix F = diag(u, v),
                                                                   !
                                                  0     x(v − u)
                                       dF =                      .
                                               y(u − v)     0

For an odd matrix G = ( 0s 0r ),
                                          dG = (xs + yr) id .
These formulas give all differential components and their signs.

Theorem 22.1 (The node endomorphism algebra). The even cohomology is ℸ id and the odd
cohomology is zero. The algebra is formal over ℸ, but is not formal over R to that cohomology
algebra with the scalar action x = y = 0.

Proof. An even cycle has u = v because R is a domain. Even boundaries are scalar matrices in the
ideal (x, y), so even cohomology is ℸ. An odd cycle satisfies xs + yr = 0, hence (r, s) = (xh, −yh)
by relative primality. These are exactly the even differentials. Thus odd cohomology is zero. The
scalar-unit map ℸ → EndR (E) is a dg algebra quasi-isomorphism, proving formality over ℸ.


                                                      93
Chapter 22. A polarized boundary calculation


   For the relative assertion, specialize derivedly along R → ℸ. The original complex is finite free
in the two-periodic sense; tensor with the finite Koszul resolution of ℸ and then compare with its
ordinary special fibre. Since x, y are termwise a regular sequence on the free modules, this gives the
supermatrix algebra Endℸ (ℸ1|1 ) with zero differential. Its even part is ℸ × ℸ.
   For the zero-differential cohomology algebra ℸ with its R action, the derived fibre is ℸ ⊗LR ℸ,
represented by the exterior algebra on two odd Koszul generators. Its even part has a nonzero
square-zero element and no two-branch idempotent decomposition. The two fibre algebras cannot
be isomorphic. An R-linear algebra quasi-isomorphism or zigzag would preserve them, so no relative
formality exists.

   The scalar quasi-isomorphism in the first part is not R-linear: multiplication by x, y is null-
homotopic in the target, not strictly zero there. This is precisely the datum that the relative
comparison must retain. The calculation therefore does not conflict with formality over a field; it
identifies which scalar structure was forgotten.


22.2      All hyperbolic ranks
Let R = ℸ[x1 , . . . , xr , y1 , . . . , yr ] and E = ΛR (Rr ). Let ai be exterior multiplication by the ith basis
vector and bi its contraction. Then

                                 {ai , aj } = {bi , bj } = 0,    {ai , bj } = δij ,

with a2i = b2i = 0 also imposed in characteristic two. Put D = i (xi ai + yi bi ). The relations give
                                                                          P

D2 = i xi yi . The endomorphism differential has dai = yi , dbi = xi .
      P

    The ai , bi generate the full supermatrix endomorphism algebra. For one pair the four operators
1, a, b, ab are a basis; tensoring the independent factors proves the statement in all ranks. As
an underlying complex the differential is the folded Koszul complex on the regular sequence
x1 , y1 , . . . , xr , yr , with the signs from the ordered odd basis. Tensoring the one-pair exact calculation
shows its cohomology is ℸ in even parity only.
   The ordinary special fibre is the full supermatrix algebra Endℸ (Λℸr ), while the derived fibre
of its cohomology algebra is exterior on 2r odd generators. Both have dimension 4r , but their
multiplications differ. In particular the matrix even part has nontrivial idempotents, whereas the
even part of the finite exterior algebra is local with nilpotent augmentation ideal. Thus the relative
nonformality persists in every positive rank.
    This polarized calculation survives characteristic two. It never symmetrizes an operator by
dividing by two. A proposed normal algebra constructed from half an anticommutator with a
forbidden denominator would lose exactly this robustness. The characteristic-free polarized viewpoint
is consistent with the programme’s supplied normal-direction calculations; it is not a replacement of
their integral scalar data by a characteristic-zero shadow.


22.3      A trace on cohomology is not a cyclic chain trace
For the one-pair model define a degree-zero functional on even matrices by τ (diag(u, v)) = u(0, 0)
and let it vanish on odd matrices. It vanishes on boundaries because those are scalar matrices in
(x, y), and induces the nonzero trace on the one-dimensional cohomology algebra.


                                                         94
Chapter 22. A polarized boundary calculation


  It is not graded cyclic on all chains. For a = E12 and b = E21 ,

                                      τ (ab) = 1,        τ (ba) = 0.

Graded cyclicity for these two odd elements would require τ (ab) = −τ (ba). The failure is not a
contradiction with the trace on cohomology, since a, b are not closed in the family. A cyclic or
Calabi–Yau enhancement at chain level requires the additional homotopies or a cyclic model; it is
not supplied by this one scalar functional.
   The same distinction applies to residue pairings obtained from derivatives of a matrix-factorization
differential. Such a formula can induce the correct pairing on cohomology without being a strict
cyclic trace on the displayed endomorphism complex. The module, its curved differential, its
endomorphism dg algebra, and a chosen cyclic enhancement are four separate pieces of information.


22.4     Probes and what they generate
For a dg algebra A and a perfect module M , the functor RHomA (M, −) identifies the thick
subcategory generated by M with perfect modules over RHomA (M, M ), with the appropriate
right-module convention. This follows by the same finite-cell and retract argument used in the
compact-generator proof: the unit and counit are equivalences on the generating module and remain
so under shifts, cones and retracts.
   The conclusion concerns the subcategory generated by the probe. Extending it to the entire
module category requires a generator theorem. A faithful action on a vector space does not prove
that the corresponding derived probe detects every object. The matrix-factorization calculation
above computes the probe endomorphisms explicitly; no statement that it generates every matrix
factorization is inferred solely from that computation.

23.1     A boundary model with all objects visible
Let S = ℸ[x] and let f = xd , d ≥ 2. A finite free matrix factorization is a Z/2-graded free S-module
with an odd endomorphism Q satisfying Q2 = f id. For two such objects the morphism differential
is
                                     ∂F = Qs F − (−1)|F | F Qr .
It squares to zero because both potentials are the same. Composition satisfies the graded Leibniz
identity. These statements define the dg category before any trace or cohomology is taken.
   Put                                                      !
                                             0 xd−r
                                   Qr =             ,                0 ≤ r ≤ d.
                                             xr 0
Every finite free matrix factorization of xd over ℸ[x] is a direct sum of these. Indeed its two
differentials become inverse up to multiplication by xd over ℸ(x), so their ranks agree. Smith
normal form over the principal ideal domain ℸ[x] diagonalizes one of them. The other is then
its diagonal inverse times xd , and polynomiality forces every invariant factor to divide xd . After
multiplying by units, these factors are xr , producing the displayed summands. The objects Q0 , Qd are
contractible: an elementary odd matrix gives ∂h = 1. Thus Q1 , . . . , Qd−1 supply all noncontractible
indecomposable building blocks in this finite free family.

Theorem 23.1 (All morphism groups). For every r, s,

              H 0̄ Hom(Qr , Qs ) = ℸ[x]/xnrs Ers ,        H 1̄ Hom(Qr , Qs ) = ℸ[x]/xnrs Ors .

The formulas hold over every field, including characteristic two when signs are read in that field.

Proof. For an even matrix F = diag(a, b) the two closedness equations are

                                    xd−s b = xd−r a,        xs a = xr b.

Cancellation in the polynomial domain shows F = tErs for a unique polynomial t. If G = ( v0 u0 ) is
odd, its even boundary has coefficient

                                        xmin(r,s) u + xd−max(r,s) v

relative to Ers . These coefficients form the ideal generated by the two displayed powers, namely
(xnrs ). This proves the even formula.
   The odd closedness equations reduce to xr u + xd−s v = 0. Factoring their greatest common power
of x gives G = tOrs . The odd boundary of diag(a, b) has coefficient

                                            xs−vrs a − xr−vrs b

relative to Ors . Its coefficient ideal is (xmin(r,s)−vrs ) = (xnrs ). The last equality follows separately
from r + s ≤ d and r + s ≥ d. Thus the odd formula holds as well. No division by the characteristic
or by a potential derivative enters.

Theorem 23.2 (The entire composition table). All kpq are nonnegative and the following identity
holds as an identity of polynomial matrices, before passing to cohomology:
                                        q p                   p+q

The parity in the superscript is read modulo two. Coefficient classes are subsequently reduced modulo
xnrt .

Proof. For two even matrices the upper-left exponent is est +ers , giving k00 ; the lower-right exponent
gives the same quotient because eab −eba = a−b. For an even map followed by an odd one, the upper-
right exponent is ust + esr and the lower-left exponent is vst + ers . The identity vab − uab = a + b − d
shows that their quotients by the two entries of Ort agree. The other mixed case is the same
computation with upper-right exponent est + urs . Two odd matrices multiply to a negative diagonal
matrix whose upper-left exponent is ust + vrs ; the lower-right entry agrees after using the same two
elementary identities. This proves the formulas. Nonnegativity follows either from these polynomial
factorizations or directly from (a + b)+ ≤ a+ + b+ in the four cases. Associativity is ordinary matrix
associativity, so no separate guessed multiplication law is imposed on the cohomology groups.

An odd class need not square to zero in this Z/2-graded associative algebra. Rescaling the odd
generator changes this square by a square scalar, not by an undeclared removal of the sign.

23.5       Relative nonformality is seen by an idempotent
Let Kx = S ⊗ Λ(ϵ) with |ϵ| = −1 and dϵ = x. It is a finite free resolution of ℸ over S. Tensoring
with it preserves quasi-isomorphisms, even for unbounded or two-periodic complexes, because its
underlying operation is the cone of multiplication by x.
   For Qr put n = nr and P = diag(1, 0). One computes ∂P = xn θr . In End(Qr ) ⊗S Kx the even
element
                                      P = P − ϵxn−1 θr
is closed. It is an idempotent because P 2 = P , P θr + θr P = θr , and ϵ2 = 0, with odd scalars
anticommuting with odd matrices.
   The actual specialization of the chain algebra has Qr = 0 and is the full matrix superalgebra
Endℸ (ℸ1|1 ). Its even part is ℸ × ℸ. If one first replaces End(Qr ) by its cohomology algebra, the
even derived fibre instead has its scalar class and the Tor class ϵxn−1 θr . The latter squares to zero.
Its degree-zero algebra is the dual numbers. Therefore no S-linear dg algebra formality zigzag can
identify End(Qr ) with its cohomology. The class P is the operation that such a replacement would
lose.
   This statement concerns formality over S. For d = 2, r = 1, the constant closed odd matrix has
square −1 and the corresponding Clifford algebra maps quasi-isomorphically to End(Q1 ) over ℸ.
Thus relative nonformality and absolute formality can occur together.
                                                     0 −x       has θ2 = −x. The odd matrix u = −E12 satisfies

The triple Massey product of θ has zero indeterminacy and is nonzero. This detects absolute

is zero in cohomology for d > 3. Consequently this particular triple test does not prove the higher-
degree cases. The higher relation must occur in its proper arity, as in the truncated-polynomial bar
construction, rather than be inferred from a nonzero chain representative.

23.7      The triangle has a nonzero but exact defect

are even and closed. Indeed ∂Iij = Qj − Qi = −(tj − ti )Uij . Also Tji Tij = I, because Uij = Uji as

23.8      An all-arity replacement of those pairwise identifications
Pairwise inverse maps and one triangle homotopy are not a complete coherent diagram. In this
family one can construct every higher component rather than merely state an obstruction tower.
   Choose the probe labelled 0 as an anchor and define

                                              Eij = T0j Ti0 .

                                                   100
Chapter 23. Every map in a boundary family and its higher coherence


Then Ejk Eij = Eik exactly, because the two middle factors cancel. The Eij form a strict diagram
of closed isomorphisms. Equation (23.1), with middle index 0, gives explicit odd homotopies hij
such that
                                       dtot hij = Tij − Eij .
For i, j ̸= 0 distinct, take the negative of the displayed triangle primitive; take hii = h0j = hi0 = 0.
This prescription is consistent with all these identities.
   For the remainder of this section assume characteristic zero. All endomorphism complexes are
Z/2-graded, and the degree statements in the convolution calculation are read in that parity together
with the bar suspensions. Fix an ordered finite list of probe labels. Let S0 be the product of one
copy of ℸ for each list position, and let L be the strictly upper-triangular ideal in the incidence
algebra of that ordered list. Its arrows are the unique arrows i → j for i < j, with their evident
compositions. This is an actual augmented algebra over S0 ; its reduced relative bar has only finite
composable strings. Let g be the convolution dg algebra of maps from that bar to the corresponding
total endomorphism algebra of the probes, with its commutator dg Lie bracket. Word length gives
a nilpotent filtration. A degree-one Maurer–Cartan element is exactly an A∞ diagram on the list,
with the bar suspension conventions of Chapter 3.
   The strict diagram E gives a Maurer–Cartan element αE . Let g have only a length-one component,
assigning −hij to the arrow i → j. Its convolution degree is zero: the bar arrow has degree −1 and
hij is odd of degree −1 in the total convention. Define

                                                        ead g − 1
                                      αT = ead g αE −             dg.                            (23.2)
                                                          ad g

The quotient is the finite series r≥0 (ad g)r /(r + 1)!, not division by an operator. It is finite on
                                  P

every composable word, and in the incidence algebra there is even a uniform finite word bound.

Theorem 23.4 (Every simplex coherence is constructed). Formula (23.2) is a Maurer–Cartan
element. Its one-arrow components are the originally prescribed Tij . It supplies all higher coherence
maps on every finite ordered probe list. Restriction to a sublist commutes with the construction. No
claim that arbitrary mixed operadic obstructions vanish is used.

Proof. In any nilpotent dg Lie algebra, differentiating et ad g αE − 0t es ad g dg ds shows that its
                                                                         R

curvature is et ad g times the initial curvature. The verification uses only the derivation rule for d
and Jacobi; expand each finite exponential, or solve the resulting linear differential equation for the
curvature coefficient by coefficient. Since αE is Maurer–Cartan, the curvature is zero at t = 1. This
proves the full equation, not just its lowest arities.
   In length one, every commutator raises the word filtration. Formula (23.2) therefore gives
(αT )1 = (αE )1 − (dg)1 = Eij + dhij = Tij . The remaining components are explicitly the finite
iterated commutator sums in that formula. The Maurer–Cartan equation in length n is precisely the
nth coherent-composition identity. Convolution, the strict maps E, and the homotopies h restrict
to the same formulas on a sublist, so the entire finite series restricts as well.

   This is an all-arity solution because there is a constructed strict starting diagram and constructed
homotopies to the prescribed arrows. It does not turn an arbitrary coefficient operad into a solved
mixed state-space action. Nor does choosing an anchor make the automorphism group of a probe
disappear. Changing the anchor transports the diagram by actual closed isomorphisms; the marked
trivialization data, not an unmarked contractible groupoid, are what have been constructed.

24.1     The geometry, the parameter, and the completion
Let d = 2s > 0, let A = ℸ[x1 , . . . , xd ] over a characteristic-zero field, and fix a nondegenerate
constant skew matrix π = (π ij ). Put S = Spec A, U = S \ {0}, and let Yr be the scheme made from
r ≥ 2 copies of S by identifying their copies of U through the identity. Write

                                         Dr = RΓ(Yr , OYr ).

The earlier two-dimensional normal-centre source [30] treats d = 2, r = 2. We develop the dimension-
and branch-dependent statement, including the full parameter torsion and a proof of which classical
residue classes lift.
   On every localization B = A[x−1

These conventions permit increasing polynomial or pole order in successive parameter coefficients

                                          [ui , uj ] = ℏπ ij − ℏπ ij = 0.

The ui multiply the coefficient on the right, so this formula is left Bℏe -linear. Commutation of the ui
gives d2 = 0. The augmentation is action on 1 in the diagonal bimodule.

Lemma 24.1 (Complete exactness). This augmented complex resolves the diagonal on every
displayed affine localization. The resolutions restrict compatibly on all overlaps.

Proof. Modulo ℏ the complex is the ordinary coordinate-difference Koszul resolution. The differences
form a regular sequence, including after localization, by the polynomial change of variables used in
Lemma 15.1. The augmented reduction is therefore acyclic.
   Let c be a cycle in the complete augmentation cone. Choose a primitive b0 modulo ℏ, lift it,
and write c − db0 = ℏc1 . The terms of the cone are parameter-torsion-free, so dc1 = 0. Repeat:
solve for b1 modulo ℏ, subtract, and divide the residual. The convergent series j≥0 ℏj bj has
                                                                                       P

differential c. This proves acyclicity of the complete cone. The argument solves a primitive equation
in the specified complete modules rather than inferring convergence from a spectral sequence name.
Coordinate generators and localization maps are identical on an overlap, so every resolution and
augmentation restricts to the one already written there.


24.3      The cochain product has an independent comparison
Applying bimodule Hom gives B[[ℏ]] ⊗ Λ(η1 , . . . , ηd ). Fix the signs so that the degree-zero differential
is the positive commutator in (24.1); this is obtained from the usual Hom signs by multiplying the
degree-q exterior-functional identification by (−1)q(q+1)/2 . Then
                                                          X
                                              δf = ℏ            π ij ∂j f ηi .
                                                          i,j


Let ω = π −1 and identify ηi with k ωki dxk . Since i ωki π ij = δkj , the differential becomes ℏdhol .
                                       P                           P

This is an actual change of basis using the nondegenerate form, not division by ℏ.
   To verify the multiplication, compare with the normalized two-sided bar. Send an exterior word
in the ei to the antisymmetrized bar word in the xi . Pairs of interior bar multiplications combine
into [xi , xj ]⋆ = ℏπ ij 1, which is zero in a normalized interior slot relative to ℸ[[ℏ]]. The remaining
endpoint terms are exactly the coordinate-difference Koszul differential. Thus this is a chain map in
every exterior degree. A permutation and a word cut are the same data as a subset, its order and

                                                           103
Chapter 24. Formal quantum normal centres in arbitrary even dimension


the order of its complement, so the comparison intertwines exterior shuffle with bar deconcatenation.
Cochain restriction is therefore multiplicative for convolution, and identifies its product with Moyal
multiplication of coefficients followed by exterior multiplication.
   Both resolutions compute the same derived bimodule Hom: the complete finite resolution
was proved exact, and the complete bar has the usual unit-insertion contraction order by order.
Comparison of the resolutions, or the cap endomorphism associated to the shuffle diagonal, shows
that this cup model has the correct associative derived algebra type. In the cap description, (1 ⊗ f )∆
is an endomorphism of Pℏ ; coassociativity gives composition of caps, and postcomposition with
the augmentation recovers f . Since Pℏ is finite complete free, that latter Hom comparison is a
quasi-isomorphism. The tensor evaluation signs are those fixed before writing δ.
   This argument identifies an associative cochain algebra. Braces on a small Koszul model require
a transported higher structure; the exterior shuffle alone is not a proof that the small model has
strict naive braces. The full bar retains those operations when they are needed.

Theorem 24.2 (Formal quantum geometric centre). The continuous diagonal cochains of Aℏ on
Yr , relative to ℸ[[ℏ]] and absolute in the coordinate directions, have the unital associative model
                                                                                          
                      Zr,d,ℏ = (Dr ⊗ Λ(dx1 , . . . , dxd ))[[ℏ]], dDr + ℏdhol , ⋆∧ .

The total-complex sign makes the two differentials anticommute. The model uses the actual derived
coefficient algebra.

Proof. Use the matched-chart neighbourhood of the diagonal in Yr × Yr . The coordinate differences
give the finite resolution of the lemma on every matched chart and overlap. Derived open pushforward
is fully faithful on these diagonal objects, just as in the classical calculation. Compute global Hom
by a compatible multiplicative derived-section model; the exterior factor is finite and commutes
with that construction. The local cochain differential and product were proved above and restrict
identically, giving the displayed global model.
   Alternatively make the entire construction modulo ℏN and then take the homotopy inverse
limit. The explicit complexes have degreewise surjective transition maps, so the written complete
complexes represent that limit. A homotopy limit of the compatible quasi-isomorphisms is an
equivalence. This proves the continuous comparison without replacing continuous Hom by an
unspecified algebraic dual.


24.4     The coefficient complex splits linearly, not multiplicatively
Let

It represents the top local-cohomology module at the origin. We recall its entire cochain calculation.
Cover U by D(xi ). In the augmented Cech complex
                                 M                 M
                          A −→        Axi −→             Axi xj −→ · · · −→ Ax1 ···xd ,

Chapter 24. Formal quantum normal centres in arbitrary even dimension


that set is a proper subset of {1, . . . , d}, insert the first missing coordinate in the alternating index

signs. If every exponent is negative, the monomial occurs only in the last term and survives in
degree d. This proves the augmented complex is quasi-isomorphic, as a complex, to Pd [−d].
   Partial differentiation preserves each negative-exponent set whenever it is nonzero. An exponent
zero differentiates to zero, a positive exponent remains nonnegative, and a negative exponent
remains negative. The just-described contraction can consequently be chosen to commute with all
the coordinate derivatives.
   The cover of Yr by its r copies of S has every intersection of distinct charts equal to U . Successive

coordinate variables. It is not asserted to preserve products.

24.5      All quantum cohomology groups, including parameter torsion
Write B q (V ) = im(d : V q−1 → V q ) for the boundary space of a complex of vector spaces. If V is
split into its cohomology and two-term contractible pairs, then
                               H q (V [[ℏ]], ℏd) ∼
                                                 = H q (V, d)[[ℏ]] ⊕ B q (V ),               (24.4)
where ℏ kills the second summand. Indeed a contractible pair becomes multiplication by ℏ between
two copies of a vector-space power-series module. This map is injective and its cokernel is the
boundary space modulo ℏ. The cohomology summand has zero differential. The decomposition
depends on a specified splitting; the parameter action and the exact sequence it expresses do not.
   Polynomial de Rham forms have only the constant cohomology class. On a monomial form of
total weight w > 0, the Euler identity dιE + ιE d = w contracts it by w−1 ιE . Here both a variable
and its differential have weight one. The constants supply weight zero.
   For negative principal parts in one variable,
                                    d(x−a ) = −ax−a−1 dx,        a ≥ 1.
The differential is injective and its cokernel is the line ℸx−1 dx. Contract the other one-forms by
h(x−a−1 dx) = −x−a /a. Tensoring these d one-variable contractions proves

Theorem 24.3 (The dimension- and branch-dependent quantum answer). As ℸ[[ℏ]]-modules, with
the preceding contractions fixing splittings,

Boundary spaces outside their natural degrees are zero. Every B-summand is killed by ℏ. There is
no higher-order parameter torsion. The free top classes are represented by the r − 1 branch-difference

Proof. Apply (24.4) to the two types of de Rham complexes in (24.3). The polynomial complex
supplies its constant free class and its boundary spaces. The principal-part complex supplies its sole
free class in holomorphic degree d, shifted by coefficient degree d, and its shifted boundary spaces.
There are r − 1 independent branch differences. The comparison of complexes proves that these
are all the cohomology groups of the actual quantum diagonal model. Multiplication has not been
declared to split with this module decomposition.

  For d = 2, r = 2, this gives degrees 0, 1, 2, 3, 4, with free classes only in degrees zero and four.
More explicitly the torsion summands are dA, Ω2A , dP2 , and the zero-residue subspace of P2 dx1 dx2 .
Thus the preceding cross-volume calculation is recovered rather than replaced.


24.6      A scalar residue is obstructed, a volume-transgressed flux is
          not
Take 0 ̸= p ∈ Pd in one branch-difference summand of the classical coefficient. It is a degree-d class.
A lift p + ℏz1 + · · · to a cocycle of the quantum model would require
                                              dDr z1 = −dhol p.
In coefficient cohomology the right side is the nonzero class dp in Pd ⊗ Λ1 , since the first differential
of ΩPd is injective. No such z1 exists.
    In contrast, the class with coefficient (x1 · · · xd )−1 and all d holomorphic differentials is already
closed in every order. Its period can be normalized to one: integrate the path and simplex directions
of the branch-difference cocycle, then take the coefficient of x−1            −1
                                                                     1 · · · xd dx1 · · · dxd . Stokes’ formula
kills coefficient boundaries. Faces lacking one inverted coordinate have zero full residue, and endpoint
chart terms are regular. Laurent residues kill holomorphic de Rham boundaries. Thus a nonzero
formal scalar multiple of this top class cannot be a boundary.
   This distinction is the precise correction to calling an untransgressed principal part a flat quantum
charge. The free quantum class includes the holomorphic volume directions and has total degree 2d,
not d.

                                                         106
Chapter 24. Formal quantum normal centres in arbitrary even dimension


24.7     The generic associative algebra and the limits of the analogy
Put K = ℸ((ℏ)). Exact localization kills all the parameter-torsion summands. The cohomology of
the generic quantum centre is
                                         K ⊕ K r−1 [−2d]
with square-zero positive part. Its associative derived algebra is formal. To see this without an
asserted strict augmentation of the Weyl coefficient algebra, transfer to a strictly unital minimal
A∞ model over the field K, choosing the cohomology unit as the actual unit and a contraction
killing it. For q ≥ 2, an operation on q positive-degree inputs has output degree 2dq + 2 − q, which
is larger than 2d. Therefore every such operation vanishes. Strict unitality supplies the remaining
products and kills higher operations containing a unit. The minimal model is exactly the stated
square-zero algebra.
   It has the graded associative algebra type of the cohomology of a wedge of r − 1 spheres of
dimension 2d. This does not identify the quantum field theory with that topological space, and it
does not prove an E∞ refinement of its full higher centre operations. The formal parameter lattice
before localization, calculated in Theorem 24.3, contains extensive information absent from this
generic algebra.


24.8     Covariance must transform the symplectic datum
An affine linear coordinate change x 7→ M x + b transports the constant bivector to M πM t . It
preserves the displayed Moyal product at fixed parameter precisely when it preserves that bivector.
A conformally symplectic change with multiplier c instead requires the corresponding semilinear
change of ℏ, or a changed bivector. The commutator of transformed linear coordinates already
detects this requirement. No scalar multiplication of all observables repairs it while also preserving
the unit.
   The geometric quantum calculation is therefore a completed, coordinate-sensitive comparison of
actual diagonal cochains. It does not identify an independently specified singular Poisson BF model
or a native chiral boundary algebra with those cochains. Such an identification must preserve the
parameter lattice, the obstructed residue classes and the free transgressed flux just computed.
