Chapter 25

All-state fields and independent
exchange

25.1      The differential-algebra field
For an associative unital algebra B with derivation T , define

                                           Y (a, z)b = (ezT a)b.

Over characteristic zero this is a power series, with vacuum 1 and translation T . The creation
identity is Y (a, z)1 = ezT a. Its associativity is an exact formal identity:

                            Y (a, z0 + z2 )Y (b, z2 )c = (e(z0 +z2 )T a)(ez2 T b)c
                                                       = Y (Y (a, z0 )b, z2 )c.

No commutativity was used. When B is noncommutative, the constant fields need not be local.
This is a nonlocal field algebra, not an ordinary vertex algebra by a change of name. In positive
characteristic, replacing ezT by a truncated exponential does not preserve this argument; one needs
an actual iterative Hasse translation action.


25.2      A full smash-product construction
Let V be a vertex algebra and H an ordinary Hopf algebra acting on V so that it commutes with
translation, sends the vacuum by its counit, and satisfies
                                                  X
                               h · YV (v, z)w =       YV (h(1) v, z)(h(2) w).

Every coproduct is a finite algebraic sum. Define on V ⊗ H
                                                   X
                           Y (v#h, z)(w#k) =           YV (v, z)(h(1) w)#h(2) k.

This is the usual smash-product field construction; we give its all-state verification rather than infer
it from a generator formula.
  The vacuum is 1#1, and translation is TV ⊗ 1. The counit and module-vacuum identities give
both creation and the vacuum field. Lower truncation follows from that of V and the finite coproduct
sum. Translation covariance follows because the H action commutes with TV .

                                                    108
Chapter 25. All-state fields and independent exchange


   For weak associativity, expand the product on a third state u#l. The Hopf module identity and
coassociativity give       X
                              YV (v, z1 )YV (h(1) w, z2 )(h(2) k(1) u)#h(3) k(2) l.
The iterate is             X
                               YV (YV (v, z0 )h(1) w, z2 )(h(2) k(1) u)#h(3) k(2) l.
After z1 = z0 + z2 , weak associativity in V identifies them after multiplying by a sufficiently high
power of z0 + z2 , in the stipulated expansion domain. There are finitely many coproduct terms, so
one exponent works for them all. This proves the identity for arbitrary states, not just generators. It
is a nonlocal vertex-algebra construction of the type used in the programme’s earlier smash-product
repair; no equivalence with an associative Ran algebra has been used.


25.3      A singular, genuinely interacting control
Use the rank-two Heisenberg vertex algebra with isotropic generators J + , J − and cross pairing κ.
Its Fock state space is the polynomial algebra on creation symbols Jr± , r ≥ 0, with
                                                           ±
                                           T Jr± = (r + 1)Jr+1 .

The generating operators are

                               J ± (z) =         Jr± z r + κ         n ∂J ∓ z −n−1 .
                                           X                   X
                                                                         n−1
                                           r≥0                 n≥1

The negative-power part acts locally finitely on each polynomial state. Its commutator with the
creation part gives the derivative of the formal delta function, hence the double-pole cross contraction.
Complete Wick normal ordering of divided derivatives supplies the field of every Fock monomial.
Each finite set of state factors has only finitely many Wick matchings, giving the standard creation,
translation and locality identities. Complete Wick ordering of oscillator monomials must not be
confused with arbitrary iterated normally ordered products, whose associators can contain derivative
terms.
   The charge operator Q with QJr+ = Jr+ and QJr− = −Jr− preserves every contraction and
commutes with translation. It is therefore a vertex derivation. Let H = ℸ[p] with primitive
coproduct and action p = Q. The preceding construction gives a nonlocal algebra whose constant p
operation satisfies
                             Y (1#p, z)(J + #1) = J + #1 + J + #p,
whereas the reverse field has creation term ezT J + #p. Their constant terms differ by J + #1. At the
same time the J + , J − sector retains its double pole. Thus the ordered action interacts with the
singular sector through an actual derivation; it is not a tensor product in which the first factor is
inert.
   The noncommuting constant operation is a defect-type control. It cannot be identified without
comparison with a bulk-local raviolo field whose cohomological product has different commutativity
requirements. Nor can one apply the ordinary tensor bar to the vertex normally ordered product:
the latter is not the associative multiplication used in Chapters 1–2.




                                                       109
Chapter 25. All-state fields and independent exchange


25.4     The full two-constant Wick–Ore field family
The same construction realizes both of the original constant fields, not only the single charge action.
Work over R = ℸ[τ, η] with char ℸ = 0, take the Heisenberg cross pairing κ = τ 2 , and set

                                     V = Hτ 2 ⊗R R[v],           T v = 0.

The second factor is the commutative differential vertex algebra with zero translation. Let Q be the
charge derivation of the first factor. The operator

                                               δ = ηQ + η 2 ∂v

is a vertex derivation and commutes with T : each summand has these properties, and their coefficient
parameters are constant. Let the primitive generator u of the Hopf algebra R[u] act by δ. Its
iterated coproduct is the ordinary binomial coproduct, so the preceding all-state smash-product
construction applies over R without inverting τ or η.
   Its state space is the polynomial normal-symbol module on v, u and all Heisenberg creation
variables. In this ordering the generating fields are

               Ynew (u) = u + ηQ + η 2 ∂v ,          Ynew (v) = v,     Ynew (J ± , z) = J ± (z).

Here the plain symbols on the right mean multiplication operators. The Weyl relation and charge
action are actual commutators:

                      [Ynew (u), Ynew (v)] = η 2 ,      [Ynew (u), J ± (z)] = ±ηJ ± (z).

There is no scalar augmentation of the constant subalgebra over R killing u, v, since it would force
η 2 = 0 in R. The vacuum of this field algebra is not such an augmentation.
   Define a normal-ordering change on the polynomial state module by

                                              F = exp(η 2 ∂u ∂v ).

It is a finite sum on each state, has inverse with the negative exponent, fixes the vacuum and the
four generating states u, v, J + , J − , and commutes with translation. Transport the entire state-field
map, not just the generator formulas:

                                      Yold (a, z) = F −1 Ynew (F a, z)F.

Every state-field axiom already proved is preserved by this transport. The elementary commutators
with η 2 ∂u ∂v give
                            F −1 uF = u − η 2 ∂v ,   F −1 vF = v − η 2 ∂u .
The charge, creation and annihilation operators of the Heisenberg factor commute with F . Conse-
quently
               Yold (u) = u + ηQ,     Yold (v) = v − η 2 ∂u , Yold (J ± , z) = J ± (z).   (25.1)
These are the original coupled generating formulas, now extended to every state by a constructed
weakly associative field map. For example the constant product in this normal-symbol ordering has
u ∗ v = uv and v ∗ u = uv − η 2 . In a current-neutral pair the two charge actions cancel, while its
Heisenberg contraction remains τ 2 /(z − w)2 . Thus the ordered constant correction and singular
contraction coexist, without treating either as the other’s definition.

                                                       110
Chapter 25. All-state fields and independent exchange


    This is an actual isomorphism of two all-state nonlocal presentations. It does not identify the
field algebra with an associative Ran algebra. It also does not remove a collision lattice: F changes
the polynomial ordering of the constant symbols and has no separation denominator. The separate
meromorphic current contraction still changes a regular two-point lattice by the term τ 2 (z − w)−2 .
A generic meromorphic gauge which removes that term must transport the coproduct and lattice
too. Conflating these two different changes of presentation would reintroduce the original loss of
collision data.


25.5       Exchange needs its own data
Let V be finite-dimensional and R(u) an invertible exchange operator over a field of meromorphic
coefficients. Relations between distinct spectral labels are

                                   A1 (u)A2 (v) = R12 (u − v)A2 (v)A1 (u).

Fix a chamber order of the labels and orient the relations toward it. Rewriting terminates by
the number of inverted labels. Disjoint adjacent exchanges commute. The unique overlapping
three-label ambiguity is equality of

             R12 (u − v)R13 (u − w)R23 (v − w)            and   R23 (v − w)R13 (u − w)R12 (u − v).

Hence the spectral Yang–Baxter equation supplies confluence. If the relations are imposed in both
directions, the two-label loop also requires R21 (−u)R12 (u) = 1. Under these conditions the ordered
words with distinct labels are a basis: the terminating reductions define a normal-form map, and
the diamond check shows it is independent of choices. This does not address coincident-label limits,
which require separate extension data.
   For Rℏ (u) = 1 − ℏP/u with P the flip, direct multiplication gives

                                        Rℏ (u)Rℏ,21 (−u) = 1 − ℏ2 /u2 .

It is not unitary as written. The formal scalar factor f (u) = (1 − ℏ2 /u2 )−1/2 makes f (u)Rℏ (u)
unitary and does not change Yang–Baxter, since the same scalar product occurs on both sides. The
Yang–Baxter identity follows by expanding the three factors and using the transposition relations
and u − w = (u − v) + (v − w); the verification code checks its full rational matrix identity on a
two-dimensional test space, while the permutation-algebra argument gives the general identity.


25.6       The logarithmic connection
Let operators tij = tji satisfy [tij , tkl ] = 0 for disjoint pairs and [tij , tik + tjk ] = 0 for distinct triples.
The connection                                      X
                                         ∇=d−           tij d log(zi − zj )
                                                    i<j

is flat. The one-forms are closed. In the curvature, disjoint-pair terms vanish by the first relation.
Each three-label contribution is reduced by the second relation to a common commutator times the
Arnold relation among the three logarithmic wedges, which is zero. The Arnold identity itself is the
differential-form version of the elementary partial-fraction identity for three coordinate differences.


                                                          111
Chapter 25. All-state fields and independent exchange


   A quadratic Casimir from an invariant bilinear form supplies the infinitesimal braid relations
on tensor representations: invariance says the diagonal Lie action commutes with the two-tensor
Casimir, and expanding this commutator on three factors gives [t12 , t13 + t23 ] = 0. Thus flat
meromorphic transport supplies braid data independently of ordered multiplication.
   The ordered chamber of points on a real interval is contractible. Its E1 multiplication alone
has no braid generator. Interval reversal, reversal of a path of exchange, and Verdier duality are
different operations with different domains. An R-matrix must come from the exchange data just
supplied, not from the existence of an ordered word.




                                                 112
Chapter 26

Formal coordinates and descent to a
curve

A Laurent coefficient is not a coordinate-free field. Its transformation law is part of the object.
This chapter constructs the flat state bundle from the formal-coordinate action and calculates the
first nontrivial regular correction to a double pole. Neither a Riemann–Hilbert equivalence nor an
unsupported holonomicity assumption is needed for this formal descent.


26.1      The exact coordinate group
Let G = Aut ℸ[[t]] be the group of series g(t) = a1 t+a2 t2 +· · · , with a1 invertible, under composition.
We use composition of series as maps of the formal point. With that convention, the Lie bracket
of point-flow generators is the negative of the usual derivation bracket. Let d = ℸ[[t]]∂t carry the
usual derivation bracket; it also contains translation. A Harish–Chandra module in this chapter is a
complete vector space V , a continuous Lie action ρ : d → End(V ), and an action R of G for which
the differential along the point flow of v ∈ tℸ[[t]]∂t is −ρ(v) and

                                        R(g)ρ(v)R(g)−1 = ρ(g∗ v).

Every infinite series of operators is required to converge in the specified filtration of V ; for a graded
vertex module one may work weightwise and then take its declared completion. This is an input,
not a conclusion obtained from a finite-dimensional character.
   Let X be a smooth characteristic-zero curve. On an open coordinate chart write its coordinate
as z. A change to w = f (z) has a formal coordinate change at the moving point

                                         gz (t) = f (z + t) − f (z).

Its coefficients are regular on a chart where f ′ is invertible, and its linear coefficient is f ′ (z). Under
another change v = h(w),
                                         gh◦f,z = gh,f (z) ◦ gf,z .
This is the formal Taylor identity. It proves the cocycle law of the transition maps R(gz ).




                                                    113
Chapter 26. Formal coordinates and descent to a curve


26.2      The connection and its transition identity
In the z-trivialization put
                                             ∇z = dz − ρ(∂t ) dz.
The sign is fixed here; when ρ(∂t ) = −T , the formula is dz + T dz. The relation between local
representatives is vw = R(gz )vz .

Theorem 26.1 (Formal-coordinate descent). These transitions define a bundle or completed sheaf
V on X, and the displayed local connections glue to a flat connection. Hence they define a left
DX -module in the chosen complete setting. Tensoring with ωX converts it to a right DX -module.

Proof. The transition cocycle was verified above. It remains to check the connection rather than
infer it from that cocycle. Differentiate the formal change at fixed t:

                              ∂z gz (t) = f ′ (z + t) − f ′ (z) = ∂t gz (t) − f ′ (z).

Composing with gz−1 identifies its logarithmic derivative as a vector field in the new formal variable:

                                     (∂z gz ) ◦ gz−1 = (gz )∗ ∂t − f ′ (z)∂t .

The negative differential convention for the point-series action therefore gives

                       (∂z R(gz ))R(gz )−1 = −R(gz )ρ(∂t )R(gz )−1 + f ′ (z)ρ(∂t ).

Consequently
                              (∂z − f ′ (z)ρ(∂t ))R(gz ) = R(gz )(∂z − ρ(∂t )),
which is exactly the change-of-coordinate rule for the connection. On a curve its local curvature is
zero: the connection coefficient is constant in a coordinate trivialization and the wedge of its single
differential with itself is zero. The glued connection is therefore flat. A flat connection acts by vector
fields with their Leibniz rule and bracket relations, and hence gives the claimed differential-operator
action. The standard left/right conversion uses the dualizing one-form line and does not assert finite
rank or regular holonomicity of an infinite state module.

   The sign can be tested without a vertex algebra. Let ρ(v) = v act on formal functions as a
derivation and let R(g)F = F ◦ g −1 . Then R(g)ρ(v)R(g)−1 = ρ(g∗ v), its flow derivative is −ρ(v),
and the connection is dz − ∂t dz. This inverse-substitution representation satisfies the displayed
transition identity by direct differentiation. A positive differential for R combined with the same
pushforward convention would fail this test.
    This is the concrete local expression of Gelfand–Kazhdan descent used in the chiral formalism
[1, 18]. A coordinate-equivariant multilinear operation descends by the same cocycle identity. If
its source is a localization away from diagonals, the localization and the formal coordinate change
must commute on its actual pole domain; the pole calculation below makes that requirement
explicit. Descent preserves equations which already hold locally. It does not manufacture the Jacobi,
associative, or mixed equation for an unspecified operation.




                                                       114
Chapter 26. Formal coordinates and descent to a curve


26.3     The vertex-algebra representation of the coordinate pair
Suppose a conformal vertex algebra has integral L0 weights bounded below, with the usual lower-
truncation property, and the positive Ln act locally nilpotently in the corresponding weight direction.
For n ≥ −1, set
                                         ρ(tn+1 ∂t ) = −Ln .
The vector-field bracket is [tm+1 ∂t , tn+1 ∂t ] = (n − m)tm+n+1 ∂t . The Virasoro commutator has
central term zero on this subalgebra: a possible m + n = 0 with m, n ≥ −1 has m = 0, ±1, for which
m3 − m = 0. Thus the formula is a Lie action. The negative of this action integrates point flows: a
flow generated by tn+1 ∂t acts through exp(aLn ), and the scaling t 7→ at acts through aL0 . Local
nilpotence and integral weights make these the required prounipotent and multiplicative-group
actions. Compatibility follows by exponentiating the Lie adjoint identity. For completed modules
these operations are interpreted in their declared weightwise topology.
  The infinitesimal covariance of a state field is
                                      m+1
                                                 !
                                      X     m + 1 m+1−j
                  [Lm , Y (a, z)] =               z     Y (Lj−1 a, z),           m ≥ −1.
                                      j=0
                                              j

It follows directly from the mode commutator identity by inserting the conformal state. For m = −1
it is translation. For m = 0 it is the weight equation. For positive m its finite sum records every
lower mode needed by a nonlinear coordinate change. Exponentiating on finite weight windows
and using completeness gives the corresponding group covariance. The standard passage from
a vertex algebra with this covariance to its chiral operation on a curve is the formal-disc chiral
correspondence of [1]. It does not identify a nonlocal vertex algebra with an associative Ran algebra;
those are different input hypotheses and different operations.


26.4     Pole order is a triangular invariant
For a formal coordinate change w = f (z) with f (0) = 0 and f ′ (0) ̸= 0, write

                                  f (z) = a1 z(1 + b1 z + b2 z 2 + · · · ).

For each integer m ≥ 1,

                            f (z)−m = a−m
                                       1 z
                                           −m
                                              (1 + b1 z + b2 z 2 + · · · )−m .

The second factor has a unique formal power series expansion with constant term one. Its terms
contributing negative powers involve only the first m − 1 coefficients. Thus a pole of order m
transforms into a nonzero multiple of z −m plus lower-order poles and regular terms. The principal-
part filtration is preserved and its associated graded transforms by the appropriate power of the
normal line.
   The same statement near the diagonal of X 2 uses the normal separation variable and gives
grm OX 2 (∗∆) =∼ N ⊗m 2 for m ≥ 1, with the chosen convention for the increasing pole filtration.
                   ∆/X
Residues of one-forms and their higher normal jets are distinct projections. A logarithmic one-form
records one normal order; it cannot recover all higher poles without additional jets or an actual
bounded-pole reconstruction map. The triangular formula proves this before any particular field
theory is considered.

26.5      The double-pole correction and its cocycle
Define
                                                f ′ (z)f ′ (w)    1
                               Kf (z, w) =                     −       .
                                             (f (z) − f (w))2 (z − w)2
Let a = f ′ (w), b = f ′′ (w), c = f ′′′ (w), and δ = z − w. Expanding the numerator gives a2 + abδ +
acδ 2 /2 + O(δ 3 ). The denominator is a2 δ 2 (1 + (b/a)δ + (b2 /(4a2 ) + c/(3a))δ 2 + O(δ 3 )). Division
cancels both the pole and the possible simple pole, leaving
                                                                      ′′ 2 !
                                      c  b2   1           f ′′′ 3     f
                         Kf (w, w) =    − 2 =                  −                 (w).
                                     6a 4a    6           f′     2     f′

The expression in parentheses is the Schwarzian S(f ). Direct substitution of the two defining kernel
terms gives
                       Kh◦f (z, w) = Kf (z, w) + f ′ (z)f ′ (w)Kh (f (z), f (w)).
Taking the diagonal gives the complete cocycle identity

                                   S(h ◦ f ) = S(f ) + (f ′ )2 S(h) ◦ f.

Thus the regular normal-ordering correction satisfies a transition law; it is not an arbitrary scalar
subtraction at each point.
                                                        1
   For a free bosonic current with pairing κ and T = 2κ    : JJ :, transformation of the double-pole
contraction contributes half of Kf (w, w). Therefore its stress tensor obeys

                                                                 1
                                Tz (z) = f ′ (z)2 Tw (f (z)) +      S(f )(z).
                                                                 12
For a Virasoro field of central charge c, the same infinitesimal central term, integrated by the
Schwarzian cocycle, gives cS(f )/12. This determines its projective-connection transformation, not
an ordinary quadratic differential unless that correction has been subtracted using a chosen projective
connection. A genus-one or higher-genus calculation consequently needs a global connection or
propagator datum. A formal coordinate identity by itself does not choose one.


26.6      Why formal descent does not prove analytic sewing
Suppose a formal sewing expansion has weight-h coefficient bounded in norm by C h and the number
of summands in that weight is bounded by Dh . Then its sum converges absolutely for |q| < (CD)−1 ,
by the geometric-series bound. This is a sufficient analytic hypothesis, independent of formal
covariance. A formal series can fail every such estimate; an algebraic identity of its coefficients does
not imply convergence on a punctured analytic disc.
   The coordinate descent proved here supplies actual transition maps and a flat state connection.
The cluster chapter supplies maps in common completed pole domains. An analytic conformal-block
sewing theorem needs both the relevant state operations and estimates of its coefficients, or a
separately cited convergence theorem under its hypotheses. Neither formal descent nor tensor-bar
reconstruction is used to suppress that distinction.




                                                   116
Chapter 27

Exchange, spectral parameters, and
exact reconstruction

The configuration space of increasing points on a real interval is contractible. Its multiplication
orders inputs but supplies no braid generator. Exchange requires a different parameter space or
an independently constructed transport. We now construct such transports and the algebra they
recognize. The formal spectral parameter, the geometric separation coordinate, and a deformation
parameter are kept distinct.


27.1      The infinitesimal braid relations
Let g be a finite-dimensional Lie algebra with a nondegenerate invariant symmetric form. Choose
dual bases xa , xa and put Ω = a xa ⊗ xa . In U (g)⊗n write Ωij for this tensor in positions i, j.
                                 P

Symmetry gives Ωij = Ωji . For four distinct indices, [Ωij , Ωkl ] = 0 because the factors are disjoint.
Invariance of the form gives
                                       [Ωij , Ωik + Ωjk ] = 0.                                   (27.1)
Indeed Ω is invariant under the diagonal adjoint action: pairing either factor with a vector reduces
the assertion to ⟨[a, b], c⟩ + ⟨b, [a, c]⟩ = 0. In the three tensor positions, invariance under each basis
element of the third position is exactly (27.1) after summing with its dual.
   On distinct complex coordinates set ωij = d log(zi − zj ). A direct calculation gives
                                 ωij ∧ ωjk + ωjk ∧ ωki + ωki ∧ ωij = 0.
For example take a = zi − zj , b = zj − zk , so zi − zk = a + b. All terms are multiples of da ∧ db, and
their coefficient is 1/(ab) − 1/(b(a + b)) − 1/(a(a + b)) = 0. Consequently
                                                      X
                                          ∇=d−λ             Ωij ωij
                                                      i<j

is flat: dωij = 0; disjoint commutators vanish; the three terms for each triple cancel by (27.1) and
the displayed differential-form identity. This is the KZ flatness calculation, with its coefficient
relation and its geometric relation both present.
   The residues also give commuting Gaudin operators
                                                  X      Ωij
                                           Hi =              .
                                                      z − zj
                                                  j̸=i i


                                                   117
Chapter 27. Exchange, spectral parameters, and exact reconstruction


In [Hi , Hj ], disjoint terms vanish. For each third index k, use (27.1) to express all three commutators
by one and use the same partial-fraction identity to cancel its coefficient. Thus [Hi , Hj ] = 0. This
does not identify their joint spectrum with the spectrum of another operator without an additional
representation-theoretic comparison.


27.2      A complete nilpotent transport
For three ordered complex points write
                                                                       z3 − z1
                                  z = z1 ,   t = z 2 − z1 ,      u=            .
                                                                       z2 − z1

The configuration is C × C× × (C \ {0, 1}). Put σ = dt/t, α = du/u, β = du/(u − 1). On a
finite-dimensional vector space let R0 , R1 , R2 strictly lower a finite filtration and satisfy [R0 , R1 ] =
[R0 , R2 ] = 0. Then ∇ = d−(R0 σ+R1 α+R2 β) is flat. The wedge α∧β is zero on the one-dimensional
u-factor; the other curvature terms are the two commutators just assumed.
   The finite algebra
                                 E = Λ(σ) ⊗ (ℸ ⊕ ℸα ⊕ ℸβ),               αβ = 0,
with this coefficient connection computes the algebraic de Rham complex of the displayed product.
For scalar coefficients, partial fractions show that every rational one-form on the punctured line
is an exact rational form plus a linear combination of du/u and du/(u − 1); on Gm only dt/t
remains. Their products give the tensor-product result. Filtering the coefficient space by the
strictly lowered finite filtration reduces the coefficient comparison to that scalar calculation on
every associated-graded piece. Finite induction proves the assertion without an infinite convergence
hypothesis.
   For a path γ, parallel transport is the finite Chen series
                                      XZ
                           Hγ = 1 +                           A(γ̇(sr )) · · · A(γ̇(s1 )).
                                      r≥1 0<s1 <···<sr <1

Products of sufficiently many residues are zero, so the sum really is finite. Differentiating its
endpoint verifies H ′ = AH and H(0) = 1. The same recursion proves uniqueness. Splitting the
simplex at the concatenation point proves the product law for paths. Varying a path with fixed
endpoints differentiates the transport by an integral of the curvature, which vanishes; hence the
result depends only on its endpoint-fixed homotopy class. The tensor-product connection has
coefficient A ⊗ 1 + 1 ⊗ A, so uniqueness also gives tensor compatibility of the transports.


27.3      A based monodromy is not a bare residue exponential
Take R0 = 0, R1 = E12 , R2 = E23 and base point b ∈ (0, 1). Since every product of three residues
vanishes, the solution on (0, 1) is
                                             1−u                                                
      H(u) = 1 + E12 log(u/b) + E23 log          + E13 − Li2 (u) + Li2 (b) − log(1 − b) log(u/b) .
                                             1−b
Its derivative is the matrix product AH: the E13 coefficient differentiates to (log(1−u)−log(1−b))/u,
the required iterated integral. With c = 2πi, the positively oriented meridians transported to b have

                                                     118
Chapter 27. Exchange, spectral parameters, and exact reconstruction


monodromies

                  G0 = 1 + cE12 − c log(1 − b)E13 ,       G1 = 1 + cE23 + c log bE13 .

Around zero, only log u changes. Around one, log(1 − u) changes and continuation of Li2 changes
by −2πi log u. Evaluating the continued solution back at b gives these matrices. Thus in the chosen
based frame they are not exp(cRi ).
  The distinction is a marking, not an absolute impossibility of conjugating the pair. The single
matrix
                              P = exp(− log bE12 − log(1 − b)E23 )
conjugates G0 , G1 to exp(cE12 ), exp(cE23 ) respectively. All nested commutators beyond the first
vanish because E13 is central in this nilpotent matrix algebra. This proves the simultaneous
conjugation directly.


27.4      The associator coefficient
Let X, Y lie in an ideal J of a unital complex algebra with J 3 = 0. For F ′ = (X/u + Y /(u − 1))F ,
normalize solutions F0 ∼ uX and F1 ∼ (1 − u)Y . Their first-order terms agree. The XY and Y X
coefficients of F0 are − Li2 (u) and ζ(2) − Li2 (1 − u); those of F1 are ζ(2) − Li2 (u) and − Li2 (1 − u).
These follow by integrating log(1 − u)/u and log u/(u − 1), with the stated endpoint constants.
Hence
                                     Φ = F1−1 F0 = 1 − ζ(2)[X, Y ].
With X = A/(2πi) and Y = B/(2πi), this is 1 + [A, B]/24. It is a comparison between two
normalized solutions; it is not an operation inferred from a loss of dimensions under symmetrization.


27.5      The rational Yang operator and unitary normalization
On V ⊗ V , let P be the flip and set Rh (u) = 1 − hP/u. Expanding

                         R12 (u)R13 (u + v)R23 (v) = R23 (v)R13 (u + v)R12 (u)

in powers of h proves the spectral Yang–Baxter identity. The linear terms coincide. The quadratic
terms coincide by the permutation identities together with 1/(uv) = 1/(u(u + v)) + 1/(v(u + v)).
The cubic terms coincide because P12 P13 P23 = P23 P13 P12 . These exhaust the expansion.
   Its inverse is not R−h (u):

                 Rh (u)R−h (u) = 1 − h2 /u2 ,      Rh (u)−1 = (1 − h2 /u2 )−1 R−h (u).

An explicit unitary normalization is
                                                      u − hP
                                           Sh (u) =          .
                                                       u−h
The scalar normalization preserves Yang–Baxter, and Sh21 (−u)Sh (u) = 1. These are rational
identities where their denominators are invertible, or identities in a stated h/u completion. One
cannot specialize at a pole without new extension data.
   For distinct spectral labels the exchange rule A1 (u)A2 (v) = S12 (u − v)A2 (v)A1 (u) can be oriented
toward a chosen ordering of labels. Each reduction lowers the inversion count. Disjoint exchanges

                                                  119
Chapter 27. Exchange, spectral parameters, and exact reconstruction


commute. The sole three-letter ambiguity is Yang–Baxter; the reverse two-letter rule is consistent
by unitarity. Thus the normal ordered coefficient tensors form a basis. Conversely, equality of the
two reductions as operators on the free three-label coefficient space is exactly Yang–Baxter. The
distinct-label qualification excludes diagonal specializations and repeated-label relations, which
require a separate analysis.

Theorem 27.1 (RTT PBW by separating evaluations). For the increasing filtration deg tij = r − 1,

                                          gr Y (glN ) ∼

                                                                (1)    (s)          (s)      (s)
Proof. Coefficient comparison in (27.2) first gives [tij , tkl ] = δjk til − δil tkj . The coefficient
recurrence then expresses [t(r) , t(s) ] as the same linear term of mode r + s − 1 plus products of
filtration degree at most r + s − 3. Induction on r gives this assertion directly from the recurrence,
starting with r = 1. Thus the symbols satisfy the current Lie relations, giving a surjection
                                                                              (r)

It remains to prove injectivity, rather than infer it from the defining relations.
   Compose the iterated coproduct with evaluations at independent variables a1 , . . . , am . The image
of T (u) is the ordered product m            [j]

                                                                               −r has leading total

a-degree r − 1 equal to j aj E . Terms using k ≥ 2 matrix factors have total a-degree r − k,
strictly lower. Consequently the associated-graded evaluation of the current generator is exactly
                                                      X        [j]

These current enveloping evaluations are jointly injective. To see this, take a nonzero element
involving modes at most s0 and take m ≥ s0 + 1. Over the rational function field ℸ(a1 , . . . , am ),
the Vandermonde matrix (asj )0≤s≤s0 has independent rows. Thus evaluation is injective on the
finite-dimensional span of these current modes. Passing to the top PBW symbol of the nonzero
element gives an injective map on the corresponding symmetric algebra, by the ordinary PBW
theorem for Lie algebras. Its image cannot vanish. This proves joint injectivity and therefore
injectivity of the displayed surjection.
    Ordered PBW monomials in the symbols give a basis of the associated graded. Their lifts span
the filtered algebra by induction on filtration, including filtration degree zero, where they are the
ordinary U (glN ) PBW monomials. They are independent because a finite dependence has a highest
filtration part and its PBW symbols are independent. This proves the stated basis.

   The general Yangian context and these evaluation methods are classical [19]. The proof fixes the
normalization needed here. At N = 1 all generators commute, and the theorem gives a polynomial
algebra on the t(r) ; its augmentation Tor is the exterior algebra on their suspensions. For general N ,
a loop-filtration spectral sequence needs its own convergence statement, since mode t0 has filtration
degree zero and bar length is not bounded by loop degree. PBW alone does not prove a completed
Yangian/bar identification or a chiral Koszul self-duality.




                                                  121
Chapter 28

Extension flags and cyclic words

A denominator product is a useful check after an algebra has been constructed. It is a poor substitute
for the construction. The two examples in this chapter begin with objects and maps that can be
counted or differentiated. The Hall algebra begins with subrepresentations. The necklace algebra
begins with cyclic words and their matrix evaluations. Neither begins with a desired quantum group
or a desired bulk spectrum.


28.1     The category and every isomorphism class
Fix a finite field Fq . A representation of the quiver 1 → 2 is a linear map f : V1 → V2 . Two such
maps are isomorphic precisely when their dimensions and ranks agree: choose bases of the kernel,
image, and complements to put the matrix in the form diag(Ir , 0). Thus every object has a unique
decomposition
                                                                        1
           M ≃ S1⊕c ⊕ E ⊕b ⊕ S2⊕a ,             S1 = (Fq → 0), E = (Fq −
                                                                       → Fq ), S2 = (0 → Fq ).

Here b = rank f , c = dim ker f , and a = dim coker f .
   Let Hq be the rational vector space with basis the isomorphism classes [M ]. Define
                              X
                                     L            L
                 [M ][N ] =         FM N [L],    FM N = #{U ⊂ L : U ≃ N, L/U ≃ M }.
                              [L]

Each sum is finite: its dimension vector is fixed, and the preceding rank classification leaves finitely
many possible L. This finiteness is established before forming the product.

Proposition 28.1 (Associativity by one flag). This multiplication is associative and has unit [0].

Proof. The coefficient of [L] in ([M ][N ])[P ] counts flags U ⊂ V ⊂ L with U ≃ P , V /U ≃ N , and
L/V ≃ M : first choose U , then the subrepresentation V /U of L/U . The coefficient in [M ]([N ][P ])
counts the same flags by first choosing V , then U ⊂ V . These two finite sets are identified by the
identity on a flag, including its actual inclusions. The only subobject with zero quotient or zero
underlying object is the evident one, proving the unit laws.




                                                       122
Chapter 28. Extension flags and cyclic words


28.2      Three products determine the algebra
Put u1 = [S1 ], u2 = [S2 ], and w = [E]. The exact relations are

                         u1 u2 = u2 u1 + w,          u1 w = qwu1 ,         wu2 = qu2 w.            (28.1)

For the first relation, the objects of dimension (1, 1) have rank zero or one. Both have one copy of
S2 as subobject with quotient S1 . Only the rank-zero object has an S1 subobject with quotient S2 .
For u1 w, the only possible object is S1 ⊕ E, with a surjection F2q → Fq . Its E subobjects correspond
to the q lines complementary to the kernel. In the reverse product, the kernel is the unique required
S1 subobject. Dually, in E ⊕ S2 , a complement to the image gives q choices for the S2 subobject
with quotient E, whereas the image gives the unique E subobject with quotient S2 . This proves all
coefficients in (28.1).
                                                     Qr
   Write [r]q = 1 + q + · · · + q r−1 and [r]q ! =     j=1 [j]q , with [0]q ! = 1.

Theorem 28.2 (A complete Hall basis). The algebra generated by u1 , u2 , w with relations (28.1) is
Hq . Its basis and its image in the representation basis are

                               ua2 wb uc1 = [a]q ![b]q ![c]q ! [S2⊕a ⊕ E ⊕b ⊕ S1⊕c ].

In particular it is generated by u1 , u2 alone.

Proof. For any of the three indecomposables, a flag with all simple factors equal to that indecom-
posable is a complete flag in its multiplicity space. Counting its first line and continuing on the
quotient gives [r]q !. In the product [S2a ][E b ], the subobject of type E b must have all of the source
and precisely the image in the target; it is unique. The extension is split: a complement to that
image identifies the quotient with S2a . In [S2a ⊕ E b ][S1c ], the S1c subobject must be the full kernel,
again unique. Choosing a vector-space complement to that kernel shows that the middle object
is the stated direct sum. These observations give the displayed coefficient and show that every
representation basis element is in the generated algebra.
   For an independent presentation check, orient the relations to move u2 left, u1 right, and w
between them. A reduction of u1 u2 has a same-length ordered term and a shorter term w. At fixed
length each exchange lowers inversions. Termination follows. The only overlapping inverted triple is
u1 wu2 . Reducing either side first gives

                                               q 2 u2 wu1 + qw2 .

Disjoint reductions commute. The least-unresolved-word argument therefore proves that ordered
monomials span the presented algebra with unique normal forms. Alternatively, their just computed
images are nonzero multiples of pairwise distinct representation basis elements, proving linear
independence directly and also proving that there are no extra relations. Finally w = u1 u2 −u2 u1 .


28.3      The Serre coefficients and the twist
Eliminate w from u1 w = qwu1 to obtain

                                   u21 u2 − (q + 1)u1 u2 u1 + qu2 u21 = 0.


                                                       123
Chapter 28. Extension flags and cyclic words


Eliminating it from wu2 = qu2 w gives the companion relation

                                   u1 u22 − (q + 1)u2 u1 u2 + qu22 u1 = 0.

These are identities of counted flags, not conjectural comparisons of Hilbert series.
   Let v 2 = q and extend scalars to Q(v). For dimension vectors define

                ⟨(a, b), (c, d)⟩ = ac + bd − ad,                 [M ] ∗ [N ] = v ⟨dim M,dim N ⟩ [M ][N ].

Bilinearity of the exponent makes the twist associative: the two threefold exponents are both
⟨M, N ⟩ + ⟨M, P ⟩ + ⟨N, P ⟩. The exponents for the words 112, 121, 211 are respectively −1, 0, 1.
Consequently the first relation becomes

                     E1 ∗ E1 ∗ E2 − (v + v −1 )E1 ∗ E2 ∗ E1 + E2 ∗ E1 ∗ E1 = 0,

with the second relation obtained similarly. The resulting presentation is the positive A2 quantum-
group presentation, in this explicit normalization. The general Hall/quantum-group comparison is
Ringel’s theorem [21]; no general comparison is required for the presentation just proved.
   The grading gives the Hilbert series
                              X                                          1
                                      ta2 (t1 t2 )b tc1 =                                  .
                            a,b,c≥0
                                                            (1 − t1 )(1 − t1 t2 )(1 − t2 )

The basis proves the series. Reading the reasoning backward from the series alone would not
construct the extension term w or its two q-commutation laws. A geometric Hall theory for a
Calabi–Yau category also needs its actual correspondences, properness conditions, orientation data
and coefficient functors. This finite example supplies none of those by analogy.


28.4     Cyclic differentiation
Let F = ℸ⟨x, y⟩ and let Fcyc = F/[F, F ] be its cyclic vector-space quotient; [F, F ] here is the linear
span of commutators, not the two-sided commutator ideal. A cyclic word is a necklace. Define
                                                            X
                              ∂xcyc [a1 · · · ad ] =             ai+1 · · · ad a1 · · · ai−1 ,
                                                       i:ai =x

and similarly for y. Rotating a word merely rotates the indexing of its occurrences, so the formula
is well defined in F . Put
                               {f, g}cyc = [∂xcyc f ∂ycyc g − ∂ycyc f ∂xcyc g].               (28.2)
Skew symmetry follows from [ab] = [ba]. We prove Jacobi without suppressing the cyclic combina-
torics.
   On pairs of N × N matrices take the coordinate Poisson bracket

                          {Xij , Ykl } = δil δjk ,          {Xij , Xkl } = {Yij , Ykl } = 0.

For a cyclic word, differentiating each matrix occurrence gives

                                 d Tr f = Tr (∂xcyc f )dX + (∂ycyc f )dY .
                                                                                        


Contracting these derivatives by the coordinate bracket proves

                                           {Tr f, Tr g} = Tr{f, g}cyc .                                     (28.3)

                                                             124
Chapter 28. Extension flags and cyclic words


Lemma 28.3 (Joint trace detection). The maps Fcyc → ℸ[Mat2N ] given by trace, for all positive N ,
are jointly injective in characteristic zero.

Proof. Separate word lengths by polynomial degree. For a nonempty word w = a1 · · · ad , take N = d
and the monomial obtained from the directed cycle 1 → 2 → · · · → d → 1, using an X-entry or a
Y -entry on its ith edge according to ai . A trace word of degree d contains this monomial exactly
when its necklace is that of w: any contributing closed traversal must follow the directed cycle. The
coefficient is a positive integer accounting for starting vertices, hence nonzero. This isolates each
necklace in a finite linear combination. The empty word has trace N , so its constant coefficient is
also detected. For d = 1 the same argument uses a diagonal entry.

Theorem 28.4 (The necklace Lie algebra and its trace action). Equation (28.2) is a Lie bracket,
and every matrix trace is a Lie map to the displayed Poisson algebra.

Proof. The Lie-map calculation was made on actual coordinates in (28.3). The coordinate bracket
satisfies Jacobi because it is a constant skew biderivation: on three coordinate generators every
nested bracket is zero, and its Jacobiator is a derivation in each variable. Therefore the trace of the
necklace Jacobiator is zero for every N . Joint trace detection makes the Jacobiator itself zero. This
argument establishes the cyclic identity, not just its value in one matrix rank.

   This is the free two-generator case of noncommutative symplectic geometry [22]. In particular
the cyclic quotient is not being supplied with the associative product of F , which generally does not
descend to a product on that quotient.


28.5     Diagonal matrix tests and the first lost relation
On a diagonal pair write
                                          N
                                          X
                                  pab =         xai yib ,         {xi , yj } = δij .
                                          i=1

Direct differentiation gives
                                 {pab , pcd } = (ad − bc)pa+c−1,b+d−1 .
Terms with zero coefficient are zero even when a displayed exponent would be negative. For one
pair, A = x2 , B = xy, C = y 2 satisfy

                     B 2 = AC,    {A, B} = 2A,               {B, C} = 2C,         {A, C} = 4B.

For two pairs the same three sums instead satisfy

                                    B 2 − AC = −(x1 y2 − x2 y1 )2 .

Thus the one-particle equation does not become a relation in the full stable trace algebra. A
restriction to a special locus must retain its kernel.

Chapter 28. Extension flags and cyclic words


28.6     A derived trace map before reduction
There is a separate chain-level construction. Let

                           Q = T (x, y, ψ),     |ψ| = −1,   dψ = xy − yx.

The augmentation Q → ℸ[x, y] is a quasi-isomorphism. Here is a direct proof. Replace each
occurrence of ψ in a word by the placeholder xy. Filter by expanded length and then by the number
of x-before-y inversions. The leading differential replaces ψ by xy; the other term replaces it by
yx and lowers inversions. Occurrences of the leading word xy never overlap with themselves. For
a fixed expanded word the choices of which occurrences are marked ψ form a tensor product of
two-term identity complexes. If there is any such occurrence the summand contracts; otherwise the
word is y a xb . The increasing exhaustive filtration has finite depth below each expanded word. Its
associated graded augmentation is therefore a quasi-isomorphism, and the filtration lemma proves
the assertion.
  For rank N set

                       RN = ℸ[Xij , Yij ] ⊗ Λ(Ψij ),    dΨij = (XY − Y X)ij .

Substitution of matrices is a dg algebra map from Q to MatN (RN ). Taking the graded cyclic trace
gives a chain map Qcyc → RN , because trace kills graded commutators. In particular

                              d Tr(ΨV U ) = Tr(U XY V ) − Tr(U Y XV )

for ordinary words U, V . This is an explicit homotopy for exchanging adjacent matrices inside a
trace. It proves independence of an ordered lift in degree-zero derived commuting invariants.
   The scalar coordinate Poisson bracket on X, Y and this derived trace complex are not automati-
cally one dg Poisson structure on all ghosts. Extending the bracket to a full gauge or BV complex
requires the ghost partners and their actual Hamiltonian. The chain map above is retained without
claiming that it is the missing native bulk–boundary equivalence. In particular, the class Tr Ψ may
survive even when the classical matrix commutator vanishes; deleting the ghost direction is not
derived reduction.

Chapter 29

Closing intervals without changing the
theory

An open algebra can be noncommutative. A closed oriented two-dimensional state algebra is
commutative. A matrix trace is a cyclic functional on the open algebra, not an augmentation and
not by itself a prescription for the closed handle. These distinctions are visible in a finite-dimensional
model in which all sewing maps can be written in matrix units.


29.1      A Frobenius pairing and its adjoint coproduct
Let B be a finite-dimensional unital algebra with a symmetric nondegenerate functional τB : τB (ab) =
τB (ba) and ⟨a, b⟩ = τB (ab) is nondegenerate. Let ei , ei be dual bases, so τB (ei ej ) = δij . The Casimir
tensor i ei ⊗ ei is independent of the basis. The coproduct adjoint to multiplication is specified,
        P

up to the fixed tensor-pairing convention, by this tensor and the identity
                                           X               X
                                 (b ⊗ 1)       ei ⊗ ei =       ei ⊗ ei (1 ⊗ b).
                                           i               i

Both expressions pair equally with arbitrary a⊗c, by associativity and trace cyclicity; nondegeneracy
proves the identity. It is the Frobenius compatibility needed to move a cut past a multiplication. It
does not imply that B is commutative.
   For a commutative Frobenius algebra (C, τC ), let hC =
                                                                        P       i
                                                                          i ci c be its handle element.    The
connected genus-g closed amplitude is
                                                Zg = τC (hgC ).                                           (29.1)
The exponent is calibrated at g = 0: the sphere is τC (1). Cutting a handle inserts a multiplication
after its adjoint coproduct and therefore multiplies the remaining state by hC . Induction proves
(29.1). The argument uses the closed state algebra C, not an arbitrary open algebra B.

Suppose that an open–closed pair has a unital map i : C → Z(B) and its adjoint i∗ : B → C,
characterized by

relation. The standard algebraic presentation of oriented open–closed sewing uses associativity,
the two Frobenius identities, centrality of i, the adjunction, and (29.2); its identification with the
bordism presentation is a foundational sewing input [20]. All these algebraic identities will be
checked in the example, rather than hidden in that input.
   Take B = Matn (ℸ) and τB = λ Tr, with λ ̸= 0. The dual to Eij is λ−1 Eji . Hence

Proof. Associativity and the unit are componentwise matrix identities. The pairing on each matrix

29.3     A second genus is independent data
A rank-one closed Frobenius algebra with trace τ (1) = µ, µ ̸= 0, has amplitudes µ1−g . Pairing this
theory with the preceding open–closed theory gives, on a product of two separately specified sewing
parameter systems, the coefficient            X
                                        µ1−gX     λ2−2g
                                                    α
                                                        ⊥
                                                          .
                                                  α

The formula is a tensor-product construction. It neither identifies gX with g⊥ nor constructs a chiral
sewing theory on moduli of curves. That latter theory must supply its conformal blocks, sewing
maps and convergence separately.
   There is a useful chain-level identity behind such products. Let DX , D⊥ be odd differentials
on complexes V, W . On V ⊗ W put DX = DV ⊗ 1 and D⊥ = 1 ⊗ DW , evaluated with the Koszul
tensor rule. Then
                                       DX D⊥ + D⊥ DX = 0.
On v ⊗ w, the two terms have signs (−1)|v| and (−1)|v|+1 . This explains the mixed cancellation
for independent edge colours. For interacting vertices the two compositions must first be shown
to agree before orientation signs are inserted. An orientation cannot make two unequal coefficient
maps equal.


29.4     A complete mixed cut identity
Let a finite rooted graph have a set E of contractible internal edges. Suppose a coefficient object
VG is supplied for every contraction G/E ′ , and for two edges the coefficient contractions commute
under the canonical identification of the twice-contracted graph. Give a summand the orientation
line det(ℸE). Define d by contracting one edge and removing it from this exterior determinant.

Proposition 29.2 (Every two-edge cancellation). The resulting signed contraction operator squares
to zero. For a partition E = EX ⊔ E⊥ , its two pieces separately square to zero and anticommute.

Proof. Choose an ordering of E solely to compute. A term of d2 is indexed by an ordered pair of
distinct edges. If their positions are i < j, removing i then j has sign (−1)i−1+j−2 , while removing
j then i has sign (−1)j−1+i−1 . These signs are opposite. The coefficients agree by the supplied
commuting contraction square. Pairing the ordered pairs cancels every term. Restricting to pairs of
a fixed colour proves the two squares; pairs of opposite colours prove anticommutation. A change of
edge ordering multiplies the oriented basis by its determinant sign and changes neither assertion.

   The proposition is an all-graph statement under an explicit coefficient hypothesis. It is not a
proof that a desired mixed graph theory supplies that hypothesis. In particular, a claimed all-genus
obstruction class κλg must first exist in its stated complex; the determinant argument does not
manufacture that class or show that its nonscalar part vanishes.


29.5     BRST and bar with the augmentation retained
Let (A, d) be an augmented dg algebra and let Q be an odd derivation with Q2 = 0, dQ + Qd = 0,
and εQ = 0. Then d + Q is a differential preserving the augmentation. Applying the already


                                                 129
Chapter 29. Closing intervals without changing the theory


constructed bar functor gives

                    B(A, d + Q) = T c (sA), bd + bQ + b2 ,
                                                            
                                                                 bQ (sa) = −sQa.

All pairwise anticommutators vanish: those involving unary maps follow from the hypotheses;
bQ b2 + b2 bQ = 0 is exactly the derivation identity for Q with the suspended tensor signs. Hence this
is an actual bicomplex totalization, not an analogy with a BRST differential.
    For a second grading which makes the two differentials a first-quadrant double complex in
each finite positive weight, its spectral sequence is strongly convergent in that weight. The first
page computes the unary differential, leaving the bar multiplication induced on its cohomology.
No unconditional interchange with an infinite completed totalization is asserted. An exhaustive
bounded-below filtration admits the finite descending correction proof; a decreasing complete
filtration needs its completeness and convergence arguments. The two are different permissions.
   If an ordinary dg algebra A has cohomology only in degree zero, there is a genuine formality
zigzag of dg algebras
                                    A ←− τ≤0 A −→ H 0 (A).
Here (τ≤0 A)0 = ker(d : A0 → A1 ), its negative terms are those of A, and its positive terms are zero.
Multiplication restricts; the left map is a quasi-isomorphism because positive cohomology vanishes,
and the right one because negative cohomology vanishes. If the augmentation is compatible, the bar
functor carries this zigzag to a quasi-isomorphism. This proves the appropriate comparison without
postulating a canonical direct chain map H 0 (A) → A.
    A Drinfeld–Sokolov complex must still be checked to have the required operations, augmentation
or chosen relative module, grading, and cohomological concentration. In particular a nonzero-level
affine vacuum is not a scalar algebra augmentation merely because it is a cyclic module. The
bar–BRST identity does not supply that missing augmentation.

Heisenberg fields. Wick contractions have two legs at each vertex and no self-contractions. Their
graphs are disjoint cycles. A two-vertex double edge has two matchings and the factor 1/4 from the
two stress tensors, giving N/2. A cycle with at least three vertices has 2r leg matchings, canceled
by the 2−r normalization, and a common field label with N choices. It has two orientations, so it
is the sum of the two directed-cycle contributions N/2 each. Disconnected cycles multiply their
weights. This gives exactly the derangement formula.
   The free-field stress tensor has the stated OPE and satisfies the Ward recursion, so this is the
same vacuum function for every positive integer N . Both the recursion result and the displayed
expression are polynomials in c with rational-function coefficients in the coordinates. Equality at
infinitely many integers proves the polynomial identity in characteristic zero, hence at every c. The
decomposition by graph connected components is precisely the moment–cumulant decomposition,
so the connected part is the single-cycle sum.

30.3      The complete five- and six-point organization
For five labelled vertices, fix vertex 1 as the start of a cycle and identify reversals. The twelve cycles
are

                           12345,
                           12354,        12435,        12453,      12534,       12543,
                           13245,        13254,        13425,      13524,       14235,        14325.


                                                               132
Chapter 30. Conformal calculations before scalar projections

                    Q                  −2
Each contributes c {i,j} in its cycle zij to the connected function. The remaining part consists of
the ten choices of a pair and its complementary triangle, each with coefficient c2 /2 and its indicated
edge denominator. This is all of W5 .
   For six vertices the connected part has sixty unoriented six-cycles, each weighted c. The
disconnected types are: forty-five pair–four-cycle graphs weighted c2 /2; ten pairs of triangles
weighted c2 ; and fifteen perfect matchings weighted c3 /8. These counts follow by choosing the
pair or partition and then its unoriented cycle, and sum to the derangement decomposition after
orientations are restored. The supplied enumeration code generates every graph without identifying
two inequivalent labelings.
   The formulas are rational in the marked coordinates but polynomial in c. Consequently a proposed
quantity with poles at 5c + 22 = 0 cannot simply be this raw connected five-point function. An
inverse-Gram contraction, a residue integral, a normalization or another projection could introduce
such poles, but the functional and its domain would have to be stated and evaluated. The all-arity
correlation formula does not validate an unspecified projected scalar tower.


30.4      What central-charge reflection proves
The affine scalar c(k) = k dim g/(k + h∨ ) satisfies
                                    c(k) + c(−k − 2h∨ ) = 2 dim g.
This is direct addition of rational functions. For the Virasoro expression c = 13 − 6(t + t−1 ), replacing
t by −t gives a sum 26. For the principal type-A expression
                             cN (t) = 2N 3 − N − 1 − N (N 2 − 1)(t + t−1 ),
                                           PN
the sum is 4N 3 − 2N − 2, also equal to      j=2 2(6j
                                                        2 − 6j + 1) by elementary summation.

   These identities do not identify a reflected-level algebra with an augmentation Koszul dual.
They compare scalars after a parameter transformation. A derived identification requires a map
of the relevant coefficient algebras, their completions and their module or diagonal resolutions.
Ghost-anomaly cancellation, determinant-line curvature and the Euler characteristic of a bar are
also values of different constructions. Equality of their scalar values alone is not a comparison
theorem.


30.5      Two sewing directions require two sets of maps
The associative bar and Hochschild chains describe an interval and a cyclic closure in their own
monoidal category. A conformal-block sewing construction on families of algebraic curves has a
different base, boundary strata and gluing maps. A two-colour graph complex can combine two
constructions only after the coefficient maps for its edge contractions have been supplied.
   The useful elementary lemma is conditional but exact: if every codimension-two contraction
square commutes before orientations, orient the two edge directions by the determinant of their
ordered edge set. The two paths to a codimension-two face then have opposite signs, so their
differentials anticommute. This proves the square-zero total differential for those supplied maps.
Orientation alone does not prove the coefficient square commutes, and a stratification does not assign
the coefficient maps. The distinction prevents a formal two-genus equation from being mistaken for
a constructed sewing theory.

                                                   133
Chapter 31

The complete Virasoro conformal
cohomology calculation

The degrees 0, 2, 3 appeared repeatedly in the original programme. They have an exact home: the
reduced cohomology of the centreless Virasoro Lie conformal algebra with trivial coefficients. They
are not, on the strength of this calculation, the amplitude of an unspecified chiral Hochschild centre.
We calculate both the basic and reduced complexes, including the contraction which eliminates
every other polynomial degree. This is the classical calculation of Bakalov–Kac–Voronov [17], with
the signs and the degree-zero exception in the short exact sequence made explicit.


31.1      The complex is a polynomial operation space
Let Vir = ℸ[T ]L with [Lλ L] = (T + 2λ)L. The module ℸ is trivial: L and T act by zero. A basic
conformal n-cochain is determined by its value on L⊗n , an alternating polynomial

                                     P (λ1 , . . . , λn ) ∈ ℸ[λ1 , . . . , λn ]alt .

Insertion of T L in the ith slot multiplies the value by −λi . Thus specifying this polynomial specifies
the entire conformally antilinear cochain on the free ℸ[T ]-module. Set Ce 0 = ℸ.
   The trivial coefficient action removes the one-input action terms in the differential. Evaluating
the bracket in a merged slot replaces T + 2λi by −(λi + λj ) + 2λi = λi − λj . With the merged
argument put first, the exact differential is
                                                  X
                 (dP )(λ1 , . . . , λn+1 ) =                (−1)i+j (λi − λj )
                                               1≤i<j≤n+1                                                        (31.1)
                                                 · P (λi + λj , λ1 , . . . , λ
                                                                             bi, . . . , b
                                                                                         λj , . . . , λn+1 ).

In degree zero the sum is empty, so d : Ce 0 → Ce 1 is zero. The differential preserves alternating
symmetry. Its square vanishes: terms involving two disjoint merged pairs cancel in opposite orders
with their exterior signs; the three ways of merging three arguments cancel by conformal Jacobi for
(T + 2λ)L. These are all terms. More explicitly, after sesquilinearity every triple contribution is the
coefficient of the Jacobiator of three L’s, whose two sides were expanded in the native deformation
chapter. This gives a complex independently of any Hochschild or associative construction.


                                                          134
Chapter 31. The complete Virasoro conformal cohomology calculation


   The differential raises polynomial degree by one while raising cochain degree by one. Hence the
difference between polynomial degree and cochain degree is preserved. Every polynomial has finitely
many homogeneous parts, so this grading causes no completion issue.


31.2      A contraction of every nonresonant degree
For n ≥ 1 define
                                                                 ∂
                      (τn P )(λ1 , . . . , λn−1 ) = (−1)n−1         P (λ1 , . . . , λn−1 , t)     ,
